# HEINRICH
# CORNELIUS AGRIPPA
## On the Nobility and Excellence of the Female Sex, dedicated to Margaret of Austria, Princess of Burgundy.
<note>original: "de Nobilitate & Præcellentia fœminei sexus, ad Margaretam Augustam Austriacā. & Burgundionū Principē." Margaret of Austria (1480–1530) was the Governor of the Habsburg Netherlands and a powerful patron of the arts and sciences.</note>

->An Expostulation with Jean Catilinet regarding the exposition of the book by Johann Reuchlin, *On the Wonder-Working Word*<-
<note>original: "Expostulatio cū Ioānne Catilineti super expositiōe libri Ioānis Capnionis de uerbo mirifico." Jean Catilinet was a Franciscan friar who accused Agrippa of heresy. "Capnio" is the Greek-style pseudonym of the famous Hebrew scholar Johann Reuchlin.</note>

->A Declamation on the Sacrament of Marriage, dedicated to Margaret, Duchess of Alençon.<-
<note>Margaret of Alençon, also known as Marguerite de Navarre, was the sister of King Francis I of France and a noted intellectual and author.</note>

->A single book on the Threefold Way of Knowing God, dedicated to William Palaiologos, Marquess of Montferrat.<-

->A Dissuasion from Pagan Theology, dedicated to the Bishop of Bazas.<-
<note>original: "Dehortatio Gentilis theologiæ." This work argues that Christian students should focus on sacred scripture rather than the "pagan" philosophy of the Greeks and Romans.</note>

->A Declamation on the debatable opinion concerning Original Sin, dedicated to the Bishop of Cyrene.<-

->A Regimen against the Plague, dedicated to the same Bishop.<-

# ON THE EXCELLENCE OF THE FEMALE SEX
## by L. Beliaquetus
<note>original: "L. Beliaquetus." This is the Latinized name of Louis de Beaujeu, a 16th-century French physician and poet who contributed this introductory verse to support Agrippa’s argument.</note>

Cease, you boastful talker, to praise the male sex
More than is fair, lest your heap of praises be found empty.
Cease (if you are wise) to condemn the female sex
With malicious words that lack all reason.
If you weigh both sexes properly in your balance,
Every man will yield his place to the woman.
If you should doubt this, and the matter seems difficult to believe,
A witness now appears before my eyes like no other:
This little book which Agrippa recently and diligently composed,
Praising the female race above that of men.
<note>original: "fœmineumq; genus." This poem participates in the "Querelle des Femmes" (the Woman Question), a centuries-long intellectual debate about the nature and worth of women. Beliaquetus uses the metaphor of the "lance" or scales of justice to suggest that any objective evaluation favors women.</note>

<note>A circular library stamp is pressed into the paper. The text "BIBL. SEM. SUP." (Library of the Higher Seminary) surrounds a central decorative monogram "M." This indicates the book was once part of a religious academic collection, likely in Europe.</note>

<summary>This page features a short Latin poem by Louis de Beaujeu (Beliaquetus) that serves as a promotional introduction to Agrippa’s treatise. The poem admonishes men for their arrogance and directs the reader to Agrippa's text as proof of female superiority.</summary>
<keywords>Louis de Beaujeu, gender roles, Querelle des Femmes, Latin poetry, Renaissance literature, library stamps, female excellence</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a short poem by Louis de Beaujeu praising Agrippa’s work. This page begins the formal dedication of the treatise to Maximilianus Transylvanus, a prominent statesman and secretary to Emperor Charles V, explaining the long delay in the book's publication.</meta>

### TO THE MOST DISTINGUISHED MAN, LORD
### Maximilianus Transylvanus, Councilor to the Emperor 
### and Caesar Charles V, from Henricus Cornelius Agrippa. Greetings.

<note>A large, decorative initial **A** begins the text, adorned with floral patterns and classical motifs.</note> Almost twenty years have passed, splendid Maximilian, since the time when I was appointed to the lecture chair at the University of Dole in Burgundy. At that time, to the great admiration of all, I was interpreting the book of Johann Reuchlin <note>original: "Ioan. Capnionis." Agrippa uses the Greek version of Reuchlin's name, "Capnion," which means "little smoke," a common humanist practice.</note> titled *On the Wonder-Working Word* <note>original: "de Verbo Mirifico." Reuchlin’s work was a foundational text of Christian Kabbalah.</note> in honor of the divine Princess Margaret, our sovereign. During those lectures, I delivered a notable oration in praise of her virtues.

At that time, many men of high standing in that city urged me—among others, Simon Verney <note>original: "Symon Vernerius." Verney was the Dean of the Church of Dole and a high official at the university.</note>, whom you know, the Dean of the Church of Dole and Vice-Chancellor of the University—to dedicate some written work to the aforementioned Princess. They all pleaded with persistent requests, urged me with letters, and suggested that by doing so, I would enter into no small favor with the Princess. I agreed, thinking it a crime to reject the prayers of such men or to ignore the favor of so great a Princess that was offered to me.

And so, I began the theme of a work on the nobility and excellence of the female sex. I thought it not unworthy to devote and dedicate this specifically to that Princess who, above all the famous women of our age, seemed to be a unique example of female nobility and excellence. My hope was that, with her as patron and witness, this little book might gain significant authority against those who, in disparaging the female sex, leave nothing undone.

As for why I did not fulfill this vow to her Highness at that time, it was not due to the distance of the place, nor the passage of time, nor a fickleness of mind, nor a change of purpose. It was not even because of the narrowness of the subject matter or a poverty of talent. Rather, the slander of a certain "Catiline" <note>original: "Catilinei cuiusdã calũnia." Agrippa is referring to Jean Catilinet, a Franciscan friar who accused Agrippa of heresy for his lectures on Reuchlin, forcing him to flee Dole. Agrippa uses the name of the Roman conspirator Catiline as a biting insult.</note> was the cause. You will be able to see the nature of this slander from my own formal complaint against him, which I am sending to you along with these present writings. Overcome by his hypocrisy and filled with indignation, I suppressed the book. Until now, I have been unwilling to "whitewash any other wall with this same pot of lime" <note>original: "fidelia aliã quãuis etiã pretiosum parietẽ dealbare." This is a Latin proverb suggesting he did not want to apply his efforts to any other project or patron until the time was right.</note>, though I knew it would one day...

->A ij<-

<summary>In this dedicatory letter, Agrippa explains that he wrote this treatise twenty years earlier while lecturing in Dole, but suppressed its publication due to a bitter conflict with a Franciscan critic named Jean Catilinet.</summary>
<keywords>Maximilianus Transylvanus, Margaret of Austria, Johann Reuchlin, Dole, Jean Catilinet, nobility of women, Christian Kabbalah</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s introductory letter to Maximilianus Transylvanus. On the previous page, Agrippa explained how, twenty years earlier, he was encouraged by officials at the University of Dole to write a treatise for Princess Margaret of Austria. This page explains why the work is only now being published.</meta>

...confident that the book would not lose its mistress <note>original: "suam dominam." Agrippa refers to Princess Margaret of Austria, the intended recipient of the manuscript.</note>. 

Now, therefore, having returned to this my fatherland, I thought it right to keep my word, and that the offering of that book to our princess should be delayed no longer; for it is owed to her by most just right through agreement and vow. And so that she may know that in the intervening time I have neither forgotten her, nor ever abandoned my devoted faith, nor that the wickedness of others has been more powerful with me than the constancy of my own mind, which favors her virtues and praises immensely. 

If now your prudence does not disapprove of this plan of mine, I will see to it that this little book comes out into the public along with many others of mine, even though I see how small this matter is, and with how little elegance of speech it has been rendered. But I wish this little book—once written in my youth <note>Agrippa was about twenty-three when he first drafted this work in 1509.</note> and now only revised here and there, as you see in this hurried copy—to be offered to its own princess (as is the custom of speaking among those they call Canonists <note>Experts in Canon Law, the ecclesiastical law of the Catholic Church.</note>) "from now as if from then" <note>original: "ex nunc ut a tunc." A legal term meaning that an action taken now should be treated as if it had happened at the earlier intended time; essentially, a retroactive dedication.</note>, even if it be offered at the cost of my reputation. 

While in the meantime, now that I am older, I shall prepare more sublime and worthy things for her highness, with a weightier and fuller argument. Nor indeed would I want the Princess herself to measure my talent by these trifles of my youth; which, if she wishes to test it, could be of use to her even in the greatest matters of both peace and war. 

Furthermore, lest anyone more arrogant or boastful of their learning, out of contempt for my mediocrity and ungrateful toward my talent, despise, slander, bite, or tear apart this work of mine, I commend this same work to your Magnanimity, to be guarded and defended, along with the splendor of female nobility and the glory of womanly excellence. And I hope that I shall easily obtain pardon for this cause—that I have preferred women to men—because I wrote these things for so great a Princess, and published them with your Greatness encouraging and protecting me. Farewell. 

From Antwerp. April 16 <note>original: "xvi. Kalendas Maij." In the Roman calendar, the 16th day before the Kalends (1st) of May is April 16th.</note>, 1529.

I shall await your judgment.

<summary>Agrippa concludes his dedication to Maximilianus Transylvanus, explaining that he is finally publishing this youthful work to fulfill an old vow to Princess Margaret, despite fears that it may seem unpolished compared to his current abilities.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Antwerp, 1529, Princess Margaret of Austria, Canon Law, publishing, reputation</keywords>

...while in the meantime, now that I am more occupied <note>original: "occupatior." Agrippa refers to his busy professional life as a physician and historian at the court in Antwerp, contrasted with the leisure of his youth.</note>, I am devoted to perhaps better studies and am contemplating more serious matters. 

Farewell, and hold me in your affection.

->From Antwerp, on the twenty-eighth of April <note>original: "quarto calendas maias." In the Roman calendar, the fourth day before the first of May, counting both ends, is April 28th.</note>, in the year 1529.<-

̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄̄

# ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE OF THE FEMININE SEX

Since both sexes were made in the image and likeness of the Almighty and Greatest God—to whom it belongs to perform only that which is good—the difference between them is not so great as common people believe. Indeed, the soul of the man and the soul of the woman are entirely the same, nor has there been any difference in the distribution of the spirit. 

<margin>The equality of the soul.</margin>

For woman has been granted the same mind, the same reason, the same power of speech, and she aims for the same end: eternal blessedness. Therefore, the difference between them is found only in the placement of the limbs and the shape of the body, through which the functions of nature are performed in various ways. 

However, let us see if we can find something by which the feminine sex might be preferred over the masculine. This is not a matter of light importance <note>original: "res haud levis momenti." Agrippa signals that his work is not merely a "paradox" or a joke, but a serious rhetorical and philosophical inquiry.</note>; for if we consider the divine origin, the nobility of the name, the order of creation, the place of birth, and the very matter from which they were formed, we shall find that woman excels man in every way.

<summary>Agrippa concludes his dedication and begins his argument by asserting that men and women possess identical souls and rational capacities. He introduces his thesis: that women are superior to men when evaluated through theological and natural history.</summary>
<keywords>Agrippa von Nettesheim, Antwerp 1529, gender equality, soul, divine image, creation order, feminine nobility</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening of the formal declamation. The previous page ended with a transitional sentence regarding the search for the inherent nobility of women. This page begins with the author’s name and a justification for his bold task, moving into a formal dedication to Margaret of Austria.</meta>

### BY HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

While I myself hesitated with a strange fear of ingratitude and even sacrilege, my shame was conquered, and I have become bolder in writing. I feared I would appear even more audacious if I remained silent, interpreting it as a good omen—as if the responsibility for this subject, which the assembly of learned men seems to have utterly neglected until now, had been left and decreed to me by the powers above. 

Therefore, I shall give yearly glory to woman and I will not hide her excellence. I am so far from being ashamed of the argument I have taken up, or from thinking I should be blamed if I prefer women to men, that I barely believe I will be excused for having embraced such a sublime matter in a style more humble than is fitting. My only defense is the shortage of time, the difficulty of the task, and the justice of the cause; furthermore, I have undertaken this work with no desire for flattery or the pursuit of favor. Therefore, my aim was not so much to adorn my words with rhetorical fictions and dutiful lies <note>original: "officiosisque mendaciis." A common Renaissance term for "white lies" or polite exaggerations used in courtly praise.</note> in these praises, but rather to demonstrate the matter itself through reason, authority, examples, and the testimonies of the Sacred Scriptures and both laws <note>The phrase "both laws" (utriusque iuris) refers to the two legal pillars of the time: Civil Law (Roman law) and Canon Law (Church law).</note>. 

But to you, most Serene Margaret—among the illustrious women of this age throughout the whole world, whom Apollo, Diana, Day, Aurora, and Vulcan (those five gods) <note>Agrippa uses these deities as metaphors for the celestial bodies and light: the Sun, Moon, Day, Dawn, and Fire. He claims even these cosmic lights have not looked upon her equal.</note> have not outshone in the nobility of your lineage, the excellence of your virtues, or the glory of your deeds—to you, I dedicate and devote this work of mine. I do this so that you—who have ascended to such a height of virtue that you have surpassed in your life and character everything preached in praise of the female sex—may be a present example and a most faithful witness. Through you, the honor and glory of your sex may shine more splendidly than the sun...

<summary>Agrippa justifies his "bold" decision to argue for the superiority of women, claiming the topic has been neglected by other scholars, and begins a formal dedication to Margaret of Austria.</summary>
<keywords>Margaret of Austria, gender equality, rhetoric, Canon law, Civil law, female virtue, dedication</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the formal dedication to Margaret of Austria and begins the main body of the declamation. The author transitions from personal praise of his patroness to a theological and philosophical argument regarding the equality of the soul between men and women.</meta>

...shine forth. Farewell most happily, you who are at once the honor, ornament, and glory of the most noble women and princes, perfect in every way.

# THE DECLAMATION OF HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA
On the Nobility and Preeminence of the Female Sex.

[Decorative initial D] GOD, the best and greatest <note>original: "Deus optimus maximus." A traditional Roman title for Jupiter, here adopted by Renaissance humanists to describe the Christian God.</note>, the creator of all things, father and the source of good for both sexes, most full of fruitfulness, created man in his own likeness; male <margin>Genesis 1</margin> and female he created them. Indeed, the distinction between these sexes consists of nothing except the differing arrangement of the parts of the body, in which the practice of procreation required a necessary diversity. Truly, he bestowed the same and altogether identical form of soul upon both the male and the female. Between these souls, there is absolutely no distance of sex. Woman herself has been allotted the same mind, reason, and speech as man; she strives toward the same end of blessedness, where there will be no exception made for sex. For according to the Gospel truth: <margin>Luke 20</margin> <margin>Mark 12</margin> those rising again in their own sex shall not perform the function of sex, but the likeness of angels is promised to them. <margin>Matthew 22</margin> Therefore, based on the essence of the soul, there is no preeminence of nobility of one over the other between man and woman. Rather, there is an innate freedom of dignity equal to both. However, regarding those things in a human being that remain besides the divine essence of the soul, in these the illustrious lineage of women excels the harsh race of men almost infinitely, which then—

<note>A 4 — This is a signature mark, used by early modern printers to keep the folded sheets of paper in the correct order for binding.</note>

<summary>Agrippa begins his formal argument by asserting that men and women possess identical souls, intellects, and spiritual destinies. He argues that physical gender differences exist solely for procreation and do not imply any spiritual or essential inequality.</summary>
<keywords>equality of the soul, theology, Genesis, human dignity, gender roles, resurrection, Margaret of Austria</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the opening argument of Agrippa's declamation. Having established that men and women share an identical soul, he now begins to argue for the woman's superiority based on her creation and name. This page picks up mid-sentence from the previous page’s discussion on the "illustrious work of God."</meta>

**HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**

...will finally be settled and firm, when this very thing (which is our goal) has been shown not with adulterated or painted words, nor even with those logical snares with which many sophists are accustomed to entrap people, but rather through the patronage of the best authors, through truthful histories of deeds, and through clear reasons, as well as the testimonies of Sacred Scripture and the decrees of both laws <note>The "both laws" (utriusque iuris) refers to Civil Law (Roman) and Canon Law (Church), which formed the foundation of legal authority in the Renaissance.</note>.

Therefore, to begin the matter itself: Woman was made so much more excellent than man <margin>Genesis 3</margin> as she received a more excellent name than he. For Adam means "earth," <note>original: "Adam terram sonat." Agrippa refers to the Hebrew etymology connecting the name Adam with 'adamah', meaning ground or red earth.</note> whereas Eve is interpreted as "life." <note>original: "Eva autem vita interpretatur." Based on Genesis 3:20, where Eve is named because she is "the mother of all living."</note> Just as life itself is more excellent than earth, so too is woman to be preferred over man. Nor should it be said that this is a weak argument—to pass judgment on things themselves based on their names. For we know that the supreme craftsman of things and names knew the things before he named them; since he could not be deceived, he fashioned the names only so far <margin>In the Novels, under the title 'On the definition of meanings,' column 3, at the beginning; in the Institutes, 'On Donations'; and in the Digest, there is another.</margin> as they might express the nature, properties, and use of the thing. For such is the truth of ancient names, as Roman laws also testify, that they are in harmony with the things themselves and are fittingly significant of them. Therefore, an argument drawn from names is of great weight among theologians and legal scholars. Just as we read it written concerning Nabal: "according to his name he is a fool, and folly is with him." <note>Nabal is a biblical figure whose name literally translates to "fool" or "senseless."</note> From this, Paul, in his Epistle to the Hebrews, <margin>Hebrews 1</margin> intending to show the excellence of Christ, uses this argument, saying: "Because he was made so much better than the angels, <margin>Philippians 2</margin> as he has inherited a more excellent name than they." And elsewhere: "He gave to him a na—" <note>The text cuts off mid-word, referring to the "name which is above every name" mentioned in Philippians 2:9.</note>

<summary>Agrippa begins his formal argument for the superiority of women by analyzing the etymological significance of the names Adam (earth) and Eve (life). He defends the validity of using names to judge the essence of a being, citing both biblical examples and Roman legal principles.</summary>
<keywords>etymology, Adam, Eve, legal authority, Sacred Scripture, Nabal, Christ, names as essence</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa's argument that names reflect the inherent nature and dignity of a person. Following the previous page's comparison of "Adam" (earth) and "Eve" (life), he now invokes legal and theological authorities to prove that etymology is a valid path to truth.</meta>

**ON THE NOBILITY AND PREEMINENCE**

...[the] name which is above every name, so that at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, of those in heaven, on earth, and under the earth. <note>original: "ut in nomine Iesu omne genu flectatur..." This is a quotation from the Book of Philippians 2:9–10, illustrating the spiritual power inherent in a name.</note> Add to this the considerable force of both laws <note>The "both laws" (utriusque iuris) refers to Civil (Roman) Law and Canon (Church) Law.</note> regarding the obligations of words: in the meanings of words, in conditions and demonstrations, in attached conditions, and in discussions of that kind, and contained in the chapters of laws, as can be gathered in those very titles and others similar to them in both laws. <margin>16. q. 1. *sicupis*. 23. q. 1. *pudenda*. *de pe. d. iij penitentia*. *ff. de verb. sig. l. tugurij*. 29. q. 1. *in pri*. Code: *de epi. & cle. l. decernim⁹*. Code: *de offi. praef. urb. l. i. de desp. ipub. puberes de prebẽd. cũ secũdũ. de ele. fũdamẽta. §. pinde li. vi.* 12. q. 1. *clericus qui*. 21. d. c. *cleros ff. ad le. aquil. ita vulnerat⁹*.</margin> For thus in law we argue from the interpretation of a name. Likewise from the force of a word and a term. Furthermore, from the etymology of a name and from the reason for a name, and from the order of words. For the laws themselves consider the meanings of names quite diligently, so as to interpret something from them. 

Cyprian <note>Agrippa refers to a work traditionally attributed to St. Cyprian of Carthage, though now often assigned to a "Pseudo-Cyprian."</note> also argues against the Jews that the first man obtained his name from the four corners of the world, namely East, West, North, and South <note>original: "ἀνατολή δύσισ ἄρκτοσ μεσημβρία" (Anatole, Dysis, Arktos, Mesembria). In Greek, the first letters of these four directions—Alpha, Delta, Alpha, Mu—spell "ADAM."</note>. And in the same book, he interprets that same name, **Adam**, because "earth was made flesh," <margin>Cyprian, *On Mounts Sinai and Sion*.</margin> although such an exposition differs from the tradition of Moses, since among the Hebrews it is written not with four letters but with three. <note>In Hebrew, the name Adam (אדם) consists of three letters: Aleph, Dalet, and Mem. The Greek etymology Agrippa mentions only works in the Greek language.</note> Yet this exposition is not to be blamed in such a holy man, who did not learn the Hebrew language, which many saints and expositors of Sacred Scripture were ignorant of without much fault. But if I am not granted a similar license of patience, so that I may be allowed at my discretion to fashion a pariform <note>meaning "similar in form" or "analogous."</note> etymology in praise of the name of **Eve** for the female sex, at least permit me to say this one thing—

->**A 5**<-

<summary>Agrippa argues that names are legally and theologically significant, using the etymology of "Adam" as a case study. He defends the use of symbolic interpretations of names, even when linguistically imperfect, to establish the dignity of the subject.</summary>
<keywords>etymology, legal theory, Civil Law, Canon Law, Adam, Eve, St. Cyprian, Hebrew language, linguistics, gender excellence</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the etymological arguments regarding names to a mystical and cosmological defense of women. Agrippa moves from the linguistic affinity between woman and the divine to the hierarchical order of creation.</meta>

### HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

According to the mystical symbols of the Cabalists <note>Scholars of Jewish mysticism. Agrippa was a famous proponent of "Christian Cabala," which used these ancient traditions to interpret Christian doctrine.</note>, the very name of "woman" has more affinity with the ineffable name of divine omnipotence—the Tetragrammaton <note>original: "τετραγράμματου" (tetragrammatou). This is the "four-letter" name of God (YHWH) in Hebrew, considered too sacred to be spoken aloud.</note>—than the name of "man" does; for the name of man agrees with the divine name neither in its characters, nor in its shape, nor in its numerical value. But we shall set these things aside for now, for they are read by few, understood by even fewer, and require a more extensive explanation than would be appropriate to include here. 

In the meantime, we will investigate the excellence of woman not only from her name, but from the actual facts, her duties, and her merits. Let us therefore "search the scriptures" (as they say) and, taking our beginning from the very start of creation, let us discuss what dignity woman herself was granted in the primary order of production over man. 

We know that whatever was made by God—the Best and Greatest—differs chiefly in this: that some of these things remain forever incorruptible, while others are subject to decay and change. In creating these, God proceeded in such an order that, starting from the more noble of one kind, He ended with the most noble of another. 

Therefore, He first created the incorruptible angels and souls; for Augustine <margin>Augustine, *On Genesis*, Book 7.</margin> <note>St. Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD). Agrippa is referencing Augustine's theory that souls were created all at once at the beginning of time, rather than at the moment of birth.</note> argues that the souls of our first parents were created together with the angels before the production of their bodies. Furthermore, He created incorruptible bodies, such as the heavens and the stars, and the elements—which are indeed incorruptible in essence but subject to various changes—out of which He established all other things that are subject to decay. He began with the baser things and ascended through individual degrees of dignity toward the univer...

<summary>Agrippa argues that the name of "woman" is mystically linked to the Tetragrammaton and begins a cosmological argument for woman's superiority based on God's method of creating from the simplest to the most perfect.</summary>

Agrippa moves from mystical linguistics to a cosmological argument: that because woman was created last, she represents the pinnacle and completion of God's work.</meta>

### ON THE NOBILITY AND PREEMINENCE [OF THE FEMALE SEX]

...proceeding toward perfection. From here, first minerals came forth, then vegetables, plants, and trees; then zoophytes <note>Organisms that appear to be both plants and animals, such as coral or sponges; in the Renaissance, these were seen as "bridge" species between kingdoms.</note>, and finally brute animals, in an order including reptiles, flying creatures, and quadrupeds. 

But last of all, He created <margin>Genesis 2.</margin> two humans like unto Himself: the male first, and finally the female. In her, the heavens and the earth were perfected, along with all their adornment <note>original: "ornatus eorum." This refers to the "furnishing" or finishing of the universe, echoing the language of the Latin Vulgate Bible.</note>. For the Creator, arriving at the creation of woman, rested in her, as if having nothing more honorable left to create. In her was concluded and consummated all the wisdom and power of the Creator, beyond whom no other creature is found, nor can even be imagined. 

Since, therefore, woman is the last of creatures, the end and most perfect complement of all the works of God, and the very perfection of the universe itself, who will deny that she is most worthy of preeminence over every creature? Without her, the world itself—even if it were already polished to the highest degree <note>original: "ad unguem," literally "to the fingernail." A Latin idiom for a work of art finished with total precision.</note> and complete in all its parts—would have been imperfect; for the world could not be perfected except in the most perfect of all creatures. 

For it is inconsistent and absurd to think that God finished such a great work in something imperfect. Since the world itself was created by God like a whole and most perfect circle, it was necessary that it be completed in that part which would join the first of all things with the last of all things in a most unified bond within itself. 

Thus, while woman was the last of all things created in time during the world's creation, she was the first of all in the concept of the Divine Mind, both in authority and dignity. Just as it is written concerning her by the Prophet: "Before the heavens were created, God chose her and pre-elected her" <note>Agrippa here adapts a passage from Proverbs 8 regarding Divine Wisdom, traditionally interpreted as a precursor to the creation of the world, and applies it to the essence of womanhood.</note>.

<summary>Agrippa argues that woman is the crowning achievement of creation because God created her last, thereby "finishing" the universe in its most perfect form. He posits that while she was the last created in time, she was the first intended in God's mind.</summary>

<keywords>creation order, Genesis, perfection, cosmology, Divine Mind, teleology, womanhood as completion</keywords>

### HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

<margin>Aristotle, at the end of Book 2 of *On the Soul* <note>original: "Aristo. vlti. ij. de auditu." While the OCR reads "de auditu" (On Hearing), Agrippa is referencing Aristotle's broader teleological principle found in his works on nature and the soul: that the purpose or "end" of a thing is its defining excellence.</note></margin> Indeed, it is a well-known opinion among philosophers (to use their own words) that the goal <note>original: "finem," meaning the final purpose or end-result.</note> is always first in intention, but last in execution. Woman, however, was the final work of God, introduced by God into this world as its queen into a palace already prepared and decorated for her, and perfected with every possible gift. Justly, therefore, every creature loves, venerates, and respects her; and justly is every creature subjected to her and obeys her, she who is the queen of all creatures and their goal, their perfection, and their glory, absolute in every way. Concerning her, the Wise Man <note>A reference to the author of the biblical Book of Wisdom, traditionally identified as Solomon.</note> says: <margin>Wisdom 3 <note>The quote actually appears in Wisdom of Solomon 8:3.</note></margin> "She glorifies her noble birth by having God as her companion; indeed, the Lord of all has loved her" <note>original: "Generositatem mulieris glorificat contubernium habens Dei, sed & omnium Dominus dilexit eam."</note>.

The sacred scriptures also testify most abundantly how much woman exceeds man in the nobility of her lineage by virtue of the place in which she was created. For woman was formed in Paradise—the most noble and pleasant of places—alongside the angels; man, however, was made outside of Paradise, in the rural fields among the brute animals. It was only afterward, for the sake of creating woman, that he was brought into Paradise. 

Therefore, by a certain special gift of nature, as if accustomed to the elevated place of her creation, woman does not suffer from vertigo <note>dizziness or lightheadedness when looking down</note> even when looking down from a great height, nor do her eyes grow dim, as often happens to men. Furthermore, if it should happen that a woman and a man are in danger together in the water, and all external help is removed, the woman will float longer than the man, who sinks more quickly and seeks the bottom. <margin>Civil Law: *Digest*, on the Edict of the Aediles, law "What if he refuses," section "Those who [sell] slaves" <note>original: "ff. de ede. edic. l. qd si nolit. §. qui mancipia." This refers to the *Corpus Juris Civilis*, the body of Roman law used in the Renaissance. Agrippa uses legal precedents to argue that the place of origin determines legal status and nobility.</note></margin> Moreover, that the dignity of a location contributes to the nobility of a person is clearly confirmed by both civil laws and the sacred canons...

<summary>Agrippa argues that woman is the "queen of creation" because she was the final goal of God's work and was created in the superior environment of Paradise, whereas man was created in the common fields. He supports this with biological claims about woman's physical resilience and legal arguments regarding the importance of one's place of origin.</summary>

### OF THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE

...confirm, as does the custom of all nations. This rule is most strictly observed not only among humans, but in evaluating all animals and even inanimate things: that the more noble their place of origin, the more well-born they are considered to be. For this reason, Isaac instructed his son Jacob not to take a wife from the land of Canaan, but from Mesopotamia in Syria, which was of a better condition <note>Agrippa alludes to the biblical idea that the "quality" of a land reflects on the quality of its inhabitants.</note>. Not unlike this is the account found in John <margin>John 1</margin> where Philip says: "We have found Jesus, the son of Joseph, from Nazareth," and Nathanael says to him, "Can anything good come from Nazareth?" 

Now let us proceed to other matters: Woman excels man in the material of her creation, <margin>Genesis 2</margin> because she was not created from some inanimate thing or from vile mud <note>original: "vili luto." In the Renaissance, the "slime of the earth" used to create Adam was often contrasted with the "refined" flesh used to create Eve.</note>, as man was, but from purified, life-giving, and animate matter—possessing a soul that participates in the divine, rational mind. To this we may add that man was made by God from the earth, which by its own nature—and with the cooperation of celestial influence—produces living creatures of every kind. 

Woman, however, was created by God alone, above all celestial influence and the readiness of nature, without any cooperating power. She was made consistent, whole, and perfect in all things, while man, in the meantime, suffered the loss of a single rib from which the woman was formed—namely, Eve from the sleeping Adam. He slept so deeply that he did not even feel the rib being plucked away, which God took from the man and gave to the woman. Thus, man is a work of nature, but woman is the handiwork of God <note>original: "Vir itaq; naturæ opus, mulier opificiū dei." Agrippa makes a sharp theological distinction here: man is a biological product of the earth, but woman is a direct, artistic creation of the Divine.</note>. Therefore, woman is generally more capable than man of receiving divine splendor, and is often full of it—a fact which even now can easily be seen from her miraculous purity and beauty. For since beauty itself is nothing...

<summary>Agrippa argues that woman is superior to man because she was created from refined, animate matter rather than "vile mud," and was a direct creation of God rather than a product of natural forces.</summary>

# ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE
...of the female sex, her hips and thighs are more well-formed, her calves more shapely, and the tips of her hands and feet taper into a rounded form, with every single limb full of vital vigor <note>original: "succi plena." In the physiological language of the time, this refers to being full of healthy "juices" or humors, signifying vitality and youth.</note>. Added to this is a modest gait and step, a more graceful movement, more dignified gestures, and furthermore, in the order, symmetry, figure, and constitution of the whole body, she is by far the most beautiful in every respect. In the entire range of creatures, there is no sight so wonderful, nor any miracle so worth seeing, such that no one—unless completely blind—could fail to see that God himself gathered together in woman all the beauty of which the entire world is capable. Because of this, every creature is struck with awe by her, and loves and venerates her for many reasons; so much so that we see it happen in experience that even incorporeal spirits and demons often pine for women with the most burning love. This is no deceptive opinion, but a truth known through many experiences <note>Agrippa is likely referring to the contemporary belief in incubi—spirit beings or fallen angels who were said to be attracted to human women due to their physical perfection.</note>. 

And to pass over what the poets have handed down to us regarding the loves of the gods and their mistresses—such as Apollo’s love for Daphne; Neptune’s for Salmonea; Hercules’ for Hebe, Iole, and Omphale; and the many loves of other gods, including the very many of Jupiter himself—this divine gift of beauty, so lovable to both gods and men, is celebrated in many places in the Sacred Scriptures <note>original: "sacra eloqua." Literally "Sacred Eloquence," a common Renaissance term for the Bible.</note> as being above all other gifts of grace in women. <margin>Genesis 6</margin> Hence we read in Genesis that the "sons of God," seeing that the daughters of men were beautiful, chose wives for themselves from among those they desired. <margin>Genesis 12</margin> We also read of Sarah, the wife of Abraham, that she was beautiful beyond the other women of the earth, indeed, the most beautiful of all. So too, when the servant of Abraham had seen Rebecca, <margin>Genesis 24</margin> a girl of extraordinary beauty, he said silently to himself: "This is she whom the Lord has prepared for the son of Abraham, Isaac." ### HENRI. COR. AGRIP.

Abigail, the wife of Nabal—a most wicked man—was prudent and wise, as well as beautiful. For this reason, she preserved the life and property of her husband from the fury of David. Thus, a bad man was saved by a beautiful woman. David addressed her with these words: “Go in peace to your house; behold, I have heard your voice and have honored your face.” Since all beauty is either spiritual, vocal, <note>original: "uocalis." This refers to the beauty of speech, eloquence, or a pleasant voice.</note> or physical, Abigail was beautiful in every respect: in the prudence of her <margin>1 Samuel 25 <note>Agrippa cites this as 2 Kings 25 following the Vulgate numbering, where 1–2 Samuel are 1–2 Kings.</note></margin> spirit, in the eloquence of her speech, and in the loveliness of her body. For this reason, after the death of her husband Nabal, she became one of the wives of David. 

And Bathsheba was a woman of such remarkable form that David, captured by love for her, wed her after the death of her husband and elevated her to royal dignity above all others. Likewise, <margin>1 Kings 1 <note>Cited as 3 Kings 1 in the original Latin.</note></margin> Abishag the Shunammite, because she was a most beautiful girl, was chosen for the purpose of restoring warmth to the now-aging King David by lying beside him. Therefore, the old king wished to increase her status with the highest honors, and after the king's death, she was held in the place of a powerful queen. 

To this end, we also read of the wonderful beauty of Queen Vashti, and of <margin>Esther 1 & 2</margin> Esther, who was preferred over her and was even more outstanding, possessing an exceedingly beautiful and graceful face. We also read of Judith, <margin>Judith 8 & 10</margin> whose beauty the Lord increased to such an extent that those who beheld her were struck with wondering amazement. Finally, there is Susanna, <margin>Daniel 13</margin> who was extremely delicate and beautiful in appearance. 

What of the fact that we also read how, after the various trials of Job <margin>Job 42:13–15 <note>Cited as the final chapter of Job.</note></margin> and the completion of his labors, the Lord—besides the other things he deserved for his supreme patience—gave him three daughters

# ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE <note>original: "DE NOBILIT. ET PRAECEL." This is a partial heading of the full title: *De Nobilitate et Praecellentia Foeminei Sexus* (On the Nobility and Excellence of the Female Sex).</note>

...most beautiful, far more pleasing than the three Graces, <note>In Greek mythology, the Graces (Charites) represent charm, beauty, and creativity.</note> because women more beautiful than these have by no means been found in the whole earth. Let us read further of the histories of holy virgins; we will certainly marvel at how the Catholic Church solemnly praises and sings of their wondrous beauty and most excellent form, beyond the rest of the children of men.

But far and wide, the ruler of all is the immaculate Mother of God, the Virgin Mary, whose beauty the sun and moon admire. From her most beautiful face there shone forth such a purity and holiness of beauty that, although it struck the eyes and minds of everyone alike, no mortal ever fell into corruption through her charms, even by the smallest thought. I have reviewed these things—even if more extensively from the Holy Bible where mention is made of beauty so often, and in almost the very same words—so that we may clearly understand that the beauty of women is honored and loaded with dignity not only among men, but also before God.

Furthermore, <margin>Numbers 31</margin> we read elsewhere in the sacred scriptures that God commanded every male, even the boys, to be killed, but that the beautiful women <margin>Deuteronomy 21</margin> should be saved. And in Deuteronomy, the sons of Israel are permitted to choose for themselves a beautiful woman from among the captives to be a wife. 

In addition to this admirable beauty, woman is also endowed with a certain dignity of modesty <note>original: "honestatis," here referring to natural decency or moral respectability.</note> that does not happen to men: for the hair of a woman grows so long that it is able to cover all the more private parts <note>original: "pudentiores," literally the "shameful" parts, a common euphemism for the genitals in Renaissance texts.</note> of the body. Add to this that for a woman to touch these parts of the body during the works of nature—something which is customary for men...

### BY HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA <note>original: "HENRICI COR. AGRIP." Henry Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535) was a famous German polymath, physician, and legal scholar whose defense of women was radical for its time.</note>

...it is never necessary to come. Finally, with a wondrous sense of decency, nature itself has arranged the private parts of women so that they do not project as they do in men, but remain within, set aside in a more secret and safer place. Furthermore, nature bestowed more modesty upon women than upon men. For this reason, it has very often happened that a woman suffering from a dangerous abscess of the groin chose death rather than submit herself to the sight and touch of a surgeon to be healed. And they retain this honest modesty even when dying and when dead, as is most clearly seen in those who perish in water. For, <margin>Pliny</margin> as Pliny <note>Referring to Pliny the Elder’s *Natural History*, a massive encyclopedia from the 1st century AD that served as a standard reference for natural phenomena in the Renaissance.</note> records and experience bears witness, a woman [who has drowned] lies face down, nature sparing the shame of the deceased, whereas a man floats on his back.

Added to this is the fact that the most dignified part of a human being—by which we differ most from the beasts and indicate our divine nature—is the head, and most especially the face within it. Indeed, the head in men is deformed by baldness, whereas woman, by a great privilege of nature, does not go bald. Moreover, the face in men is often so disfigured by that most hateful beard and covered with dirty hairs that they can hardly be distinguished from wild beasts; in women, on the contrary, the face always remains pure and graceful. Hence, in the Law of the Twelve Tables <note>The Law of the Twelve Tables (c. 450 BCE) was the foundation of Roman law. Agrippa here interprets an ancient rule against women scratching their faces in mourning as a prohibition against shaving, to preserve their natural hairless beauty.</note>, a provision was made that women should not shave their cheeks, lest a beard might ever grow out and their modesty be hidden. This is also the most evident proof of the cleanliness and purity of a woman to everyone: that once a woman has been cleanly washed, however often she is rinsed afterward in pure water, the water itself receives no mark of impurity...

### ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE [OF THE FEMALE SEX] <note>original: "DE NOBILIT. ET PRAECEL" – Part of the full title *De Nobilitate et Praecellentia Foeminei Sexus*.</note>

...vessel. <note>The text likely refers to the body as a "vessel" or container of the soul.</note> A man, however, no matter how much he is washed, as often as he washes again, he muddies and disturbs the water. Added to this is the fact that, by nature's design, superfluities <note>A Renaissance medical term for excess bodily fluids or waste.</note> are expelled from women every month through more secret places; in men, however, these are continually emitted through the face—which is supposed to be the most dignified part of the human body. <note>Agrippa is satirically suggesting that a man’s beard is actually the body’s way of "draining" waste through the face, whereas women's "waste" is hidden and periodic.</note>

Furthermore, since among all other living creatures it is granted only to humans to lift their faces toward heaven, <margin>Ovid</margin> <note>Agrippa cites the Roman poet Ovid, who famously wrote about man's upright posture in *Metamorphoses*.</note> nature and fortune have wonderfully provided for women in this regard and spared them so much that, if they happen to fall by chance or accident, women almost always fall on their backs, and are either never or very rarely thrown forward on their heads or faces. <note>This was a common argument in the Renaissance for "divine modesty"—that nature protects a woman's modesty even during an accidental fall by ensuring she lands on her back.</note>

And why (not to omit this point) do we not see that in the procreation of the human race, nature has preferred women to men? This is most clearly evident because, according to the testimony of Galen <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the most influential physician of the Roman Empire.</note> and Avicenna, <note>Ibn Sina or Avicenna (c. 980–1037), the Persian polymath whose *Canon of Medicine* was the standard text in European universities.</note> only the female seed <margin>Galen, *On Seed*, book 2</margin> is the matter and nourishment of the fetus. <margin>On the Use of Parts, book 14</margin> The man's contribution is minimal, for his role enters into the process only as an accident <term>accident: In Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is a non-essential quality that does not change the essence of a thing.</term> to the substance. For the greatest and chief duty of women, <margin>Avicenna, *Canon*, Book 1, Fen 1, Doc 5</margin> as the law says, is to conceive and protect that which is conceived. For this reason, we see that most children resemble their mothers, because they are procreated from their blood. This is most apparent in the physical build of the body, but it is always true of their character; for if mothers are foolish, the sons also become foolish; but if mothers are wise, their sons reflect their wisdom. Conversely, in the case of fathers, even if they are wise themselves, they mostly beget foolish sons; and foolish fathers may produce wise sons, provided the mother is wise. Nor is there any other reason why mothers...

<meta>This page continues Henry Cornelius Agrippa’s argument for the superiority of women based on their physical and moral nature. # HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA <note>original: "HENRICI COR. AGRIP." — The author’s name in its Latinized form.</note>

...mothers love their children more than fathers do, simply because they feel much more of themselves in their children; mothers possess a greater share in them than fathers. For the same reason I mentioned, I also believe it is innate in us to be more affectionately inclined toward our mother than our father, to such an extent that we seem to respect the father, but only to love the mother. For the same reason, nature has bestowed milk of such vigor upon women that it not only nourishes infants but also restores the sick and suffices as a <term>pillar of life</term> <note>original: "uitæ columen" — a foundational support or mainstay of existence.</note> for adults. 

<margin>Valerius, Book 5, Chapter 4</margin> <note>Valerius Maximus was a 1st-century Roman author who compiled a collection of memorable deeds and sayings.</note> We read of an example of this in Valerius concerning a certain common young woman who sustained her mother in prison in this way when she would otherwise have died of hunger; because of this <term>piety</term> <note>In the Roman sense, *pietas* (piety) refers to the deep duty and devotion one owes to parents and country.</note> the mother’s life was spared, and perpetual nourishment was given to both, and the prison was consecrated as a Temple of Piety. <note>This story is famously known in art and literature as "Roman Charity." Though Agrippa mentions a mother here, the most common version of the tale involves a daughter, Pero, breastfeeding her father, Cimon, to save him from starvation.</note> 

Moreover, it is nearly always evident that a woman is of greater <term>mercy</term> <note>original: "misericordiæ" — a heart for the miserable; compassion.</note> and compassion than a man, which <margin>Aristotle, *On Animals*</margin> <note>Referring to Aristotle's *Historia Animalium*, where he discusses the different temperaments of the sexes.</note> Aristotle himself attributed as a characteristic to the female sex. 

<margin>Ecclesiasticus 36</margin> <note>original: "Ecclesia. 26." — likely a reference to the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 36:27: "Where there is no wife, the sick man sighs."</note> For this reason, I believe Solomon said: "where there is no woman, the sick man groans," either because she possesses wonderful skill and eagerness in serving and attending to the infirm, or because woman’s milk is the most immediate remedy for the sick and weak—even those close to death—by which they are restored to life. 

Hence, as they say, the warmth of their breasts applied to the chest of men worn out by extreme old age excites, increases, and preserves the <term>vital heat</term> <note>In Renaissance medicine, vital heat was the essential internal energy that fueled life and digestion; its loss was thought to cause aging and death.</note> within them. This was not hidden even from David, who <margin>1 Kings 1</margin> <note>original: "3. Reg. 1." — In the Vulgate Bible, the books of Kings were often numbered 1-4. 3 Kings corresponds to 1 Kings in modern Bibles.</note> chose Abishag the Shunammite girl in his old age, being warmed by her embraces. Furthermore, she is also more ready than a man...

->## ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE<- <note>original: "DE NOBILI. ET PRAECEL." — Abbreviated running header for the book's title, *De Nobilitate et Praecellentia Foeminei Sexus*.</note>

...woman, for that sacred duty of procreation (as is evident to all); for she is capable of this at ten years of age or even younger, whereas a man reaches this power much later. Moreover, it is obscure to no one that among all fruit-bearing creatures, it is only the woman who—even after she is pregnant and has begun to carry—retains a womb <note>original: "uterū"</note> that is inclined again toward the work of renewal not long after she has been released by childbirth. Her vessel (which they call the womb) is so inspired by human conception that it is sometimes recorded that a woman has conceived without intercourse. 

<margin>Averroes, Book 2 of *The Colliget*</margin> <note>Averroes (1126–1198) was a famous Andalusian philosopher and physician. His *Colliget* was a foundational medical text in Renaissance universities.</note> For so that natural philosopher recorded in his literary monuments concerning a certain woman who absorbed male semen that had been discharged in a bath. 

To this is added another stupendous miracle of nature: that a pregnant woman, if driven by her cravings <note>Agrippa is describing "pica," the psychological or physiological urge to eat non-food items during pregnancy.</note>, may feed with impunity on undercooked meats and raw fish, and not infrequently on coals, mud, stones, even metals and poisons; she digests many such things without harm and converts them into health-giving nourishment for the body. 

How many miracles beyond these nature rejoices to produce in women, no one will wonder who has read through the volumes of philosophers and physicians; of these, I will submit only one example which is readily at hand. Regarding menstrual blood: besides the fact that it liberates one from quartan fevers, from rabies <note>original: "hydrophorbia"</note>, from the **falling sickness** <note>original: "morbo comitiali" — a historical term for epilepsy.</note>, from elephantiasis, from melancholic impressions, from mania, and many most pernicious diseases of that kind, it also effects many other things no less worthy of admiration. Among other wonders, it even extinguishes fires, calms storms, averts the dangers of the waves, drives away all harmful things, dissolves witchcraft, and puts **evil spirits** <note>original: "cacodæmones"</note> to flight. Regarding those things which...

...the rest; it is not my plan to prove more at present. I will, however, add this much further by way of an addition: that there is in women, according to the traditions of philosophers and physicians proven by experience, a divine gift to be admired by all, by which they themselves, through their own innate gifts, can heal themselves of every kind of disease, without any external or imported assistance. But what surpasses all wonders, the most wonderful thing of all is this: that a woman alone, without a man, was able to produce human nature, a power which is by no means given to a man. Indeed, this is an admitted fact among the Turks or Muslims, among whom many are believed to have been conceived without male seed; these they call *nefesogli* <note>original: "nefesogli." From the Turkish *nefes oğlu*, meaning "son of breath" or "son of the spirit," referring to individuals supposedly born of the spirit rather than physical union.</note> in their own language. It is also told of islands where women conceive by the blowing of the wind, although we do not concede that this is true. For only the Virgin Mary—only she, I say—conceived Christ without a man and gave birth to a son from her own substance and natural fecundity. For the most blessed Virgin Mary is the true and natural mother of Christ, and Christ himself is the true and natural son of the Virgin Mary. I say "natural" because he is a man, and again "natural son of the Virgin" insofar as the Virgin herself was not subject to corrupted nature <note>Agrippa refers to the doctrine that Mary was free from Original Sin, allowing her biological functions to operate in a "perfect" or uncorrupted state.</note>. Therefore, she did not even give birth in pain, nor was she under the power of a man; so great was her fecundity from a prevenient blessing that she did not require a man's labor for conception. Among brute animals, it is well known that some females are fertile without the male; for instance, Origen <note>Agrippa likely conflates Origen’s *Against Celsus* with Augustine’s *Against Faustus the Manichaean*. Both authors used examples of "virgin birth" in nature to defend the possibility of the Virgin Birth of Christ.</note>, writing against Faustus, reports that it has been handed down in history that female vultures are thus fertile; moreover, antiquity discovered that certain mares conceive by the blowing of the West Wind <note>The *Zephyrus*; this was a common belief in ancient natural history, cited by authors like Virgil and Pliny the Elder.</note>, about which these things are sung:

<note>original: "Ore omnes versae in zephyrum stant rupibus altis / Exspectantque leves auras, et saepe sine ullis / Conjugiis vento gravidae." Now what shall I say of speech, that divine gift by which alone we <margin>Pimander 12</margin>
greatly excel the beasts? <term>Hermes Trismegistus</term> <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the *Corpus Hermeticum*, a series of texts on philosophy and magic; the "Pimander" is the first and most famous of these books.</note> considers speech to be of the
same value as immortality. And <term>Hesiod</term> <note>An early Greek poet, often cited alongside Homer as a foundational source for Greek mythology and ethics.</note> calls it the <margin>Hesiod</margin>
best treasure of man. Is not a woman more eloquent in speech
than a man, more articulate and abundant? Is it not true that as many of
us as are human, we first learned to speak only from our mothers or
our nurses? Indeed, nature itself, the <term>architect of things</term> <note>A common Renaissance philosophical phrase for God or the creative force of the universe.</note>,
looking sagaciously upon this human race, has so gifted
the female sex that a mute woman is hardly ever found.
It is truly a beautiful and laudable thing to excel men in that very
quality by which humans most especially surpass the other animals.
But let us return from <term>profane</term> <note>In this context, "profane" refers to secular or pagan classical literature (like Virgil and Hesiod), as opposed to "sacred" Scripture.</note> to sacred letters, as if by <term>postliminy</term> <note>original: "postliminio." A Roman legal term for the right of a person returning from captivity to regain their former status; here used metaphorically to mean returning to the "homeland" of the Bible.</note>,
and let us begin the matter from the very fountains of religion. We
know, first of all, that God undoubtedly blessed man for the sake of
the woman; which blessing the man, as one unworthy, did not
deserve to have until the woman had been created. With which the
proverb of Solomon agrees: "He who finds a good <margin>Proverbs 18</margin>
woman, finds a good thing, and draws a blessing from the Lord." And
that of <term>Ecclesiasticus</term> <note>Also known as the Wisdom of Sirach, a book of the Biblical Apocrypha/Deuterocanon.</note>: "Blessed is the husband of a good woman, the <margin>Ecclesiasticus 26</margin>
number of his years is doubled." And no man can be compared in
dignity to him who has been worthy of having a good woman.
For as Ecclesiasticus says: "A good woman is a grace <margin>Ecclesiasticus 26</margin>
above every grace." And therefore Solomon in the Proverbs <margin>Proverbs 12</margin>
calls her the crown of the man. And Paul, the glory of the man: <margin>1 Corinthians 11</margin>
moreover, "glory" is defined as the consummation and perfection of a thing.

### ON THE NOBILITY AND PREEMINENCE [OF THE FEMALE SEX]

...prudent, in that she was tempted by the devil. Man, therefore, sinned out of certain knowledge, but woman erred through ignorance and being deceived. For she was first tempted by the devil because he recognized her as the most excellent of all creatures. And as **Bernard** <margin>Bernard</margin> says: "The devil, seeing her admirable beauty, and knowing her to be such as he had previously recognized in the divine light—one who rejoiced in conversation with God above all the angels—cast his envy upon the woman alone, on account of her excellence." <note>original: "Bernardus" — Agrippa refers to St. Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a highly influential mystic and church doctor. Therefore, when Christ was born into this world in the greatest humility, so that by his humility he might atone for the pride of sin, he assumed the male sex of the first parent as the lowlier sex, rather than the female sex, which is more sublime and noble. <note>This is a provocative argument for the 16th century: Agrippa suggests that Christ became a man specifically because being male was a "humbler" or lower status than being female, thus making his incarnation a greater act of self-abasement.</note> Furthermore, because we were condemned on account of the sin of the man, and not the woman, God willed that the atonement for sin should occur in that sex in which the sin had originated; and that by the same sex which had been ignorantly deceived, vengeance should be taken <note>Referring to the prophecy in Genesis 3:15 that the offspring of the woman would crush the head of the serpent.</note>. ->HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...should assume. For on account of the woman, Christ was called the "Son of Man," <note>original: "filius hominis" — Agrippa argues that Christ's title "Son of Man" refers to his birth from a woman (Mary), rather than any biological contribution from a male.</note> not on account of the male. This is that great miracle at which the Prophet was so greatly amazed: because "a woman shall encompass a man," <margin>Jeremiah 31</margin> <note>original: "mulier circumdedit virum" — A reference to Jeremiah 31:22. In medieval and Renaissance exegesis, this was often interpreted as a prophecy of the Virgin Birth.</note> namely, when the male sex is encompassed by a virgin and she carries Christ in her body.

Also, when Christ arose from death, he appeared first to women, not to men. <margin>John 20; Mark 16</margin> Nor is it unknown that after the death of Christ, the men departed from the faith, whereas it is certain that women never fell away from the faith and the Christian religion. Furthermore, no persecution of the faith, no heresy, and no error in faith has ever emerged from women; it is known to be quite the opposite with men. Christ was betrayed, sold, bought, accused, condemned, suffered, crucified, and finally handed over to death by none other than men. 

Nay, he was even denied by his own Peter and abandoned by the other disciples; he was accompanied only by women <margin>Luke 24</margin> to the cross and even to the tomb. And even Pilate's wife herself, a Pagan woman, <margin>Matthew 27</margin> tried more to save Jesus than any of the men, even those who had believed in him. Added to this is the fact that nearly the whole school of theologians asserts that the Church then remained only in a single woman—namely, the Virgin Mary—and for this reason, the female sex is rightly and deservedly called "religious and sacred."

But if someone should say, along with Aristotle, <margin>Aristotle, *On Animals*</margin> <note>Aristotle's biological works were often cited in the Renaissance to argue for the natural inferiority of women. Agrippa is setting up a counter-argument using scripture.</note> that among all animals the males are stronger, wiser, and more noble, to this the more excellent and learned Apostle Paul responds, saying: <margin>1 Corinthians 1</margin> "The foolish things of the world God has chosen to confound the wise; and the weak things of the world God has chosen to confound the strong; and the ignoble and contemptible things of the world God has chosen, and those things which are not..."

But a woman did provoke him—being in this matter superior and bolder than the devil—stirring him up to speak a curse. <note>Referencing Job 2:9, where Job's wife tells him to "Curse God and die." Agrippa uses this ironically to argue that women possess a power of persuasion that even the devil lacks.</note>

Indeed, if it is permitted to bring Christ himself into this comparison <margin>Matthew 15</margin>—than whom nothing is more powerful, nothing wiser, since he is the eternal wisdom and power of God—did he not allow himself to be overcome by that little Canaanite woman? When he said: "It is not good to take the children’s bread and throw it to the dogs," she responded in turn: "Yes, Lord, for even the puppies eat the crumbs that fall from their masters' table." When Christ saw that he could not overcome her with this argument, he blessed her, saying: "Let it be done for you as you wish." 

Who was more fervent in faith than Peter, the first of the Apostles? <margin>John 18; Matthew 26 <note>original: "Matth. 20" — This is likely an error in the original text or OCR; the denial of Peter occurs in chapter 26.</note>; Mark 14; Luke 22</margin> A woman seduced him—no minor shepherd of the church—into denying Christ. Let the canon lawyers say what they will—that their church cannot err—yet a female Pope <margin>24. q. 1. *By the right way*; Platina on John VIII</margin> <note>Agrippa refers to the legend of Pope Joan, whom he identifies here as John VIII. The citation "24. q. 1" refers to the *Decretum Gratiani*, a collection of Church law, while Platina was a famous Renaissance historian of the Popes.</note> deceived it with an extraordinary imposture. 

However, some might say that these examples tend more toward the reproach of women than toward their praise.

**OF HENRY COR. AGRIPPA**

<margin>On the authority and use of the pallium, chapter *nisi*</margin> <note>original: "De aut & usu pall. c. nisi" — A reference to Canon Law within the Decretals of Gregory IX regarding the Pope’s authority.</note>
<margin>Digest, on the Aquilian Law, Law: If anyone with smoke</margin> <note>original: "ff. ad leg. aquiliam i. si quis fumo" — A reference to the Roman Civil Law (the Digest or Pandects). The specific law discussed damage caused by smoke to a neighbor's property.</note>
To which women will respond in this way: "If it is necessary for one of us to suffer either the loss of some good, or even of life itself, I would rather you perish than that I should be destroyed." This follows the example of Innocent III, who, in a certain decretal letter to a Cardinal acting as a legate from the Roman See, left this written: "If it should be necessary for you or me to be confounded, I would choose that you be confounded." <note>Pope Innocent III (reigned 1198–1216) was a powerful jurist; Agrippa uses his legal pragmatism to justify women’s self-preservation.</note> Furthermore, even in civil laws, it is granted to women that they may look out for themselves at the expense of another. 

And in the Sacred Scriptures themselves, is not the "iniquity" of a woman often blessed and extolled more than a man doing good? <note>Agrippa is provocatively referencing Ecclesiasticus 42:14, which says "Better is the iniquity of a man than a woman doing a good turn." He subverts this by showing that what men call "iniquity" in women is often divine wisdom or necessary strategy.</note> <margin>Genesis 31</margin> Is not Rachel praised, who deceived her father as he searched for his idols, eluding him with a clever invention? <note>Rachel stole her father Laban’s household gods and hid them by sitting on them, claiming she could not rise because of "the way of women" (menstruation).</note> <margin>Genesis 27</margin> Is not Rebecca also praised, because through a deception she obtained the blessing of the father for Jacob, and later more cautiously made him turn away from his brother's wrath? <margin>Joshua 2</margin> Rahab the harlot deceived those who were seeking the spies of Joshua, and it was reckoned to her as righteousness. 

<margin>Judith 11</margin> Jael went out to meet Sisera, and said to him: "Enter to me, my lord." And when he asked for water, she gave him milk to drink from a bottle, and covered him as he lay down. While Sisera was sleeping, she entered secretly and drove a nail into his head, and killed him—he who had trusted his life to her protection. And for this remarkable "treachery," the Scripture says: "Blessed among women be Jael in her tent." Read the history of Judith, and note her words to Holofernes: "Receive," she said, "the words of your handmaid, for if you follow them, the Lord will make you perfect; I will come and announce all things to you, so that I may lead..."

<meta>This page continues Henry Cornelius Agrippa’s argument regarding the biblical precedent for women’s "iniquity" being superior to men’s "goodness." # ON THE NOBLE AND EXCELLENT [SEX]

"I will lead you through the midst of Jerusalem, and you shall have all the people of Israel as sheep who have no shepherd, and not even one dog shall bark against you, for these things were told to me through the providence of God." <note>original: "te per mediam Hierusalem... per prouidentiā Dei" — This is the speech of Judith to Holofernes from the Book of Judith 11:19. Judith used deception to infiltrate the enemy camp.</note> And then, having lulled Holofernes to sleep with her charms, she struck him in the neck and cut off his head. What counsel, I ask, could be devised that is more iniquitous, what snares more cruel, or what betrayal more deceptive? And yet, for this very reason, Scripture blesses, praises, and extols her; and the "iniquity" of a woman was reputed to be far better than a man doing good. <note>Agrippa is subverting Ecclesiasticus 42:14: "Better is the iniquity of a man than a woman doing a good turn." He argues that Judith's "iniquity" (deceiving a tyrant) saved Israel, making it superior to any conventional "good deed" by a man.</note> 

But now let us return to the excellence of this most happy sex. To everyone, this can be the most evident argument: that the most worthy of all creatures—than whom there never was, nor ever shall be, one more worthy—was a woman. I speak, of course, of the most blessed Virgin Mary. Insofar as she was conceived without original sin, <note>Agrippa here supports the doctrine of the Immaculate Conception, which was a subject of intense theological debate in the 16th century.</note> not even Christ himself, as far as his humanity is concerned, will be greater. 

For there is a valid argument from Aristotle: "That genus whose best specimen is more noble than the best specimen of another genus, is itself more noble than that other genus." <note>original: "Cuius generis optimū est nobilius..." — A principle of comparative logic found in Aristotle’s *Topics*.</note> In the feminine genus, the best is the Virgin Mary; in the masculine, "there has not arisen one greater than John the Baptist." <note>A reference to Matthew 11:11.</note> And how much the divine Virgin excels him—she who is exalted above all the choirs of angels—no Catholic is ignorant. 

Similarly, it is permitted to argue that the genus whose worst specimen is worse than the worst of another, is also inferior to that other genus. Now, indeed, we know that the most vicious and worst of all creatures is a man—whether it be Judas, who betrayed Christ, of whom Christ said: "It would have been good for that man if he had not been born," <margin>Mark 14</margin>

<summary>Agrippa uses the example of Judith's heroic deception and a logical syllogism comparing the Virgin Mary to John the Baptist to argue for the inherent superiority of the female sex. He concludes by suggesting that the "worst" of humanity is also found in a man, Judas Iscariot.</summary>

<keywords>Judith and Holofernes, Virgin Mary, John the Baptist, Judas Iscariot, Aristotle's logic, feminine superiority, Immaculate Conception, biblical rhetoric</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Henry Cornelius Agrippa’s argument for the superiority of women. Having previously discussed the Virgin Mary and Judas Iscariot, he now transitions to the nature of the Antichrist and uses examples from the animal kingdom and the lineage of biblical patriarchs to argue that sin and vice originated with men.</meta>

**BY HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**

...or whether there will be someone even worse than him, some Antichrist, in whom the entire power of Satan shall dwell. Furthermore, Scripture reveals many men condemned to eternal torments, whereas nowhere is it read that any woman was condemned. To this is added the testimony of a certain prerogative found even in the nature of brute animals; for the queen of all birds, and the most noble, which is the eagle, is always found to be female, and never of the male sex. <note>In Renaissance natural history, it was a common (though incorrect) belief that the most powerful birds of prey were exclusively female, or that the female was the "true" representative of the species' nobility.</note> The Egyptians also reported that the Phoenix—the only bird of its kind—is nothing other than a female. Conversely, the "little king" of serpents, which they call the basilisk, the most pestilential of all venomous creatures, is only male; moreover, it is impossible for a female basilisk to be born. <note>The basilisk was legendary for being born from a cockerel's egg, hence the belief that it had no female counterpart.</note>

Furthermore, the excellence of this [female] sex, as well as its integrity and innocence, can be demonstrated abundantly by these arguments: that the origin of all evils came from men, and not at all from women. Indeed, it was that first-formed man, <margin>Genesis 3</margin> Adam, who dared to transgress the law of the Lord; he locked the gates of heaven and rendered us all subject to sin and death. For we have all sinned and we die in Adam, not in Eve. <note>Agrippa is making a radical theological point: while Eve was deceived, Adam sinned knowingly, and therefore the "Fall" is legally and spiritually attributed to the male line.</note> Likewise, the first-born of this man <margin>Genesis 4</margin> opened the gates of the lower world. He was the first to be envious, the first murderer, the first parricide, and the first to despair of God's mercy. <note>A reference to Cain, the son of Adam and Eve, who killed his brother Abel.</note> The first bigamist was Lamech; <margin>Genesis 9</margin> the first drunkard was Noah; the first to expose the shame of his own father was that son of Noah, Ham. <margin>Also Genesis 9</margin> The first tyrant, as well as the first idolater, was Nimrod. <margin>Genesis 10</margin> The first adulterer was a man, the first incestuous person was a man; furthermore, men were the first to enter into pacts with demons and to invent profane arts. The sons of Jacob were the first to sell their brother; <margin>Genesis 37, Exodus 1</margin> and Pharaoh the Egyptian [was the first to oppress them].

<summary>Agrippa argues that women are naturally more virtuous by noting that the Antichrist and the greatest biblical sinners—from Adam to Nimrod—were all men, while using (pseudo-)natural history about eagles and basilisks to support his claims.</summary>
<keywords>Antichrist, eagle, phoenix, basilisk, Adam, Cain, Noah, Nimrod, original sin, male vice, natural history</keywords>
