<meta>This page continues Henry Cornelius Agrippa’s argument that sin and vice are primarily male traits. Having just argued that original sin belongs to Adam alone, he now provides a historical and biblical catalog of male depravity compared to female virtue and self-sacrifice.</meta>

# ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE <note>original: "DE NOBILI. ET PRAECEL."</note>

...Lamech was the first to kill boys, and the first men indulged in unnatural lust, as witnessed by Sodom and Gomorrah—cities once famous that perished because of the crimes of men <margin>Genesis 18</margin>—everywhere we read of men being twice-married <note>original: "digamos," referring to those who marry a second time, which Agrippa views here as a sign of lack of restraint.</note> through reckless pleasure, polygamists, men of many lives, adulterers, and fornicators. Thus, Lamech, Abraham, Jacob, Esau, Joseph, Moses, Samson, Elkanah, Saul, David, Solomon, Asshur, Rehoboam, Abijah, Caleb, Ahasuerus, and countless others were men of many wives and concubines, each of whom kept several wives and, in addition, mistresses and concubines. Nor were they content with marriage to these women; to satisfy their lust, they even mingled with their handmaids. 

Truly, with the sole exception of Bathsheba, we find no woman anywhere who was not always content with only one man; nor will you find any woman who married twice <note>original: "digamā"</note> if she had already borne offspring by her first husband. For women are far more continent in their modesty and chastity than men themselves. We read that they often abstained from the company of their husbands because they were barren, and even introduced another woman as a wife, just as Sarah, Rachel, and many other barren women did, who brought in their handmaids so that they might raise up a lineage for their husbands. <margin>Genesis 16 &amp; 30</margin> 

But what man, I ask you—however old, cold, sterile, or unfit for marital duties <term>rei uxoriae ineptus</term>—ever showed such piety or mercy toward his wife that he would substitute someone else in his place to water the wife’s fertile womb with fruitful seed? <note>Agrippa argues that while women selflessly provided "surrogates" for their husbands to ensure heirs, men are too possessive and jealous to allow their wives the same opportunity, even when the man is sterile.</note> Even though we read that Lycurgus and Solon <margin>Lycurgus, Solon</margin> once established laws of this kind—namely, if someone was advanced in age and ill-suited for marriage, or otherwise... <note>Lycurgus of Sparta and Solon of Athens were legendary lawgivers. Agrippa refers to ancient laws that allowed or encouraged younger men to father children with the wives of older citizens to ensure the health of the state.</note>

<summary>Agrippa argues that sexual vice, polygamy, and violence originated with men, citing a long list of biblical patriarchs. He contrasts this with the inherent chastity of women, who he claims only sought multiple partners (through handmaids) to benefit their husbands' lineages.</summary>
<keywords>polygamy, biblical patriarchs, chastity, Lamech, Sarah and Rachel, Lycurgus, Solon, masculine vice, feminine virtue</keywords>

-># HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...had betrothed a girl while being too sluggish in the acts of love <note>original: "in uenerẽ [venerem] ignauior," referring to a lack of sexual potency or desire</note>, it was permitted for the wife to choose from the young men and close relatives one notable for his strength and character, who might sweetly play and sport with her <note>original: "colluderet & luctaretur," literally "play and wrestle," a common classical euphemism for sexual intimacy</note>, provided that the child brought forth would be attributed to the husband; nor would anything born of this arrangement be called foreign or adulterous. We read that these laws were indeed enacted, yet we do not read that they were ever practiced—not so much because of the harshness of men, but because the self-restraint of women rejected them.

There are still countless most excellent women who, through their notable modesty and even marital love, have far surpassed all men. Such were Abigail the wife of Nabal, Artemisia the wife of Mausolus, Argia the wife of Polynices the Theban, Julia the wife of Pompey, Portia the wife of Cato, Cornelia of the Gracchi, Messallina the wife of Sulpicius <note>Agrippa likely refers to the Messallina praised for her devotion to her husband in Roman history, rather than the more famous empress of the same name.</note>, Alcestis the wife of Admetus, Hypsicratea the wife of Mithridates, King of Pontus, as well as Dido the founder of Carthage, the Roman Lucretia, and Sulpicia the wife of Lentulus. 

There are infinite others whose faithfulness to virginity and modesty could not be altered even by death. Examples of these present themselves: Atalanta of Calydon, Camilla of the Volscians, Iphigenia the Greek, Cassandra, and Chryse. To these are added the Lacedaemonian, Spartan, Milesian, and Theban virgins, and countless others recorded for us in the histories of the Hebrews, the Greeks, and the foreign nations <note>original: "Barbarorum," referring to non-Greek or non-Roman peoples in the classical tradition.</note>.

<summary>Agrippa lists numerous historical and mythological women to prove that female virtue and marital fidelity far exceed those of men. He highlights that even when ancient laws permitted women to seek other partners for the sake of procreation, women’s own modesty led them to reject such license.</summary>

**ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE [OF THE FEMININE SEX]**

...tragedies <note>The text likely completes a word like "prejudices" or "misfortunes" from the previous page.</note> and such things, which if anyone were to look into with lynx-like eyes <note>original: "linceis oculis," a classical idiom for keen or sharp sight, referencing the mythological lynx known for seeing through solid objects</note> (as they say), he would find that wives are falsely accused; for no wicked wife ever fell to the lot of a good man. For there are no bad wives except to bad husbands; and although good wives sometimes fall to them, they are often corrupted by the fault of those very men. Do you not think that if women had been allowed to establish laws and write histories, what great tragedies they might have written concerning the inestimable malice of men? Among whom are found very many murderers, thieves, kidnappers, counterfeiters, arsonists, and traitors; who, even in the time of <margin>Joshua 7</margin> Joshua and <margin>2 Samuel 19</margin> King David, committed robberies in such numbers that they appointed leaders over their bands <margin>1 Kings 4</margin>; and even today their number is infinite. For this reason, all prisons are filled with men, and everywhere the crosses are burdened with the corpses of men.

But on the contrary, women have stood out as the inventors of all the liberal arts, and of every virtue and benefit; a fact which the feminine names of the arts and virtues themselves primarily demonstrate <note>In Latin, abstract nouns for virtues like *Sapientia* (Wisdom), *Justitia* (Justice), and the names of the arts like *Musica* (Music) are grammatically feminine.</note>. To this is added that notable fact, that the world itself is named after women—namely, after the nymph Asia, after Europe the daughter of Agenor, and after Libya the daughter of Epaphus, who is also called Africa. Finally, if a woman’s path is traced through every kind of virtue, she will everywhere hold the first place. For it was a woman who first vowed virginity to God: the Virgin Mary herself, who thereby merited to be the Mother of God. Women prophets were always inspired by a more divine spirit than men. This is witnessed by <margin>Lactantius, Divine Institutes</margin> Lactantius and <margin>Eusebius, Preparation for the Gospel</margin> Eusebius...

What again shall we say of Priscilla, that most holy woman, who instructed Apollos, an apostolic man and most learned in the law, who was a bishop of the Corinthians? <margin>Acts 18</margin> Nor was it shameful for an Apostle to learn from a woman what he should teach in the church. Add furthermore those women who, by their endurance in martyrdom and contempt for death, testified to the constancy of their faith, being no fewer in number than the men.

Nor should we pass over in silence here that miraculous mother, worthy of the memory of all good people, who saw her seven sons struck down in cruel martyrdom before her eyes and not only bore it with a good heart, but bravely exhorted them to death; <margin>2 Maccabees 7</margin> and she herself, trusting in God through all things, was consumed for the laws of her fathers after her sons <note>This refers to the unnamed mother in the Second Book of Maccabees, a symbol of maternal fortitude who encouraged her sons to die rather than violate Jewish law</note>. A Bavarian princess and Queen of the Lombards who played a crucial role in converting her people to Nicene Christianity</note>, daughter of the King of the Bavarians, convert the Lombards? ->**OF HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**<-

...to this day the Catholic faith and continuous works of piety shine forth. But lest anyone should doubt that women are capable of everything men are, let us examine the matter with examples; we will find that no excellent deed in any kind of virtue has ever been performed by men that has not also been carried out just as remarkably by women. In ancient times among the Gentiles <note>In this context, "Gentiles" refers to non-Christian, pagan civilizations such as the Greeks and Romans</note>, women were renowned in the priesthood, such as the Melissae of Cybele <note>original: *Melissæ Cybeles*. The "Bees," a title given to the priestesses of the mother goddess Cybele</note>, from whose name the priestesses of other goddesses were later called Melissae. Likewise, there were Hyperaustria, the priestess of Minerva; Mera, the priestess of Venus; and Iphigenia, the priestess of Diana. There were also the female priests of Bacchus, famous by many names, such as the Thyades, Maenads, Bacchae, Eliades, Mimallonides, Aedonides, Euhyades, Bassarides, and Triaterides <note>These various names refer to groups of women who participated in the ecstatic and sacred rites of the god Bacchus/Dionysus</note>. Among the Jews also, Miriam <note>original: *Maria*. Agrippa uses the Latin form of the name for the sister of Moses</note>, the sister of Moses, entered the sanctuary along with Aaron and was regarded as a priestess. In our own religion, although women are forbidden from the function of the priesthood, it is nevertheless handed down by famous histories that a woman once, by disguising her sex, ascended to the peak of the supreme pontificate <note>A reference to the legend of Pope Joan, a woman rumored to have reigned as Pope in the mid-9th century</note>. Nor are there lacking among our own people so many most holy abbesses and nuns, whom antiquity did not disdain to call priestesses. They shone in prophecy among the peoples of every region: Cassandra, the Sibyls, Miriam the sister of Moses, Deborah, Huldah, Anna, Elizabeth, the four daughters of Philip, and many other more recent holy women, such as Bridget and Hildegard <note>St. Bridget of Sweden and St. Hildegard of Bingen, two of the most influential female mystics and intellectuals of the Middle Ages</note>. Furthermore, in magic—the invincible discipline of either good or evil spirits—Circe and Medea were far more wonderful than others...

Furthermore, in philosophy, there stood out the illustrious Theano, wife of Pythagoras, and finally his daughter Dama, who was famous for explaining the obscure riddles of her father's teachings. Likewise, Aspasia and Diotima, disciples of Socrates, and Lastheneia of Mantinea <note>original: *Mantinea*</note> and Axiothea of Phlius <note>original: *Philesia Axiochia*. A title referring to her wisdom or perhaps a corruption of a title denoting her scholarly status</note>, whose sacred works Nicomachus translated into Greek. Let us move on to Oratory and Poetry. Here present themselves Armesia, nicknamed "the Man-Woman" <note>original: *Androgynæa*. Ameisia Sentia was a Roman woman who defended herself in court with such vigor that she was given this nickname</note>, Hortensia, Lucretia, Valeria, Copiola, Sappho, Corinna, Cornificia the Roman, and Erinna of Telos or Lesbos, who was nicknamed the epigrammatist. And in the writings of Sallust, we find Sempronia; among the legal scholars, Calpurnia. Indeed, if it were not forbidden today for women to study letters, there would even now be found most illustrious women far more excellent in intellect and learning than men. What is to be said of this: that by nature alone, women seem easily to surpass the masters of all disciplines? Do not the grammarians boast that they are the masters of speaking well? And yet, we learn this far better from our nurses and mothers than from the schoolmasters.

# OF HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

...grammarians? Was it not Cornelia, the mother, who shaped the Gracchi in the most eloquent language of the Greeks? And did not Istrine, his mother, teach the Greek language to Syles, son of Ariphithes, King of Scythia? Have not children born in colonies established among foreign nations always held to the speech of their mothers? For no other reason did Plato and Quintilian so carefully decree the selection of a suitable nurse for children, so that the child's tongue and speech might be formed correctly and clearly. 

Now indeed, are not even the Poets in their trifles and fables, and the Logicians <term>Dialecticians: scholars specializing in formal logic and the art of debate</term> in their contentious chatter, surpassed by women? An orator is nowhere so good or so successful that he is superior in persuasion to even a simple woman <note>original: *meretricula*. While literally "little prostitute," Agrippa uses it here to contrast a professional male orator with a woman of the lowest social standing to emphasize that even she possesses more natural eloquence.</note>. What arithmetician, by calculating falsely, can deceive a woman when she is settling a debt? Or what musician equals her in song and the sweetness of her voice? 

As for philosophers, mathematicians, and astrologers, are they not frequently inferior in their divinations and foreknowledge to rustic women? And very often an old woman <term>anicula: a term for an elderly woman, often used dismissively by male professionals, which Agrippa subverts here</term> defeats the physician. Socrates himself, the wisest of all men—if we are to believe the Pythian testimony <note>The Oracle at Delphi, known as the Pythia, famously declared Socrates the wisest man in Greece because he knew that he knew nothing.</note>—even in his old age did not disdain to learn something further from the woman Aspasia. Just as Apollos the Theologian <note>original: *Apollo Theologus*. A reference to Apollos from the New Testament (Acts 18:24-26), who was a learned man instructed in the faith by the couple Priscilla and Aquila.</note> did not fear to be instructed by Priscilla. 

If prudence is also required, we have the examples of Opis, who was numbered among the goddesses; Plotina, the wife of Trajan; Amalasuntha, the Queen of the Ostrogoths; and Emilia, the wife of Scipio. To these we add Deborah, a most prudent woman and the wife of Lappidoth, who herself, as is read in the <margin>Judges 4</margin> Book of Judges, for a time judged the people of Israel...

# [OF THE FEMALE SEX]

<margin>Judges 4</margin> [She judged Israel], and the children of Israel came up to her for all judgments. We read, moreover, in the history of the Kings <margin>2 Kings 11</margin> <note>original: *4 Reg 11*. That faithful writer of antiquity, Josephus, tells wonderful things about them. <margin>Josephus, *Antiquities of the Jews*</margin> To this is added Nicaula <note>original: *Nicaula*. The other, the mother of Solomon, wisely secured the kingdom for her son. Furthermore, in the invention of new things, we have the examples of Isis, Minerva, and Nicostrata <note>Nicostrata, also known as Carmenta, was a legendary prophetess credited with adapting the Greek alphabet into the Latin one.</note>. In the struggles of war, there was Tomyris, Queen of the Massagetae, who defeated Cyrus, the monarch of the Persians. Likewise, there was Camilla of the Volscian people, and Valisca <note>Valisca (or Vlasta) was a legendary Bohemian heroine said to have led an uprising of women to establish a female-led state.</note>.

->HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...Bathsheba.
Both of the powerful queens of Bohemia <note>Likely a reference to the legendary Queen Libuše and the warrior Vlasta, who featured prominently in early modern accounts of female-led history in Bohemia.</note>. Likewise, the Pandean women of India, the Amazons, the Candaces <note>The "Candace" was a title for the warrior-queens of the Kingdom of Kush, in modern-day Sudan.</note>, the Lemnian women, and the women of the Phoenicians, the Carians <note>original: *Chiazrum*, likely a variant or error for *Cariarum*, referring to the people of Caria in Asia Minor.</note>, and the Persians. We read of many other most illustrious women who, with wonderful courage, restored safety to their entire nation when they were in the greatest state of desperation. 

Among these is Judith, whom the blessed Jerome <margin>Jerome in the prologue to Judith</margin> extols with these words, saying: "Take the widow Judith as an example of chastity; proclaim her with triumphal praise and perpetual commendations. For He who rewards her chastity gave her as an example to be imitated not only by women, but also by men; He granted her such virtue that she conquered one who was invincible to all, and overcame the insuperable." <note>original: *Accipite Iudith uiduam castitatis exemplum...* St. Jerome’s prologue was the standard introduction to the Book of Judith in the Latin Vulgate Bible.</note>

We read also that a certain wise woman <margin>2 Samuel 20</margin> <note>original: *2. Reg. 20*. In the Latin Vulgate, 1 & 2 Samuel were titled 1 & 2 Kings.</note> called to Joab and delivered into his hands the head of Sheba, the enemy of David, so that she might preserve the city of Abel, which was a "mother of cities" in Israel. And a certain woman <margin>Judges 9</margin>, by throwing a fragment of a millstone, struck the head of Abimelech and crushed his skull, acting as the executor of God’s vengeance upon Abimelech; for he had done evil before the Lord against his father by killing his seventy brothers upon a single stone. 

So too Esther <margin>Esther 7, 8, 9</margin>, the wife of King Ahasuerus, not only delivered her people from a most shameful death, but furthermore adorned them with the highest honor. And when the city of Rome was besieged by the Volscians under the leadership of Gnaeus Marcius Coriolanus—a city which the men were unable to defend with arms—Veturia, a woman of great age and the mother of Coriolanus, saved it by rebuking her son. Artemisia likewise stripped the Rhodians, who were attacking her, of their fleet and subdued the island...

<summary>This page lists famous women from history and scripture—including Judith, Esther, and Veturia—who saved their nations through wisdom, courage, or divine intervention. Agrippa uses these examples to prove that women are capable of the highest forms of leadership and military salvation.</summary>

### ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE OF THE FEMININE SEX

...a statue was erected in the city of Rhodes which branded him <note>The text likely refers to a defeated general or adversary whose failure against a woman was memorialized as a mark of shame.</note> with a perpetual stigma. Now, who could sufficiently praise that most noble girl (though born of humble stock) who, in the year of our Lord 1428, when the kingdom of France was occupied by the English, took up arms in the manner of an Amazon? Leading the vanguard, she fought so vigorously and successfully that, having overcome the English in many battles, she restored the kingdom—which was already considered lost—to the King of the French. In perpetual memory of this deed, in the town of Genabum <note>The ancient Roman name for Orléans.</note>, which they now call Orléans, a sacred statue of the girl was erected on the bridge over the River Loire <note>original: *Ligurim*. Agrippa uses the Latin name for the Loire.</note>. 

I could still recount countless most excellent women from Greek, Latin, and barbarian histories, both ancient and more recent, but lest this work swell to a size too great, I have wished to strive for brevity. For Plutarch, Valerius, Boccaccio <note>original: *Bocatius*. Giovanni Boccaccio was the author of *De Mulieribus Claris* (On Famous Women), a major source for this genre of literature.</note>, and many others have written about them. This is why I have not spoken of so many praises of women and have remained silent on very many more; for I am not so arrogant <note>original: *ambitiosæ ceruicis*, literally "of an ambitious neck," an idiom for pride or stiff-necked arrogance.</note> as to presume that I could embrace the infinite excellences and virtues of women in so short a discourse. 

For who would be sufficient to enumerate the infinite praises of women, upon whom all our being, the preservation of the entire human race (which otherwise would shortly perish), and every family and state depends? This fact was not hidden from the founder of the city of Rome <note>Romulus, the legendary first king of Rome.</note>, who, while he lacked women, did not [hesitate] to enter into a most bitter war with the Sabines after their daughters were seized...

<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s argument regarding the historical importance of women, shifting from general praise to specific examples from Roman history. It describes how the founder Romulus recognized that his empire depended on women and details the legal honors bestowed upon them after the Sabine women brokered peace between the warring sides.</meta>

# HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

...[Romulus] did not doubt; for he knew that an empire of this kind would shortly perish if women were not present. Finally, after the Capitol had been seized by the Sabines, when the battle was being fought most bloodily in the middle of the forum with standards joined <note>This refers to the legendary Conflict between the early Romans and the Sabines following the "Rape of the Sabine Women," where the women were abducted to become wives to the Roman men.</note>, the fighting ceased through the intervention of the women between the two battle lines. Peace was finally made, a treaty struck, and they entered into a perpetual friendship. 

For this reason, Romulus gave their names to the <term>Curiae</term> <note>The thirty original political subdivisions or wards of the Roman people.</note>, and with the consent of the Romans, it was recorded in the public tablets that a woman should neither be forced to grind grain nor perform kitchen work <note>In the ancient world, grinding grain by hand was considered the most grueling form of slave labor; exempting wives from this was a mark of high social status.</note>. Furthermore, it was forbidden for a wife to receive a gift from her husband, or a husband from his wife, so that they might know that all their goods were held in common. From this, a custom at last emerged: those leading in a bride were ordered to say, "Where you are, I am," <note>original: *ubi tu ego*. This is a shortened version of the traditional Roman marriage formula: *Ubi tu Gaius, ego Gaia* ("Where you are Gaius, I am Gaia"), signifying legal equality in the household.</note> signifying "Where you are master, I am mistress; where you are lord, I am lady." 

Later, after the kings had been driven out, when the legions of the Volscians, under the leadership of <term>Coriolanus Martius</term> <note>A Roman general who, after being exiled, led an enemy army to the very gates of Rome.</note>, had encamped at the fifth milestone, the women dared to go out to them. Through their intervention, the city was saved; for which benefit, a notable <term>Temple of Fortune</term> <note>Agrippa refers to the *Fortuna Muliebris* (the Fortune of Women), a temple dedicated to the female power that saved Rome when its men could not.</note>...

<summary>Agrippa describes how the Sabine women mediated peace between the Romans and Sabines, leading to specific Roman laws that honored women's status and property rights.</summary>

# ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE [OF THE FEMALE SEX]

<margin>Or as in *Judging himself sufficient*, § *Thinking on all these things*. <note>Refers to the Preface of Justinian’s *Novels* 8, where the Emperor discusses his administrative reforms.</note></margin> [They] were granted [these honors] through persuasion; it was even permitted by law that the funerals of women be celebrated with public praises and orations, just as is done for illustrious men. Indeed, when a gift had to be sent to Delphic Apollo in accordance with a vow made by Camillus <note>Marcus Furius Camillus, a celebrated Roman general. After the defeat of the city of Veii, he vowed a tenth of the spoils to Apollo, but the treasury was empty.</note>, and there was not enough gold in the city, the women voluntarily contributed their own personal jewelry and ornaments. Furthermore, in that war which Cyrus waged against Astyages <note>Cyrus the Great, founder of the Persian Empire. Astyages was the last king of the Medes.</note>, when the Persian battle line had turned to flee, they were rebuked by the sharp scolding of the women; the line was restored anew, and this gave them a remarkable victory. Because of this, it was enacted as a cautionary law by Cyrus that whenever Persian kings were about to enter the city, they must pay out one gold coin to every woman. Even the Macedonian <note>Alexander the Great.</note>, having entered that city twice, paid out this sum just as many times. In addition, he ordered that the gift be doubled for pregnant women. Thus, by those ancient Persian kings and by the Romans—indeed, from the very cradle of the Roman city and empire—women were always gifted with every kind of honor.

<margin>Code, *on marriages*, last book. Or *on the consulship*, § *if a man*, column 4. <note>Citations from the *Corpus Juris Civilis*, the fundamental collection of Roman law.</note></margin> Furthermore, they were no less venerated by the emperors themselves. For this reason, the Emperor Justinian, even when establishing laws, judged that his wife <note>Empress Theodora, who had a significant influence on Justinian's legal reforms, particularly those regarding women's rights.</note> should be consulted and involved. Elsewhere the law states that because a wife "shines," she should deservedly feel her husband’s brilliance in honor; for as much as the man is raised on high, so too is his spouse. Thus, the wife of an emperor is called Empress, and the wife of a king a Queen, and the wife of a prince is called a Princess and illustrious, regardless of where she was born. And Ulpian <note>A famous 3rd-century Roman jurist whose work makes up a large part of the *Digest*.</note> says: "The Prince—that is, the Emperor—is exempt from the laws." <margin>Digest, *on laws*, law: *The Prince*.</margin> The *Augusta* <note>The title given to the Empress.</note>, however, who is the wife of the Emperor, although she is not strictly exempt from the laws by her own right, the Prince has nevertheless conferred upon her the same privileges that he himself possesses.

<summary>Agrippa lists historical instances where women's contributions—such as donating jewelry for a religious vow or shaming soldiers into victory—earned them permanent legal honors. He concludes by citing Roman law to show that a wife shares in the dignity and legal exemptions of her husband’s rank.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s argument by transitioning from the legal protections afforded to women in Roman and Canon law to their historical participation in military, civic, and economic life. ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE [OF THE FEMININE SEX]

...involved discipline: in the bow, in the sling, in the throwing of stones, in <margin>In the *Authenticum* <note>The *Authenticum* was a medieval collection of the *Novels* (new laws) of Emperor Justinian.</note>, [the chapter] "that no," paragraph "adulterer," collection 12. Azo. Speculator. Lycurgus. Plato. <note>Azo and "Speculator" (Guillaume Durand) were famous medieval legal commentators; Lycurgus and Plato are cited here for their philosophical defenses of equal training for women.</note></margin> archery, in the clash of arms, both on horseback and on foot, in the positioning of camps, and in the ordering of battle lines, in leading an army, and—to speak briefly—they assigned all the same exercises to women as were common to men. Let us read the trustworthy writers of antiquity, and we shall find that among the Getulians, the Bactrians, and the Galicians, it was the custom that men were surrendered to softness, while women tilled the fields, built houses, traded, rode on horseback, fought in battles, and did everything else which among us now men are accustomed to do. Among the Cantabrians <note>A people of ancient northern Spain.</note>, the men gave the dowry to the women, brothers were given in marriage by their sisters, and daughters were designated as heirs. Among the Scythians, Thracians, and Gauls, duties were common to both women and men. And in matters of war and peace, women were admitted to judgment and consultation, which the treaty struck by the Celts with Hannibal demonstrates in these very words: 

-> "If any of the Celts complains that he has been affected by an injury from any of the Carthaginians, let the Carthaginian magistrates or the commanders who are in Spain be the judges of that matter. If any of the Carthaginians shall have suffered any injustice from any of the Celts, let the women of the Celts pass judgment on that matter." <- 

But by the excessive tyranny of men, prevailing against divine right and the laws of nature, the liberty once given to women is now—as you might say—interdicted by laws, abolished by custom and usage, and extinguished by education. For as soon as a woman is born, she is kept at home from her earliest years in idleness, and as if [incapable] of a higher province... <note>original: "altioris prouinciæ"</note>

<summary>Agrippa lists ancient cultures where women performed roles now reserved for men—including farming, law, and war—and cites a treaty with Hannibal where Celtic women acted as supreme judges. He concludes that women's current subordinate status is not natural, but the result of male tyranny and restrictive education.</summary>

# ON THE NOBILITY AND EXCELLENCE [OF THE FEMALE SEX]

...curse, they will again raise the same objection from the words of Peter <margin>1 Peter 3</margin>, to which Paul also adds: "Women should be subject to their husbands," <margin>To the Colossians 3</margin> and "Women should keep silence in the church." <margin>To the Ephesians 5</margin> But whoever understands the various tropes of Scripture and its intent will easily see that these passages conflict [with my argument] only on the surface <note>original: *in cortice*. Agrippa uses the metaphor of "the bark" or "the shell" to refer to a literal, superficial reading of the text versus its deeper spiritual meaning.</note>. For there is indeed this order in the church: that men are placed before women in ministry <margin>To the Romans 2</margin>, just as the Jews were placed before the Greeks in the promise <margin>Acts 10</margin>. Yet God is no respecter of persons; for in Christ there is neither male nor female, but a new creature. 

Moreover, many things were permitted to men regarding women because of the hardness of their hearts <note>This is a clever rhetorical move where Agrippa applies Jesus's explanation for divorce laws (Matthew 19:8) to the social subordination of women, implying that men's "hardness of heart" is the cause of patriarchy, not divine will.</note>, just as divorce was once granted to the Jews; yet these things do nothing to diminish the dignity of women. On the contrary, when men fail and go astray, women have the power of judgment to the shame of men. And the Queen of Sheba herself shall judge the men of Jerusalem <note>A reference to Matthew 12:42, where the "Queen of the South" is held up as a model of wisdom who will condemn the faithless.</note>. Therefore, those who, justified by faith, have become children of Abraham—children, I say, of the promise—are subject to woman and are bound by the command of God speaking to Abraham: "In all things that Sarah says to you, listen to her voice." <margin>Genesis 21</margin>

Now at last, to summarize as briefly as possible, I have shown the excellence of the female sex from her name, her order, her place of creation, her material <note>Agrippa previously argued that woman is superior because she was created in Paradise (place), from Adam's rib rather than dust (material), and her name "Eve" is closer to the divine name than "Adam."</note>, and what dignity woman has received from God above man. Furthermore, we have demonstrated it through religion, nature, human laws, and now through various authorities, reason, and the examples set forth. Yet I have not said as much as I have left still to be said, because I was not moved by ambition, or...

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page concludes Henricus Cornelius Agrippa’s "On the Nobility and Excellence of the Female Sex." Following his defense of women's spiritual and intellectual standing against contemporary legal and religious biases, Agrippa offers his closing remarks, framed as an act of duty rather than a search for personal glory.</meta>

->### BY HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

I have come to write not for the sake of my own praise, but out of duty and truth. If I were to remain silent, I might seem, like a sacrilegious person, to steal away the praises due to so devoted a sex <note>original: *deuoto sexui*. In the 16th century, this phrase often implied that women possessed a natural inclination toward piety and religious devotion.</note> through an impious silence—as if I were burying the talent entrusted to me <note>original: *talentum mihi creditum*. A reference to the Parable of the Talents in the Gospel of Matthew (25:14–30), where a servant is punished for burying the money (talent) entrusted to him instead of using it. Agrippa views his ability to advocate for women as a divine gift he is obligated to share.</note>. 

However, if anyone more diligent than I should find some argument passed over by us which he thinks contributes to the building of this work of ours, I shall believe myself not criticized but assisted by him; for he will have made this good effort of ours better through his own genius and learning. 

Lest the work itself grow into too large a volume, let this be the end of it.

<summary>In this concluding passage, Agrippa justifies his treatise as a moral obligation to restore the "stolen" honors of women and invites future scholars to expand upon his arguments.</summary>
<keywords>Henricus Cornelius Agrippa, conclusion, Parable of the Talents, feminine virtue, rhetoric, Renaissance feminism, intellectual duty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s "Expostulatio" (protest) against the Franciscan friar Jean Catilinet. Agrippa is defending himself against accusations of heresy that Catilinet leveled against him during public sermons while Agrippa was away. He frames his defense by contrasting Catilinet’s hostile behavior with the Christian virtues of charity and peace as taught by St. Paul and the Gospels.</meta>

->HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...what did I desire, except to attain honor from virtue? Yet you slandered me even while I was absent, across the furthest distances between places, with such unworthy bitterness of speech. <margin>Romans 12</margin> You who, by the profession of your name <note>As a Franciscan friar, Catilinet’s "name" or "profession" bound him to a life of Christian virtue and peace.</note>, ought to do what Paul teaches the Romans: to hate evil and cling to the good, to love brotherly charity, and not to speak evil—but rather to bless even those who persecute you, and to have peace with all people as far as possible. Truly, you have not done a thing worthy of your profession or of a Christian teacher. You should have exhorted the Christian people toward those things which belong to Christ: toward the works of the spirit, charity, and peace, and the other virtues which Paul <margin>Galatians 5</margin> commemorates to the Galatians. For he who urges hatred, wrath, strife, rivalry, and enmity, urges not the works of the spirit, but of the flesh. Nothing ought to be more foreign to a Christian man, nor is anything more unsuitable for a Christian teacher, than to teach and persuade such things.

For Christ, the author of our religion, and the Apostles, and all of Holy Scripture (as you ought to know better than I) call out for peace and tranquility. <margin>John 14 <note>The original marginalia reads "Ioan. 4," but the quotation "Peace I leave with you" is found in John 14:27.</note></margin> Hence the Evangelist John records that Christ said to his disciples: "My peace I give to you, my peace I leave with you" <note>original: *Pacem meam do uobis, pacem meam relinquo uobis*</note>. <margin>Hebrews 12</margin> And Paul says to the Hebrews: "Pursue peace with all people, and holiness, without which no one will see God" <note>original: *Pacem sequamini cum omnibus, & sanctimoniam, sine qua nemo uidebit Deum*</note>. To this peace, it ought to be your duty, and also mine, not only to exhort but also to pray. <margin>Ephesians 4</margin> Does not the Apostle, warning the Ephesians, say: "Let no evil speech proceed from your mouth"? ### COMPLAINT AGAINST CATILINET

The Epistle to the Corinthians so detests slander <note>original: "maledicentiam"</note> that <margin>1 Cor. 5</margin> it judges that one should not even take food with slanderers. And in the same <margin>1 Cor. 6</margin> epistle, shortly after, he mentions slanderers among those who will not possess the Kingdom of God. He commands the same to the Colossians, saying: Put away anger, indignation, malice, blasphemy, and foul language. <margin>1 Pet. 3</margin> Peter also commands that whoever wishes to love life and see good days should restrain his tongue from evil. And James <margin>James 4</margin> says: Do not disparage one another, brothers. Thus we are taught everywhere by the Apostles to abstain from slander and the offense of anyone, which are the seeds of envy and discord—the kind of seeds you very recently sowed against me among the people and the princess. <margin>Catilinet’s Hypocrisy</margin> While just before these most recently past Easter festivals in Ghent, Flanders, in the presence of our most illustrious Princess <note>Margaret of Austria (1480–1530), the Governor of the Habsburg Netherlands and Agrippa’s patroness.</note>, of the whole...

<summary>Agrippa cites several New Testament passages (Paul, Peter, and James) to condemn slander as a sin that bars one from the Kingdom of God, specifically accusing Catilinet of sowing discord in Ghent before Margaret of Austria.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Henry Cornelius Agrippa’s defense against the Franciscan monk Jean Catilinet. Agrippa is responding to the specific accusation that his lectures on Kabbalah and Johann Reuchlin’s work were "heretical" and "Jewish."</meta>

HENRY COR. AGRIPPA

...[that I practice] that art; that, having despised the holy fathers and Catholic doctors, I prefer the rabbis of the Jews; and that I twist the sacred scriptures toward heretical arts and the Jewish Talmud. <margin>Agrippa’s profession of faith.</margin> In truth, I am a Christian, and neither death nor life shall separate me from the faith of Christ. I prefer Christian doctors above all others; nevertheless, I do not despise the Jewish rabbis. And if I have erred in anything as I might, I nonetheless do not wish to be a heretic, nor do I intend to "Judaize" <note>To "Judaize" was a common theological accusation meaning to adopt Jewish customs or interpretations of scripture in a way that supposedly undermined Christian doctrine.</note>. As for condemned and prohibited arts, so far am I from teaching them that I do not even wish to learn them. I never twist the holy scriptures; rather, according to the various expositions of different doctors, I employ them in various ways as testimony. <margin>Agrippa’s lectures in Dole, Burgundy, in the year 1509.</margin> I have not taught heretical arts or the errors of the Jews. Instead, I expounded with much labor and many sleepless nights the Christian and Catholic book entitled *On the Mirific Word* <note>original: "De Verbo Mirifico," a 1494 work by Johann Reuchlin that explored the name of God through a Christianized interpretation of the Kabbalah.</note> by the Christian doctor Johann Reuchlin of Pforzheim. This was not done secretly in private rooms, but in public schools, in a public auditorium, through public lectures which I gave for free to the honor of the most illustrious Princess Margaret and the University of Dole. Nor was my audience lacking in men of both great gravity and learning, including the venerable fathers of the senatorial rank of the Parliament of Dole, as well as the most erudite masters and doctors of that university, and the ordinary lecturers. Among them, the reverend Lord Simon Vernerius, Vice-Chancellor of that university and conservator of the Church of Dole, a Dean and Doctor of both laws <note>original: "utriusque iuris," referring to his mastery of both Civil Law and Canon (Church) Law.</note>, never missed attending even a single one of my lectures. <margin>Catilinet’s malice.</margin> But you, to whom I am entirely unknown, who were never present at my lectures, nor heard me speaking anywhere else, even in private...

<meta>This page continues Henry Cornelius Agrippa’s formal defense, known as the *Expostulatio*, addressed to the Franciscan friar Jean Catilinet. Agrippa continues to rebuke the friar for attacking his character and lectures in public sermons without ever having met him.</meta>

### EXPOSTULATION AGAINST CATILINET

[...you have] heard, nor as far as I know, have you ever seen me; yet you dared to pass such an unfair judgment upon me, which could and should have more rightly been omitted—both because it is utterly false, and also because it is not fitting for a religious man to insert such insults and slanders into the most holy and serious Christian sermons. Indeed, it is most unworthy of the divine office of preaching. For to scatter insults, curses, and hatred is not a sign of sincerity, nor is it speaking from Christ, but is rather (to use the words of Paul) **adulterating the word of God**, <margin>2 Corinthians 4</margin> <note>original: "Verbum Dei adulterari." In this context, Agrippa uses Paul’s language from 2 Corinthians 4:2 to accuse Catilinet of "corrupting" or "watering down" the divine message by mixing it with personal malice and lies.</note> which that great Apostle, set apart for the Gentiles, says he never did—and certainly it should not be done by anyone who wishes to be a Christian teacher. 

Nevertheless, you have done these things. Without cause and without any fault of mine, you have plotted evil against me. You have snatched away my good reputation; you have stained my good name with the impurity of your hypocrisy; and, to the destruction of your own soul, you have spoken a lie against me. For Christ says in the Gospel of Matthew: *Whosoever shall say to his bro[ther...]* <note>Agrippa is beginning to quote Matthew 5:22: "Whosoever shall say to his brother, 'Raca,' shall be in danger of the council; but whosoever shall say, 'Thou fool,' shall be in danger of hell fire."</note>

<summary>Agrippa rebukes Catilinet for using the pulpit to spread lies and insults, arguing that such behavior "adulterates" the Word of God and violates the duties of a Christian teacher.</summary>

### THE WORKS OF HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

<margin>Matthew 5</margin> For the five-lettered <note>original: *pentagrammus*. Agrippa refers to the name of Jesus as the *Pentagrammaton* (YHShVH), a concept in Christian Kabbalah where the letter *Shin* is added to the four-letter Name of God (YHVH) to represent the Incarnation.</note> Jesus Christ himself, in the Gospel of Matthew, promises blessedness to those who are cursed by men and suffer persecution for the sake of his name. <margin>1 Peter 4</margin> And Peter likewise calls those blessed who are reproached for the Lord’s sake. <margin>Agrippa’s Profession of Faith</margin> What part have I with the Jews—I, who confess Jesus Christ as the Son of God and worship Him most piously? Why should I be counted among heretics—I, who observe and teach, to the best of my ability, the true unity of the Church, its most wholesome precepts, and the rites of the sacred councils and canons, by which faith is strengthened and purged of every wicked heresy?

Those who were present are able to know—I mean those most upright and learned men—they are able to judge and testify if anything was ever said by me that offends the Christian faith or the Church; unless, perhaps, you are going to say that they "Judaize" <note>To "Judaize" was a common accusation against scholars like Agrippa who studied Hebrew and Jewish texts to better understand Christian theology.</note> along with me and are heretics themselves. Indeed, it would be neither fitting nor Christian for them to have tolerated hearing it in public, to have consented by remaining silent and not contradicting it, and—what is more—to have approved of it by rewarding me. For this lecture was the very reason they received me into the college and granted me both an ordinary and a regency lectureship with a stipend. Would they have done so if it had been shameful, Jewish, or heretical for me to lecture on it publicly?

<margin>A common injury</margin> This injury is no longer mine alone, but belongs to the entire Parliamentary Senate and the whole University of Dole. See into what an abyss you have cast yourself! While you wished to pursue me with calumny, you have deceived the prince, the nobles, and the whole court with a wicked pretense; you have exposed the Senate and the University to mockery, and you have profaned the word of God. Is this...

<margin>Galatians 6. Thessalonians 3.</margin>

<note>A large decorative initial S, often featuring floral or geometric patterns, begins the body of the text.</note> **S**o that I might be able to fulfill my devotion toward you with equal knowledge and leisure for writing, most illustrious Princess: I would have already undertaken some arduous work in a massive volume, and I would have explained either the deepest mysteries of the Holy Scriptures <note>original: *sacrarum literarum*. In the Renaissance, this term referred specifically to the Bible and its theological interpretation.</note> or the most difficult principles of Gospel philosophy <note>original: *Euangelicae philosophiae*. This was a term favored by Christian Humanists like Erasmus to describe a simplified, lived Christianity based on the teachings of Christ rather than complex medieval scholasticism.</note>, and those truths which still lie hidden in the shadows. <meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle to Margaret of Valois, which began on the previous page. Agrippa is transitioning from praising her character to presenting his "Oration on the Sacrament of Marriage," framing it as a modest gift that her own virtuous life already exemplifies.</meta>

...and integrity of character, so that even the most sublime teaching <note>original: *doctrina*. In this context, it refers to formal instruction or academic knowledge, which Agrippa argues is eclipsed by Margaret’s natural virtue.</note> can scarcely be sufficient. Therefore, we must be granted pardon if we have offered this small, due gift <note>original: *munusculum*. A "little gift," a humble term frequently used by Renaissance authors when presenting their books to powerful patrons.</note>—not as something befitting your excellence, but according to our own meager strength and the constraints of time.

Accept, therefore, with a kind spirit, this brief declamation <term>declamation</term><note>A *declamatio* was a specific genre of formal rhetorical speech used by Humanists to explore moral, social, or religious themes, often intended to persuade the audience through elegant style.</note> on Marriage. It is not that I wish to teach you about this way of life, which you know most perfectly and have observed most holily; indeed, what this entire oration expresses in words, you have until now most religiously performed in your very deeds. Thus, it is not you who requires the instruction of this oration, but rather this oration which requires you as its example. 

For this reason, it had to be dedicated to you above all others, so that your life’s work might bear witness to our writings. This work may then stand against the insulting and sacrilegious objections <note>Agrippa is likely referring to contemporary theological debates regarding the "superiority" of celibacy over marriage, or perhaps specific critics who questioned the spiritual validity of the matrimonial bond.</note> of others, countering them not so much with words as with [deeds...]

<meta>This page begins the formal text of Agrippa’s declamation, moving from the personal dedication to Margaret of Valois (seen on the previous page) to the theological and historical defense of marriage as the primary and most ancient sacrament.</meta>

# HEINRICH
# CORNELIUS AGRIPPA’S
## Declamation on the
## Sacrament of Marriage.

<margin>Genesis 1</margin> <note>original: *Gen. 1.* Agrippa refers to the creation narrative where God commands humanity to "increase and multiply."</note>
->**T**HE SACRAMENT<-
of marriage is the most ancient of all; it was the first to be instituted and commanded by God at the very beginning of things, once humanity had been created. Among the peoples of every nation, it has always been celebrated with the highest 
<margin>John 2</margin> <note>original: *Ioan. 2.* A reference to the Wedding at Cana, where Jesus performed his first miracle, thereby sanctifying the institution of marriage.</note>
veneration, hallowed by religion, made holy by laws, and illustrated by miracles and wonders. Indeed, it is worthier than all other mysteries <term>mysteries</term><note>In this context, the Latin *misteria* refers to the sacraments or divine rites of the Church. Agrippa argues that marriage holds a unique dignity because of its antiquity.</note> and laws for this reason: it was instituted before any of them. 

It was instituted, I say—not by the opinion of men 
<margin>Case 32, question 2, "Whatever"</margin> <note>original: *32. q. 2. qcquid*. This is a citation from the *Decretum Gratiani*, the foundational collection of Canon Law. The specific canon "Quicquid" discusses the nature of lawful marriage.</note> 
but by God, the Prince of all, from the first origin of the world, before sin, in the time of innocence: for the lawful duty of propagating the human species. 

While all other commands and mysteries emerged only after the fall of man—for the purpose of restoration and protection, as a remedy, an improvement, or lastly, as a punishment—these others are scarcely accepted by a few, whereas marriage has spread through every corner of the world...

<summary>Agrippa introduces marriage as the oldest and most divine institution, arguing that it holds a higher dignity than other sacraments because it was established by God before the Fall of Man.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa’s theological defense of marriage. It follows the previous page’s discussion of marriage being instituted before the Fall of Man, contrasting it with other sacraments that were only established as remedies for sin.</meta>

### HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

...that this most holy bond is joined by such an indissoluble glue, and is so enduring, that the husband might always live and subsist in the wife, and the wife in the husband: as bone from bones and flesh from flesh. For that was the first precept of God when instituting marriage: 
<margin>Genesis 2</margin> <note>original: *Gen. 2.* Agrippa quotes the foundational biblical verse for marriage: "Therefore shall a man leave his father and his mother, and shall cleave unto his wife: and they shall be one flesh."</note> 
a man shall leave his father and his mother, and shall cleave to his wife, and they two shall be in one flesh. This we read not only in the Old Testament, but it is confirmed in the Gospel as well. 
<margin>Matthew 19</margin> 
<margin>Mark 10</margin> 
<margin>Case 28, question 7</margin> <note>original: *xxviij. qō. vij*. This and the following marginal notes refer to the *Decretum Gratiani*, the primary collection of Canon Law used in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> 
<margin>only</margin> <note>original: *tantum*.</note> 
<margin>Case 28, question 5, "If indeed"</margin> <note>original: *xxviij. q. v. si uero*. Referring to a specific legal canon regarding the separation of spouses.</note> 
<margin>matter ibid. chapter "Horrible." 2 chapters following.</margin> <note>original: *rē ibidē. c. hor- / rēdus. 2 c. seq.* A reference to a legal chapter titled *Horrendum*.</note> 
<margin>Case 27, question 2, chapter 6</margin> <note>original: *xxvij. q. ij. c. vi.*</note> 
Thus, it is not permitted for this bond to be dissolved at any time by any diversity of habits, nor by any satiety or weariness, nor by old age, nor sterility, nor illness, nor pestilence, nor leprosy <term>elephantiasis</term><note>original: *elephantico*. In the medical language of the time, this referred to a severe form of leprosy or skin disease that was often cited in legal debates about whether a spouse could be abandoned.</note>, nor any other contagious disease; finally, it cannot be broken by any crime of heresy, nor by any human force. This is by the command of Christ: that those whom the omnipotence of God has joined together, no power should presume to separate. 

Nor is it permitted for any cause for a wife to depart from her husband, or a husband from his wife, because it is permitted to no one to depart from himself, nor for anyone to abandon himself. For she who was made from the husband's rib—flesh from the same flesh, the same bone from his bones—was created to be a helpmate to the man. God never wished her to be separated from the man, 
<margin>Case 28, question 7, "intervening"</margin> <note>original: *xxviij. qō. vij. in / terueniente.*</note> 
<margin>Chapter 6 following, Case 28</margin> <note>original: *vi. c. se. xxviij.*</note> 
<margin>Question 5, question for God.</margin> <note>original: *qō. v. qō deo.*</note> 
except on account of fornication, which is the sole cause God excepted. But if the covenant and unity of marriage is dissolved for any other cause, those who are separated remain nonetheless spouses to one another; nor can they be excused from adultery if they are joined to others after their divorce. 
<margin>Matthew 19</margin> 
And therefore Christ adds, saying: "Whoever marries another commits adultery, and whoever marries her who is divorced commits adultery."

<summary>Agrippa argues for the absolute indissolubility of marriage, citing both Genesis and the Gospels to claim that the union creates a single fleshly identity that no human law or illness can break. He asserts that divorce is only permissible in cases of fornication and that any remarriage after separation is considered adultery.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa’s discourse on the sanctity of marriage, transitioning from the indissolubility of the bond to the specific purposes and spiritual benefits of the union, even in old age.</meta>

### HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

Now we shall relate to what ends and for what uses this venerable and most worthy sacrament was commanded: namely, for the sake of mutual help, for the propagation of the species, and for the avoidance of fornication. The first of these is found where God instituted this sacred mystery with these words: 
<margin>Genesis 1</margin> <note>original: *Gen. 1.* Although the marginal note cites Genesis 1, the following quote is from Genesis 2:18.</note> 
"It is not good for man to be alone; let us make for him a helper like unto himself." For man (since he is by nature a most social animal) will only then truly and rightly fulfill the duty of humanity, and provide a guardian for his life and his affairs, when he has entered into the stable and indissoluble society of marriage. And therefore, even for the elderly, the decrepit, and those in whom no power of procreating remains and no hope of offspring is left: nevertheless, it is lawful to marry; and (if it is right to say) it is often necessary. By this means, they may lead the old age of their life in the company of a beloved wife more pleasantly, more safely, and more securely; an example of which is found in David, already old, with the Shunammite girl. 
<margin>1 Kings, chapter 1</margin> <note>original: *3. Reg. c. 1.* In the Latin Vulgate Bible used by Agrippa, the books of Kings are titled 1-4 Regum; thus, *3 Regum* corresponds to the modern 1 Kings.</note> 
For what holier or more pleasant society could occur among men? What safer? What more secure? What more chaste than that of husband and wife, when each of them is the same as the other: two bodies in one conforming soul; in two bodies, one soul and one consensus. Only spouses do not envy one another; they alone love each other infinitely, since each of them depends entirely on the other and rests in the other: one flesh, one mind, one concord. The same sorrows are theirs, the same joys, the same and equal will for both, a common sharing of profit, the same riches, the same poverty, equal dignity, always of one bedchamber and one table, by night and by...

<summary>Agrippa outlines the three purposes of marriage—mutual assistance, procreation, and the avoidance of sin—emphasizing that marriage remains vital and holy even for the elderly as a source of companionship and security.</summary>
<keywords>sacrament of marriage, social animal, mutual help, Genesis, King David, Abishag the Shunammite, companionship in old age, unity of soul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa’s discourse on marriage, moving from the benefits of companionship to the absolute necessity of monogamy and the priority of the marital bond over all other family relationships.</meta>

->### ON THE SACRAMENT OF MARRIAGE<-

<unclear>daily</unclear> companions: departing from each other neither in sleep nor in waking: but their life is joined in all actions, labors, dangers, and in every fortune, serving each other in turn for as long as they live. They accompany each other even to the exit of life: they never depart unless through death, and once one is dead, the other can scarcely survive. So great is conjugal charity <note>In this context, *charity* refers to *caritas*, the highest form of selfless, Christian love.</note> that those whom life has joined, death itself can scarcely tear asunder. Such is the perseverance of marriage, and such its unity, that there should be one husband for one wife, and for one husband a single wife; for one rib from the beginning was turned into one wife. And God said, "They two shall be in one flesh." <margin>Genesis 2</margin> He did not say three, nor more. And in <margin>Genesis 7</margin> the ark of Noah, God commanded no more women than men to be preserved, so that for one man there should be only one single spouse. For where there is a plurality of spouses, there the unity of marriage ceases to exist. 

Therefore, whatever single wife a man has taken, let him preserve her with inviolate love, with constant affection until the final boundary of life, and with continuous memory. Let the father yield, let the mother yield, let the children yield, let brothers and sisters yield, and let the whole crowd of friends yield to the benevolence of the husband and wife—and indeed, by right. For father, mother, children, brothers, sisters, and kin are all works of nature and fortune. Husband and wife are a mystery of God. <note>original: *mysterium*. In Agrippa's thought, a "mystery" is a sacred truth or a divine secret that reflects a higher spiritual reality.</note> Furthermore, the husband had a wife, and the wife a husband, before they had a father, a mother, a brother, or children. <note>Agrippa argues that since the union of Adam and Eve preceded the birth of any children or the existence of other social structures, the marital bond is the primary and most ancient human relationship.</note> And therefore it is commanded for a man to leave father, mother, sisters, brothers, and children, and to cling to his wife: <margin>Institutes: In what ways the right of paternal power is dissolved.</margin> <note>original: *Instit. quibus mo. ius. pat. pote. soluat.* This is a legal citation to the *Institutes* of Justinian, a foundational work of Roman law, specifically Book 1, Title 12, discussing the legal separation of a son from his father’s authority.</note> so that what was given to him first of all, he should preserve above all others until the very end. Nor ever...

<summary>Agrippa argues for the absolute monogamy and indissolubility of marriage, asserting that the bond between husband and wife is the most ancient and sacred of all human ties, superseding even the duty to parents or children.</summary>

Therefore, no law prohibits children from departing from their parents, nor parents from their children; sometimes necessity compels it, utility persuades it, or judgment impels it. Often children are <term>emancipated</term> <note>Legally freed from their father's control.</note>; they claim freedom for themselves for the sake of religion; often as foreigners they live for themselves and seek a home elsewhere. But for a wife to depart from her husband, or a husband from his wife, no law, no necessity, no utility, no judgment, no <term>repudiation</term> <note>A formal legal rejection or divorce.</note>, no religious vow, no license, and no absence permits. If either person departs from the other, or neglects to be joined to them, they must necessarily lead a most unhappy life, as one deserted and solitary, destitute of all joy and help. This is because they have, through neglect, despised the "help" created for them by God <note>A reference to Eve being created as a "helpmeet" in Genesis 2:18.</note> and the partner of joy given to them; or, through contempt, they have dared to cast that partner away.

¶ We have said that the other end <note>The second "final cause" or purpose.</note> of marriage is the procreation of children, just as we read where God blessed Adam and Eve, saying: "Increase and multiply, and fill the earth." This blessing was restored again in its entirety after the Flood with these same words. The effect of this is that man returns to nature what he received as a loan from nature; he produces and nourishes children similar to himself in the image of God, and returns the public society of the human race through a certain succession, thus keeping it perpetual. He who neglected this blessing under the <term>Old Law</term> <note>The Mosaic Law of the Old Testament.</note> was considered cursed by everyone and most unhappy, just as it is written: "Cursed is the sterile woman who has no seed in Israel, and blessed is he whose seed is in Zion." <note>original: *Maledicta sterilis quæ non habet semen in Israël, & beatus cuius est semē in Syon*. This is a common medieval paraphrase of biblical themes regarding the shame of infertility in the Old Testament.</note> Hence Abraham...

**HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**

He indeed is a true heir, a true son, whose mother is married to a certain and proven father without the infamy of sin, without the insult of relatives, and without offense to God. She enters the union with the dignity of marriage and with the certain fruit <note>The "fruit" refers to the expectation of legitimate children.</note> of offspring, adhering to her husband perpetually without any disgrace. <margin>Case 32, Question 5, Chapter *Facit* <note>original: *xxxij.q.v.c.f.* This refers to the *Decretum Gratiani*, a collection of Canon Law. It increases the lineage, reconciles kin, and pleases and reveres God.

[¶] I have called the third use of marriage the avoidance of fornication, <margin>1 Corinthians 7</margin> as the Apostle Paul commands: let him who cannot practice self-restraint marry. For marriage excuses the person who lacks self-control from sin, from fornication, and from adultery. Even if the desire is improper, for the sake of avoiding such lack of restraint, Paul even commands widows to remarry, <margin>1 Timothy 5</margin> saying: "I desire the younger widows to marry, to be mistresses of families, to bear children, and to give the adversary no occasion for slander." Yet, the power and dignity of marriage is no less for those who lack self-restraint, even though they may enter into it less worthily. For they are joined without fault, and the marital debt <note>In medieval theology, the "marital debt" refers to the obligation of spouses to provide sexual intimacy to one another upon request, viewed here as a duty that prevents sin.</note> is paid without sin, even if the demand for it is immoderate, and even if the spouses mingle with one another for a reason other than the procreation of offspring. <margin>Case 27, Question 2, Chapter *Si tu*</margin> <margin>Case 32, Question 2, Chapter *Quicquid*</margin> This is because marriage was instituted not only for the sake of companionship and the procreation of children, but also for the sake of mutually sustaining one another in weakness, for tolerating superfluous intercourse while avoiding illicit acts. <margin>Case 32, Question 2, Chapter *Quicquid*</margin> It was commanded for mutual service, as the Apostle says: "For it is better to marry than to burn," <margin>1 Corinthians 7</margin> and elsewhere he says of widows: "Lest on account of..."

## BY HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

<meta>This page continues Agrippa's argument on the necessity and social utility of marriage. It picks up from the previous page's discussion on the husband's role in the household, completing a sentence regarding the character of the wife and children.</meta>

# HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

...[only will these things come to pass] unless you obtain an excellent wife (for a bad wife does not usually befall any but bad husbands) and children like her.

[Ornamental initial N] **N**either can a name be extended, nor offspring perpetuated, nor a lineage increased, nor a family exist, without a wife. Without her, one cannot be called a <term>head of a household</term> <note>original: *paterfamilias*; in Roman law, this was the male head of a domestic group who held absolute legal power over his family.</note>, nor can a place be named a home, nor a republic stand, nor can any empire endure. This fact was not hidden even from the founder of the Roman Empire <note>Romulus, the legendary first king of Rome.</note>, who, since he lacked wives, undertook a most savage war against the Sabines when they refused him their daughters. <note>This refers to the legendary "Rape of the Sabine Women," where the first Romans abducted women from a neighboring tribe to ensure the survival of their new city.</note> For he knew that this empire would last for a very short time if wives were missing.

For since a city consists of houses and a republic of private affairs, and is established through the discipline of governing domestic and family matters—which is like a small republic in itself—how shall he rule a city who has not learned to rule a house? How shall he govern the state who has never known private and family business? 

Hence Socrates testified that he had learned moral philosophy <note>The study of ethics and human conduct.</note> more from wives than he had learned natural philosophy <note>The study of nature and the physical universe, a precursor to modern science.</note> from Anaxagoras and Archelaus. <note>Anaxagoras and Archelaus were Pre-Socratic philosophers; Socrates' wife, Xanthippe, was traditionally portrayed as difficult, which Socrates claimed helped him practice patience and ethics.</note> Indeed, marriage provides a great exercise for moral philosophy. For it has a certain domestic republic attached to it, in the governing of which a man can easily learn and experience prudence, temperance, piety, and all other virtues. Through these—in loving his wife, educating his children, ruling his family, protecting his affairs, governing his home, and propagating his offspring—he may lead the most happy life of all. 

Furthermore, to look upon a beloved wife with one's own eyes, and through children and the successions of the family, and the [extension] of the name...

<summary>Agrippa argues that marriage is the essential foundation of the state and the best training ground for virtue, citing Romulus and Socrates as examples.</summary>
<keywords>marriage, family, Roman history, Sabines, Socrates, moral philosophy, paterfamilias, domestic government, virtue</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s discourse on the necessity of marriage for social and personal stability. ## ON THE SACRAMENT OF MARRIAGE

...to see one’s lineage extended is the highest joy and happiness, and a sweet consolation for one's labors. He who lacks a wife has no home, because he does not establish a permanent household; and if he does possess a house, he dwells in it merely as a stranger in an inn. He who has no wife (even if he be very wealthy) has almost nothing that he can truly call his own, because he has nothing safe from treachery, nor does he have anyone to whom he can leave his estate, nor anyone in whom he can fully trust. 

He who has no wife lacks a family, lacks relatives, and—being without hope of offspring—is always deserted and destitute. His servants rob him, his partners cheat him, his neighbors despise him, and his friends neglect him. His relatives lie in wait for him. <margin>Wisdom of Solomon, Ch. 4 / Code on natural children / Case 32, Question 4, "Let someone say" / Panormitanus on the chapter "Venerabilem" regarding legitimate children / Baldus on the law "When legitimate marriages," Digest, on the status of men / Code, Orphitian Senate-Consult, law "If any illustrious woman"</margin> If he has any children, they are <term>natural sons</term> <note>original: *filios naturales*; in Renaissance law, these were children born out of wedlock who lacked the legal rights and inheritance status of legitimate heirs.</note>, who are a source of shame to him and partners in his own wrongdoing; he can leave them neither his wealth nor his family name. Just as he remains in life without marriage, so when he dies, he perishes without a name. Nor does he deserve to receive any goodwill in this life—which is the greatest comfort to mortals. Instead, as a deserter of human nature, he is worthy of every calamity; he is judged most unhappy by everyone and is deservedly abandoned. 

For this reason, Lycurgus <note>The legendary lawgiver of Sparta, famous for his severe and highly organized social reforms.</note> established a law that those who neglected to marry at the proper time should be barred in the summer from games, spectacles, and every comfort and pleasant sight. In the winter, however, they were to be led naked around the <term>forum</term> <note>The public square and center of judicial and business life in an ancient city.</note> and subjected to insults. They were to be detested by all men and forced to confess that they suffered these things justly, because they had despised religion, disobeyed the laws, and transgressed against nature. Insofar as it was within their power, they prevented the republic, the empire, and religion from standing, for they did not strive to procreate those very people by whom these institutions were to be protected and propagated. Plato, in his *Laws*...

-> E 5 <-

<summary>Agrippa describes the social and legal miseries of the unmarried man, citing ancient Spartan laws that punished celibacy as a crime against the state and nature.</summary>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the legal and social consequences of remaining unmarried. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, likely referencing the Spartan lawgiver Lycurgus, and transitions into a discussion on the severe legal penalties for adultery in both biblical and Roman law.</meta>

->HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

...[Lycurgus] established in his laws: that whoever did not take a wife should be completely separated from all public offices and honors; moreover, he should be pressed with public burdens <note>i.e., taxes or compulsory labor</note> more heavily than other citizens. Augustus Caesar also passed a law: that young men should take wives to increase the Roman population, which many others afterwards confirmed. Furthermore, they granted a year of immunity <note>original: *immanitatem*, likely a typesetter's error for *immunitatem*, referring to an exemption from taxes or military service given to newlyweds</note> and established a reward from the public treasury for him who had newly taken a wife.

Adultery everywhere in ancient times was always heavily punished by the most severe laws, because—by the violation of marriage—an injury is done not only to man but also to God himself. Hence in the Old Law <note>The Mosaic Law of the Hebrew Bible.</note>: whoever had committed adultery by violating marriage was sentenced to death and overwhelmed with stones; but he who had committed theft by stealing another's goods was punished with a fourfold or fivefold penalty. For the thief took human and earthly things illicitly, while the adulterer, besides the offense against his neighbor, is also injurious to God and defiles divine things. Roman laws also strike adulterers with the axe <note>Referring to execution by beheading.</note>; even to the husband...

<summary>Agrippa details historical and legal precedents from Sparta, Rome, and the Bible that penalized bachelors and strictly punished adultery as a crime against both society and God.</summary>
<keywords>Lycurgus, Augustus Caesar, Roman law, Mosaic law, adultery, bachelors, marriage rewards, capital punishment</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s argument regarding the legal and spiritual consequences of violating marriage. ### ON THE SACRAMENT OF MARRIAGE

...they drive them out from holy places, take away the right of second marriages, and deny the Eucharist, judging them unworthy of the Viaticum of God <note>The final Eucharist given to those near death; Agrippa refers here to ancient church canons that were extremely strict regarding remarriage and adultery</note>: those who did not blush to offend God with such a monstrous crime. But by some unknown justice, and through negligence toward God, wife-killers and adulterers now escape almost all punishment, while thieves are killed by hanging from a rope even for a light crime—unless it is because our judgment has been surrendered <margin>Romans 1</margin> to a reprobate mind, so that they might make the commandment of God void <margin>Matthew 15</margin> for the sake of the traditions of men. But now let us conclude.

¶ Therefore, you—whoever you are—if you wish to be a man, if you do not wish to strip off your humanity, if you wish to perform the duty of human kindness <note>original: *humanitatis officio*; in Humanist thought, this refers to the moral and social duties that distinguish humans from animals</note> above others, if you wish to be a legitimate son of God, if you wish to be pious toward your fatherland, your family, and the state; if you wish to possess the earth and merit heaven, it is necessary that you enter into the legitimate bond of marriage, in diss— <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "indissolubile" (indissoluble).</note>

<summary>Agrippa critiques the legal systems of his time for punishing theft more harshly than adultery or uxoricide, and begins a final appeal urging the reader to marry as a requirement for civic and spiritual duty.</summary>
<keywords>adultery, uxoricide, capital punishment, legal reform, Romans 1, Matthew 15, duty of humanity, civic piety, indissoluble marriage</keywords>
