--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedicatory letter from Agrippa to Michael d’Arande. In the previous section, Agrippa compared his own controversial writings on marriage to the ancient works of Theophrastus, which drew fierce criticism from women. Here, he shifts to defending his theological positions against specific clerical critics.</meta>

they would prefer to set my errors before my face (now that they are public), rather than to drag them into slander behind my back among some courtly women or even great ladies. For in that way, I would receive their warnings with the reverence they deserve, and I would either explain myself to them, or clear my name, or correct myself, and I would give my monitors worthy thanks. I know, however, that there are two knots <note>The "knots" (Latin: *nodi*) refer to difficult or controversial points of doctrine that require untangling.</note> in our declamation, of which the Right Reverend Robert Cénalis, Bishop of Vence <note>Robert Cénalis (1483–1560) was a French bishop, historian, and doctor of the Sorbonne who frequently sparred with reformers and humanists.</note>, a man most learned in the Sorbonne style, very modestly warned me. One is that I seem to assert that spouses, separated on account of adultery, are able to contract marriages with others. I responded to him that I did not intend that, but as the speech preceding and following that passage shows, I am dealing there with the unity of marriage, which according to the words of the Lord consists in the inseparable union of the flesh; I say that this unity can be dissolved by no other cause of divorce except fornication alone, where the flesh is already divided among many. Furthermore, if anyone beyond this were to press me with the interpretation of another sense, I told him that I know those who also defended this: Origen, Ambrose, Gratian, and most legal experts, but also Pollentius <note>Pollentius was a contemporary of St. Augustine who argued that the New Testament permitted remarriage after divorce; their debate is preserved in Augustine’s "Adulterous Marriages."</note>, against whom Augustine disputes regarding this matter (though without the crime of heresy <note>original: "citra tamen hæresis crimen"; Agrippa is emphasizing that even though Augustine disagreed with Pollentius, he did not label his view heretical.</note>); <margin>Causa 32, question 7, chapter "Quid" <note>A reference to the *Decretum Gratiani*, the foundational collection of Canon Law.</note></margin> and Pollentius, in turn, having published books, so defends his own opinion that it is difficult to judge the victory. <margin>In the section "Regarding all," paragraph "The wife," etc.</margin> I have, moreover, the examples of certain bishops and the practice of the Church, which acts by the authority of God—an authority that is no less in the Church than it once was in the Synagogue. <margin>certain ones</margin> If, therefore, Moses in the Synagogue [granted] to the Jews against the divine law (which was from the beginning)...

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s defense of his "Declamation on the Sacrament of Marriage" against the criticisms of Robert Cénalis, Bishop of Vence. Agrippa is specifically addressing the "first knot" or theological difficulty: the question of whether an innocent party can remarry after a divorce caused by adultery.</meta>

...and that for this reason, even when spouses have been rejected for other causes, they could be joined to others. I argued that even Papal authority—after an adulteress has been cast out by the authority of the church—grants the innocent party another marriage. For it is not read in the Gospel that a woman who has married another after her husband was rejected commits adultery, but rather that "he who marries a rejected woman commits adultery." <note>original: "neq; enim in euangelio legitur, quod mulier quæ repudiato marito aliũ duxerit, mœchatur: sed qđ qui repudiatam duxerit, mœchatur." Agrippa is making a technical legal distinction based on the phrasing of the New Testament (specifically Matthew 19:9), suggesting the text prohibits marrying the person cast out, but is silent or more lenient regarding the person who does the casting out.</note> Therefore, in this matter, the authority of the Church is free to grant marriages for such a reasonable cause, just as it is to take them away. 

For Hostiensis, Panormitanus, Vincentius <note>These were three of the most influential medieval canon lawyers (jurists of Church law). Henricus de Segusio (Hostiensis), Niccolò de' Tudeschi (Panormitanus), and Vincentius Hispanus were often cited as the ultimate authorities on the extent of Papal power.</note>, and many other interpreters of canon law do not fear to say that the Pope, for some grave cause, can even grant a dispensation against the entire New Testament—provided only that the articles of faith remain intact, though even those are for him to interpret. And yet these doctors, writing in such a way, are meanwhile held to be the most orthodox of men. 

Finally, I said that this entire disputation did not actually belong to my declamation, but was rather a previous exposition of my own thoughts. I referred him to the annotations of Erasmus and his apologies against Lee <note>Agrippa aligns himself with the humanist Desiderius Erasmus, who faced fierce criticism from the English theologian Edward Lee for his "Annotations on the New Testament," which suggested that the early Church permitted remarriage after divorce.</note>. 

The second point that the aforementioned Bishop had noted was this: among those whom I said were exempt from the law of marriage, I mentioned those who were driven... <note>The text breaks off here mid-sentence.</note>

<summary>Agrippa defends the possibility of remarriage after divorce for adultery by citing famous canon lawyers and the biblical interpretations of Erasmus, arguing that the Church has the authority to grant such dispensations.</summary>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page concludes Agrippa’s letter of defense regarding his "Declamation on the Sacrament of Marriage." Having addressed the legal and theological arguments concerning divorce and remarriage in the previous pages, he now describes the resolution of his dispute with the "Reverend Father" (the Bishop of Vence) and takes a final, rhetorical swipe at his remaining critics at court.</meta>

...to commit fornication than to marry. <note>original: "scortari, q̃ nubere." This is the conclusion of a comparison regarding the moral risks of prohibiting remarriage for an innocent party.</note> Having heard these things, that Reverend Father yielded to the opinion; he requested and advised that it be declared thus by letter, so that the cause of offense might be removed. Finally, he praised everything else, and I cannot think who the remaining parts could offend, unless perhaps some advisors on courtly marriages (not to say pimps) <note>original: "lenones." Agrippa is using very harsh language here, accusing court officials who arrange marriages for political or financial gain of being no better than procurers.</note> from whom this declamation seems to snatch away a profitable morsel; or perhaps those lost in lust who, like mad horses (as Jeremiah says), greedily neigh after their neighbor’s wives, <note>Agrippa cites Jeremiah 5:8: "They were as fed horses in the morning: every one neighed after his neighbour's wife." He is comparing his critics to animals driven by instinct rather than divine law.</note> and wish for their vices to be approved by the sheer number of fellow sinners. 

But to your courage, I now further commend the defense of this little declamation, which is dedicated to your most illustrious princess; <note>Agrippa often dedicated his works to high-ranking noblewomen, such as Margaret of Austria or Louise of Savoy, to secure political protection.</note> I challenge you to this contest against such Cerberuses. <note>In Greek mythology, Cerberus was the three-headed hound guarding the gates of the Underworld. Agrippa uses it as a metaphor for his fierce, multi-headed opposition.</note> Like a Hercules, I trust you will fight most successfully and powerfully for the glory of this sacrament, for the defense of me in my absence, and for truth itself.

Farewell most happily, from Lyon, the seventh of May, in the year 1526.

<summary>Agrippa concludes his defense by reporting that his primary critic, the Bishop, has been satisfied, while simultaneously insulting his remaining detractors as immoral "pimps" and "mad horses." He calls upon his correspondent to act as a "Hercules" in defending his work and his reputation while he is away.</summary>

<note>A decorative woodcut initial 'S' marks the beginning of the letter.</note> You write to me, my dearest Capellanus, that there are some in the court—who are nevertheless counted among the number of the learned, and who frequently converse with the great ladies—who little approve of my declamation <note>In the Renaissance, a "declamation" was a formal rhetorical exercise or speech, often used to explore controversial topics from a specific viewpoint.</note> on marriage. Therefore, fearing lest this matter result in controversy rather than a recommendation for me, you have so far delayed offering those little books of mine to our princesses until you could consult me again. 

Hear then my opinion: among the masters of the court there are those who write foul and filthy jests, and comedies about the art of pimping, <note>original: "arte lenonica." Agrippa is likely referring to the bawdy Italian and French comedies popular at the time.</note> and lewd poems drawn from the innermost cabinets of Venus, and pestilential books about wars and loves; there are even those who translate these into various languages. Yet books of this kind are offered to the ladies without offense and without reproach, and are read greedily even by young girls: the *Novellas* of Boccaccio, <note>Agrippa refers to *The Decameron*, which was often criticized by moralists for its sexual content.</note> the jests of Poggio, <note>Poggio Bracciolini's *Facetiae*, a collection of humorous and often obscene anecdotes.</note> the adulteries of Euryalus with Lucretia, <note>A reference to *The Tale of Two Lovers*, a popular erotic novel by Aeneas Silvius Piccolomini, who later became Pope Pius II.</note> the wars and loves of Tristan and Lancelot, <note>Popular chivalric romances which Agrippa views as distractions from moral virtue.</note> and similar things, in which women become accustomed to wickedness through reading. The woman who is most thoroughly instructed in these books, who can wittily recite their jokes, and from their training can chatter eloquently for many hours with suitors—she is the one finally called a "properly courtly woman." At which I wonder that wise...

<meta>This page concludes Agrippa’s letter to Johannes Capellanus, defending his treatise on marriage against critics at the French court by contrasting his work with the secular and erotic literature often favored by the clergy and courtiers.</meta>

those most keen-nosed <note>original: "nasutissimos." In the Renaissance, having a "big nose" was a common metaphor for being overly critical, cynical, or prone to mockery.</note> censors, who often stir up such great tragedies over small matters, not only pretend not to see such things, but even the heads of religion—bishops like that one from Angoulême <note>Agrippa is referring to Octovien de Saint-Gelais (1468–1502), the Bishop of Angoulême, who famously translated Ovid’s *Heroides* (Epistles of the Heroines), a collection of erotic and emotional poems, into French.</note> who translated the amorous *Epistles of the Heroines* by Ovid into the French tongue—are very much occupied in reading them aloud, translating them, and explaining them. The ladies of the court usually have such men as the ministers of their sacred rites; since these men have never learned from any good authors, nor possess even a tiny shred of true learning, how can they possibly approve of these works of mine, which are so far removed from their own way of life? 

Therefore, I now boldly offer those little books to you; and do not think that your Agrippa (a name which many interpret as meaning "ailing feet" <note>Agrippa is making a scholarly pun on his own name. A popular, though likely incorrect, etymology of the time claimed "Agrippa" derived from the Latin *aegre partus*, meaning "born with difficulty" or "feet-first."</note>) is so crippled by gout <note>A painful form of arthritis often associated with the wealthy and scholarly in this period.</note> that he does not dare to descend into the arena with those "wise" courtly censors. I am not so unarmed in the best literature that I lack the strength to both defend these writings of mine and, at the same time, strike down my opposing enemies. Farewell most happily, from Lyon, the first of May, in the year 1526.

F

<note>A decorative initial "C" contains a dragon or a similar mythical creature, a common feature in Renaissance printing used to mark the beginning of a significant text or chapter.</note> SINCE, most illustrious Prince, a divine and immortal soul has been given to us—which, in this temple of our body, as it were, performs its priestly office—it should look back toward its own beginning and origin, and should venerate, know, and love that origin in all integrity and innocence, in all piety and justice. Certainly, to know and to love the Beginning <note>Agrippa uses the term *principium* (beginning/principle) to refer to God as the First Cause or source of all existence.</note> of all things: this shall be the supreme knowledge, this the highest piety, and invincible justice. On the contrary, there is no greater injustice, no greater impiety, than to lack the awareness of God, or to despise the knowledge of God through neglect. Regarding this even the pagan Virgil <margin>Virgil</margin> warns us with this verse: *Be warned, learn justice, and do not slight the gods.* <note>original: "Discite iustitiam moniti et non temnere divos." This famous line comes from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (Book VI), spoken by Phlegyas in the Underworld as a warning to the living.</note> For we—both that we live, and that we move, that which we feel, that which we contemplate, and that which we understand, <note>Agrippa is paraphrasing Acts 17:28 ("For in him we live, and move, and have our being"), a common bridge used by Renaissance scholars to link classical philosophy with Christian scripture.</note>

<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s dedicatory epistle to William IX Palaeologus, Marquess of Montferrat. Having established that the soul must seek its origin, Agrippa now argues that self-knowledge is impossible without the knowledge of God, as man is created in God's image. He references a previous work he dedicated to the Marquess, likely his "De homine" (On Man).</meta>

...[contem]plate, and exist, and understand, if he who is the essence and author of our intellect, in whose image we are made, remains unknown. Therefore, regarding the knowledge of God—as something most necessary and most desirable, and regarding the steps that lead us to him—I have set out to describe these following things, though more briefly than the subject itself demands. For I am well aware of how vast a field this is for writing, but I chose to write in such a way that something might be added rather than removed, lest I be rightly accused of being too wordy or arrogant in matters divine. Indeed, those who think something should be added to these writings of ours, I shall believe myself to be helped by them rather than criticized. 

<margin>A dialogue on man composed by Agrippa.</margin> And since, in days past, we dedicated to your Highness the **Dialogue on Man**, who is the image of God, it is also right that this service concerning the knowledge of God be owed to your Highness (for what of mine is not rightfully owed to you?). For no one can know the image <note>original: "imaginem". Agrippa relies on the "Imago Dei" doctrine from Genesis, suggesting that because humans are reflections of the divine, psychology is a subset of theology.</note> unless he knows that of which it is an image. Receive therefore, most divine Prince, these writings of ours: a monument of our owed obedience to you. May you truly be a glory and ornament both to the writings and the author. 

Farewell, most blessed one. 

At Casale of Saint Evasio. <note>Casale Monferrato, the capital of the Marquess's territory. Saint Evasio is the patron saint of the city's cathedral.</note> In the year 1516.

<meta>This page begins the main text of the treatise. Following the dedication to the Marquess of Montferrat, Agrippa moves from the personal address to a formal theological and cosmological opening, establishing God as the source of all order before describing the fall of the angels.</meta>

# HENRY
CORNELIUS AGRIPPA’S BOOK
*on the threefold way of knowing God.*
CHAPTER ONE.

<note>A large, decorative initial E marks the beginning of the chapter.</note> THE LORD OF ETERNITY, the beginning, middle, and end of all things, the renewal, the fountain of piety, and the origin of justice, the Father, and the Good itself <note>original: "bonum ipse". Agrippa uses "the Good" in a Neoplatonic sense, identifying God not just as someone who does good, but as the very essence of Goodness from which all things flow.</note>—Almighty God—by His most excellent will (if I may put it so) created all good things for the sake of His infinite glory, so that all things might glorify Him and act in a holy and incorruptible manner as pertains to His honor. He established a fixed end and a beautiful order for all things within prescribed limits, which He forbade them to cross. 

This was the will of God regarding created things: <margin>Genesis 1</margin> God created the angels, the heavens, the stars, the elements, the plants, and the living creatures—the four-legged, the crawling things of the water, and those that fly. At the same time, He created man as the prince and final purpose of all these in His own image, and He congratulated him <note>or "rejoiced in him"</note> as a father would a son. 

<margin>Genesis 1</margin> Then, first among all, the angelic nature—not content with its own high standing and ambitiously seeking higher things—set itself in opposition to God and said: <margin>Isaiah 14</margin> “I will ascend into heaven, I will exalt my throne above the stars of God; I will sit upon the mount of the covenant in the sides of the north; I will ascend above the heights...” <note>original: "In cœlum ascendam super astra Dei exaltabo solũ meum...". This is a quote from the Book of Isaiah, traditionally interpreted by theologians like Agrippa as the internal monologue of Lucifer during his fall from grace.</note>

<summary>Agrippa begins his treatise by defining God as the sovereign source of universal order and describing the creation of the world and humanity. He then introduces the first disruption of this order: the prideful rebellion of the angels who sought to exalt themselves above their Creator.</summary>
<keywords>Cosmology, Creation, Order of Nature, Imago Dei, Fall of Angels, Lucifer, Isaiah 14, Genesis</keywords>

HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA
...the heights of the clouds, and I will be like the Most High. The prince of this ambition was Satan, the first transgressor of the divine will. Because of such great pride and injustice, he was driven out of heaven and cast down into this contagious valley <note>Agrippa uses "contagious valley" (*valle contagiosam*) to describe the material world, viewing it as a place infected by the presence of evil and decay following the fall.</note>, where he has lived miserably ever since with his hateful army, hostile to all things. He does not cease to propagate his own injustice within God’s creation, persisting stubbornly in such great pride; nor does he wish to acknowledge his own sin, but instead constantly accuses God Himself regarding his own crime. For this reason, he is called *diabolos* in Greek, which is to say, an "accuser." <note>original: "criminator". The Greek word *diabolos* literally means a slanderer or one who falsely accuses.</note> In him began all injustice, iniquity, malice, death, and deformity; from him proceeds every evil, and nothing but evil. 

Man, however, was created on earth and placed in paradise so that he might obey the divine will <margin>Genesis 2 & 3</margin>—from which obedience he was endowed with both wisdom and perpetual life. Yet, he was targeted by the devil with a hostile temptation. By listening to him, man likewise became a transgressor of the divine will. For this reason, he too was driven out of this garden of delights and into this valley of misery and ignorance. He was made subject to death, dying at every hour and becoming negligent. Then, once the knowledge of God was neglected, the fountain of sins was opened: crimes flowed forth, and the companions of darkness slipped in. 

Man, by ignoring God, was in turn ignored by God; and by abandoning the knowledge of God, he was abandoned by God. As the Apostle Paul says, he was left to his own desires, to shameful passions, and to a reprobate mind <margin>Romans 1</margin>; he was corrupted and became abominable in all his endeavors. You see now how

...because <note>original: "niam", the suffix of *quoniam* (since/because), continuing the sentence from the previous page.</note> the ignorance of God is the fountain of all evils, the origin of all sins, the root of crimes, and the tree of destruction; it is the highest impiety and injustice, through which all vices grow strong and increase. This ignorance perverts the soul itself, corrupts nature, subverts man, and entangles him in the most shameful sins. It plunges the body itself into every deformity and insult against nature, in which the soul, at the same time drenched in these vices, is suffocated. Thus, men are transformed into the nature of wild beasts and the habits of brute animals; indeed, they often suffer worse things than the beasts themselves. 

From this state, they are cast headlong into the shameful allurements of the senses and into the deadly filth of sins. They fall into every outrage and into the perversion of nature, and are subjected to the empire of desires <margin>Pimander, chapter 12 <note>Referring to the *Poimandres*, the first tractate of the *Corpus Hermeticum*, a collection of Greco-Egyptian wisdom texts attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note></margin>, to the fulfillment of which (as Hermes says) they are driven by a certain burning impulse. In the immoderate manner of wild beasts, they both rage and desire; and what is worse, they set no limit to their lust, nor do they find an end to their evil passions. 

All of this Paul clearly teaches in his epistle to the Romans <margin>Romans 1 & 3</margin>. For this reason, unclean spirits—the avengers of crimes—glide into such a wicked soul and lash it with the whips of sins. By violent punishment, they drag, seize, and compel it toward every kind of sin: to murders, to robberies, to lusts, and to all things through which men transgress. They wound the soul with incurable vices, which, once corrupted, swell up as if infected by poisons. You see now that those who are ignorant of God are ignored by God, and those who forsake the knowledge of God are forsaken by God.

## CHAPTER TWO.

**T**herefore, to know and to love the very principle of all things—God the Creator of all—this is the highest piety, this the highest justice, this the highest wisdom, and the highest happiness of man: <margin>Romans 1</margin> God cries out to us from heaven, from his holy mountain: <margin>Acts 7</margin> "Contemplate the creatures, hear the angels, listen to <margin>Matthew 17 & Mark 9</margin> my son, so that you may be pious and just." Behold, these are the three books of the knowledge of God, which God sent into this world for men. The first book is that of creatures <note>This refers to the "Book of Nature," the idea that the physical world is a text written by God that reveals His attributes.</note>, set before the Gentiles who lived under the law of nature. They had learned philosophers who, through the tangible creatures perceived by the senses, knew God, just as Paul says: "The invisible things of God are clearly seen, being understood through those things which have been made." <note>original: "inuisibilia dei per ea quæ facta sunt intellecta conspiciuntur", a quote from Romans 1:20.</note> Second...

<margin>Psalm 147</margin> God becomes the book of the law and of the divine utterances which He gave to the Jews, announcing His word to Jacob, and His justice and judgments to the people of Israel. Whence Paul says: "In these last days, God has spoken to us in His Son, whom He appointed heir of all things, through whom He made all things," and we have teachers, the apostles, taught by the Son of God, Jesus Christ.

<meta>This page transitions from the specific revelations of the Bible to the concept of "Natural Theology"—how God, though fundamentally unknowable in His essence, reveals Himself through the created world. It continues the argument that while the divine substance is hidden, we can perceive God through His "emanations" in nature.</meta>

### ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD

...by a voluntary separation of Himself from things, and a retraction into Himself; nor can we distinguish what His substance might be in the deepest recess of His divinity. For this is impossible, and incomprehensible above all intellect. Therefore the Apostle says that God dwells in **inaccessible light** <margin>1 Timothy 6</margin>, and the prophet says: **He made darkness His hiding place.** <margin>Psalm 17 <note>original: "Posuit tenebras latibulum suum" — Psalm 18:11 in modern numbering</note></margin> And John says: **No one has seen God, nor can see Him.** <margin>John 1 and 4</margin> And Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian Neoplatonist whose works on "Divine Names" explored how we can speak of an unspeakable God</note> says: "Furthermore, those divine things, of what kind they are in their own principle and seat, no sense reaches, no substance, and no science penetrates. Finally, whether we call that hidden thing **supersubstantial** <term>Supersubstantial: a term used to describe God as being "above" or "beyond" the very category of "substance" or "being"</term>, or God, or Life, or Substance, or Light, or the Word, we understand nothing other than the **participations** and virtues emanating from Him into us, by which we are drawn up into God, and which bestow upon us either substance, or life, or wisdom."

Let us therefore know God through certain participations emanating from Him into those things which are created; by understanding these, we may know God through a certain **reflection**, so to speak. Or, as Hermes says <margin>Asclepius 2</margin>: "It happens to us humans that we see those things which are in heaven as if through a mist, as much as is possible through the condition of human sense." <note>Hermes refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage to whom the Hermetic corpus is attributed</note> This intention of seeing through such great goods is very narrow, but it becomes very wide when you see with the **happiness of conscience**. Therefore, in creatures, on account of a certain participation in God, God Himself can be sensed and searched for: for **God shines everywhere through individual created things.** And He freely shows Himself to everyone, as Mercury <note>Another name for Hermes Trismegistus, often used interchangeably in Renaissance occult texts</note> says.

<margin>Asclepius, 10.</margin> ...not where He may be in a place, nor what He may be in quality, nor how great He may be in quantity, but illuminating man by the intelligence of the mind alone. <margin>Pymander, 2.</margin> And elsewhere he says: God fabricated all things for this reason, that you might perceive Him through every individual thing; this is the goodness of God, this is His power, that He shines through all things. Who assigns the measure of motion to each? Who pulls the machine of the world? Who uses this instrument? Who has circumscribed the sea within its boundaries? Who stays and balances the weight of the earth? In the midst of these, there is some author and lord by his art. Whence also Dionysius <margin>Dionysius, On Divine Names</margin> <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian and philosopher</note> says: perhaps we say this truly, that we do not know God from His own nature—for that is indeed unknown and surpasses all reason and sense—but from the most orderly disposition of all creatures produced by Him, as if they were certain images and likenesses of His own divine patterns; by this path and order, we climb according to our strength toward that which transcends all things. Thus far did the philosophers of the nations <note>The "Gentiles" or non-Christian philosophers of antiquity</note> ascend from the mere apprehension of creatures; that is, they embraced by their own intelligence all things which are created on the earth, in the waters, in the elements, in the heavens, and those things which are furthermore above the heavens. At last, they arrived at the first mover, and the true [source] of all...

<summary>Agrippa cites Hermes Trismegistus and Dionysius the Areopagite to argue that while God's nature is unknowable, He can be perceived through the order and beauty of the physical universe. This "natural theology" allowed ancient philosophers to ascend toward a realization of a First Mover.</summary>

**ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD**

...[the] beginning, and by the intellect they saw God Almighty, one, eternal, the creator of all, and the highest good, as well as His everlasting power, divinity, goodness, wisdom, truth, justice, beauty, and the rest, which Paul calls the "invisible things of God." <margin>Romans 1</margin> <note>original: "inuisi-lia dei"; a reference to Romans 1:20, "For the invisible things of him from the creation of the world are clearly seen."</note> For they saw God in heaven and on earth, in fire, in water, in spirit, in animals, in trees, in every body, and in all creatures, just as Lucan sings: <margin>Lucan</margin> "Jupiter is whatever you see, wherever you move." <note>original: "Iuppiter est quodcunq; uides, quocunq; moueris." Lucan was a Roman poet; here "Jupiter" is used as a poetic synonym for the divine presence in nature.</note> Virgil also sings among the cattle: <margin>Virgil</margin> "All things are full of Jove." <note>original: "Iouis omnia plena." A quote from Virgil’s Eclogues, suggesting the divine permeates the entire world.</note> From this Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage</note> says: Man was made a contemplator of divine works; <margin>Pymander, 4</margin> indeed, while he marveled at these, he came to know their author. For he knows God easily and completely (as far as human readiness allows) who easily discerns His individual works with the mind. Therefore, every man can know God, if he wishes. On account of this, the man who is ignorant of God is inexcusable, as is everyone who, drowning his soul in the body, doubts that he can know God. But let this impiety be far from you! Return into yourself, emerge from the body, assume nothing in yourself is impossible; trust and you shall understand, will and you shall achieve. Thus at last you may know God, if you do not lose faith in yourself. For the human mind (as Hermes says) grasps all things, penetrates all things; it mixes with the elements with swiftness; with the sharpness of the mind it descends into the depths of the sea; <margin>Asclepius, 3</margin> all things shine for it; heaven does not seem too high, for as if from close at hand, by its sagacity...

<summary>Agrippa argues that God is visible through His creations in nature, citing Paul, Virgil, Lucan, and Hermes Trismegistus to prove that the human mind can ascend from the physical world to divine knowledge. He posits that ignorance of God is a choice, as the human soul has the innate capacity to penetrate all mysteries if it rises above the body.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse of Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, likely from his "De Occulta Philosophia" or a related philosophical oration, focusing on the divine nature of the human soul and the moral failings of the ancient philosophers.</meta>

->HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

The soul beholds all things. No gloom of the air confuses its focus, no density of the earth hinders its work, and no profound depth of water blunts its gaze. <margin>Pimander, Book 2</margin> <note>Agrippa is quoting the "Pimander," the first book of the Corpus Hermeticum attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note> And elsewhere it says: Command your soul, and swifter than your command, it will fly forth. Order it to cross over into the Ocean, and it will be there before you have finished the order. From where it is now, without departing at all, command it again to fly into heaven; it will need no wings, and nothing shall obstruct its course—not the burning of the sun, nor the vastness of the upper air, nor the turning of the heavens. It penetrates the bodies of all other stars until it transcends even the highest celestial body. Indeed, if you wish to transcend all the spheres of the heavens and investigate what lies beyond, that too is permitted to you.

Observe now how great is the power of the soul, how great its virtue, and how great its speed; for this reason, man is without excuse if he remains ignorant of God. But even more so is he who, knowing God in some way, neither worships nor venerates Him. For this is the most hateful and inexcusable impiety, which Paul, reproaching the Gentiles, describes thus: <margin>Romans 1</margin> "So that they are without excuse, because although they knew God, they did not glorify Him as God, nor gave thanks." <note>original: "ut sint inexcusabiles, quia cum cognovissent deum, non sicut deum glorificaverunt, aut gratias egerunt." A citation of Romans 1:20-21.</note> For the philosophers of the nations, being learned in various disciplines—Arithmetic, Music, Geometry, Astronomy, Physics, Metaphysics, Dialectic, and the rest—knew by their science the one, only, and true God. Yet, being impious and ungrateful for such a great benefit, they turned away from pure and holy knowledge toward a certain false image—

<meta>This page continues the theological discussion on the knowledge of God, transitioning from the theoretical recognition of a creator to the moral obligations of piety and justice.</meta>

# ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD

Following a rash imagination rather than inspecting any true reason, they did not worship Him as the one and only true God, nor did they give thanks to Him who enlightened them with the light of recognizing His divinity. Therefore, all those shall be condemned for impiety, injustice, and ingratitude, according to the words of Paul, who says: *For the wrath of God is revealed from heaven against all ungodliness and unrighteousness of men* <margin>Romans 1 & 2</margin>. For impiety is a sin against God, while injustice is against men; however, the extreme form of impiety is not to know God. From impiety comes intemperance <note>Lack of self-control or moderation</note>, and this is the foundation of injustice. Indeed, we define intemperance as the depravation of the will arising from the slumber of reason, where the sensual affection dominates too much—namely, where reason is put to sleep and all things are done at the command of the senses. Whence this is called the "sterile soul," producing no good fruit in its time; and this is the soul's highest impiety: to be sterile. Concerning this, Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Mercurius," referring to the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the Hermetic corpus</note> says: *Impiety happens to him who departs from life without children* <margin>Pimander 2</margin> <note>In Hermetic thought, "children" often metaphorically represent divine knowledge or good deeds</note>, for which reason he pays the penalty to demons after death. But we know God in vain unless we worship Him rightly and live lawfully with other men. Whence Hermes says: *The contest of religious piety is to recognize God and to do injury to no one* <margin>Pimander 2</margin>. Christ also commanded this, saying: *Love the Lord your God, and your neighbor as yourself* <margin>Matthew 22, Mark 12, Luke 10</margin>. These two precepts are necessary for salvation and are the source of all good: the first belongs to piety, the second to justice. On the contrary, impiety and injustice are the source of all...

<summary>This passage argues that true knowledge of God must result in both religious piety and social justice, warning that a soul governed by senses rather than reason becomes "sterile" and faces divine judgment.</summary>

But now, consequently, let us speak concerning the secondary knowledge of God, which is obtained through the book of the law.

### CHAPTER FOUR

The second book was given to the Jews: the book of the law, set forth openly, and the book of divine utterances <note>original: "eloquiorum," referring here to the oral or mystical traditions</note> handed down only to the wise. For they were the first with whom God spoke in many ways through His angels, and to whom were given the oracles and secrets <note>original: "archana"</note> of God, as the Psalmist says: <margin>Psalm 147</margin> *He hath not dealt so with any nation: and as for his judgments, he hath not manifested them to them.* Moreover, it is established from the opinions of the Hebrew masters, and also the Christian doctors, that Moses himself—the great legislator of the Hebrews—received more than just that law which God gave him on Mount Sinai (which he left written and contained in five books). There was also revealed to this same Moses, by God Himself, the true exposition of the law, with the manifestation of all the mysteries and secrets that are contained under the bark <note>Agrippa uses "cortex" (bark) to describe the literal, outer layer of scripture that hides a deeper spiritual meaning</note> and rough face of the words of the law. Whence it is read that God said to Ezra: <margin>4 Esdras 14</margin> *In revealing, I was revealed upon the bush, and spoke to Moses when my people served in Egypt; and I sent him, and led him out of Egypt, and brought him up to Mount Sinai, and I kept him with me for many days, and...*

<summary>Agrippa concludes his critique of those who misuse wisdom before introducing the 'second way' of knowing God: the revealed Law given to the Jews, which he claims contains both a literal and a secret, mystical meaning.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter Four regarding the revelation given to Moses on Mount Sinai, specifically transitioning from the quote of Ezra to the distinction between the written and oral traditions of the Law.</meta>

-> ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD <-

I told him of many wonders, and showed him the secrets of time and the end, and I commanded him, saying: These words you shall make public, and these you shall hide. It is established, therefore, that Moses on the mountain received a double law: namely, the literal and the spiritual. According to the command of God, he communicated both to the Jewish people, though in different ways. The written law he established openly for the common people, but the other he communicated only to seventy wise men. This was done neither in writing, nor so that they themselves should write it down, but by the living voice <note>original: "viva voce," referring to oral tradition passed directly from teacher to student</note>, so that each of them in a perpetual order should reveal it to their successors by the living voice. Because of this successive tradition of the living voice, it is called the science of divine utterances <note>original: "scientia eloquiorum"</note>, which the Hebrews call Kabbalah <term>Kabbalah: from the Hebrew 'Qabbalah,' meaning 'reception' or 'that which is received'</term>, because it is received as if by a hereditary right from one person to another. This opinion also corresponds with that of Hilary <margin>Hilary, on Psalm 2</margin> in his exposition of the Psalm: *Why do the nations rage?* <note>original: "Quare fremuerunt gentes," the opening of Psalm 2</note> In that work, Hilary says it was established by Moses that there should be seventy elders in every synagogue, to whom Moses...

<summary>Agrippa explains that Moses received both a written law for the public and a secret oral law (Kabbalah) intended only for seventy elders and their successors. He cites the Church Father Hilary of Poitiers to support the historical existence of these seventy elders within the Jewish tradition.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the secret oral tradition of the Law, moving from the testimony of Church Fathers to the specific teachings of Jewish scholars regarding the mystical properties of the Hebrew language and the nature of God.</meta>

# HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

<margin>Rabbi Moses the Egyptian <note>original: "Rab. Mos. Aegyptius," referring to Moses Maimonides (1135–1204), the preeminent medieval Jewish philosopher.</note></margin>
And Rabbi Moses, in the second book of his *Guide for the Perplexed* <note>original: "morae," short for *Moreh Nevukhim*</note>, states that the entire concern of the Law consists in this: to teach true ideas concerning God and the angelic choirs. Once men have been instructed in these things, they may come to understand the world itself in its proper order. Therefore, the primary education of the prophetic <term>Kabbalah: the Jewish mystical tradition involving the interpretation of scripture and the nature of the divine</term> is concerned with those knowable things which can be understood regarding God and the angels. From this foundation, it teaches that the various sacred names of both God and the angels are to be invoked; it also lists various physical acts through which men—having become similar to the gods—conform themselves to divine things through certain stages. By doing so, they transcend toward the lights of the eternal Father. Filled with these lights, they attain a knowledge of God that goes beyond the ordinary way of nature. For the sacred names, when invoked, work more powerfully upon a mind that is properly prepared for them than any physical body acting upon another—just as fire acts upon flax <note>original: "stuppam," the coarse fibers of flax or hemp which catch fire instantly</note>. 

Furthermore, the Hebrew Law possesses this divine quality: that beyond the various names of God and the angels hidden within it, not even a single letter <note>original: "elementum"</note> is present without some prophetic mystery. By the transposition and rotation of these letters according to the rules of the Kabbalists, stupendous oracles are often revealed. 

<margin>Rabbi Moses the Egyptian</margin>
Hence, Rabbi Moses says in his second book that through the divine names and prophetic words—by transposing the order of the letters and using other unusual signs—one may often discern great oracles of divine wisdom. 

<margin>Rabbi Moses, Chapter 29</margin>
The other Moses, of Gerona <note>original: "Moses Gerundinus," referring to Nachmanides (1194–1270), a leading medieval Jewish scholar and Kabbalist</note>, confirms this in his commentary on the beginning of Genesis, as does the entire school of the elder Kabbalists. They maintain that the powers of God are most present and active within these divine names.

<summary>Agrippa cites Maimonides and Nachmanides to explain that the Hebrew Law contains hidden mysteries within its very letters. He argues that by invoking divine names and following Kabbalistic rules, a person can transcend nature and gain direct knowledge of God.</summary>
<keywords>Maimonides, Nachmanides, Kabbalah, divine names, angelic choirs, Guide for the Perplexed, Hebrew Law, mysticism</keywords>

### ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD

...and prophetic characters than in any material body in the world. 
<margin>Dionysius, *On Divine Names* <note>original: "Dio. de di. no." refers to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian Neoplatonist whose works were highly influential in medieval and Renaissance mystical theology.</note></margin>
Therefore, Dionysius says that through the veneration and contemplation of these things, we may more easily ascend to God the Father and be made partakers of His splendor more than by the observation <note>original: "intuitu"</note> of natural things and physical creatures. 
<margin>Book 3 of *Occult Philosophy* <note>original: "Lib. 3. de occulta philoso." refers to Agrippa’s own famous work, *De Occulta Philosophia libri tres*, which served as a primary textbook for Renaissance magic.</note></margin>
Indeed, I have written extensively and profoundly on this subject in my book *On Occult Philosophy*, in the section where the mysteries and ceremonies of hidden operations are discussed. 

But it is enough for us to know here that the Jews' knowledge of God was far more sublime and perfect through the Law than was the knowledge of the Gentiles through [the study of] created things. However, they were only able to possess a certain shadowy knowledge of God; the true and perfect knowledge of God (as the entire school of Kabbalists <term>Kabbalists: practitioners of Kabbalah, the Jewish mystical tradition that explores the hidden meanings of the Torah and the nature of the Divine</term> testifies) was reserved for the coming of the Messiah. He has finally come: our Lord Jesus Christ, in whom all things are perfected and continue to be brought to fulfillment. 

But let us return to Kabbalah, which is the spiritual law lying hidden beneath the words of the literal Law, and which was formerly passed down solely by living voice <note>original: "viva voce," referring to an oral tradition passed from teacher to student</note> from one person to another. This spiritual law—after the restoration of the Jews from the Babylonian captivity by Cyrus, King of the Persians, and after the temple was rebuilt under Zerubbabel <note>The leader of the first group of Jewish exiles who returned from Babylon to Jerusalem.</note>—was first committed to writing by Ezra. Ezra, who was then the leader of the Jewish assembly, called together the wise men in a council so that each might bring forward into the light whatever mysteries of the Law they held in their memory. With scribes <note>original: "notariis"</note> employed, these teachings were recorded into seventy volumes—for there were seventy wise men in that council—about which Ezra speaks in this way:

## [OF] HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

**4 Esdras 14** <note>original: "4. Esdr. 14." refers to the apocryphal Second Book of Esdras (or 4 Esdras in the Vulgate), which describes Ezra rewriting the lost scriptures under divine inspiration.</note> After forty days were finished, the Most High spoke, saying: "The first things you have written, place in public; let the worthy and the unworthy read them. But the last seventy books you shall preserve, so that you may hand them over to the wise among your people, in whose hearts you know the ability to open and keep these secrets. For in these is the vein of understanding, the source of wisdom, and the river of knowledge." 

For the Law itself contains an ineffable Theology concerning the super-substantial deity, an exact Metaphysics concerning intelligible and angelic forms, and a most firm philosophy regarding the corporeal world and natural things. And from this source it has come into practice among the more recent Hebrews that any science which is more hidden and concealed, or which deals with the secret operations of wondrous effects, is called **Kabbalah** <term>Kabbalah: from the Hebrew 'Qabbalah,' meaning 'received tradition.' It refers to the mystical and esoteric interpretation of the Torah.</term>. 

From this it happened that even those who boasted of performing stupendous deeds through some secret treaty, pact, and agreement entered into with demons—in order to hide the wickedness of their own cursed craft and superstition—called themselves by the more honorable name of **Kabbalists**. Thus, at last, the holy name of Kabbalah fell under suspicion, just as the sacred name of **Magic** has; both are suspected, and both are profaned, according to the old proverb which says: *holy things are profaned when they are usurped by the profane.* 

Therefore, the Jews possessed a written law, made public to the common people; but they also possessed the utterances of God—namely, the hidden mysteries of the highest divinity—lying concealed beneath the shell <note>original: "sub cortice," literally 'under the bark' or 'under the rind.' This is a common medieval and Renaissance metaphor for the outer, literal meaning of a text that hides its inner, spiritual truth.</note> of the words of the written law. These were handed down only to the wise, for it was not permitted to reveal them to the common people. For mysteries filled with such great divinity...

<summary>Agrippa uses the Book of Esdras to argue that God provided two versions of the Law: a public one for the masses and a secret, "Kabbalistic" one for the wise. He also warns that the terms "Kabbalah" and "Magic" have been wrongly claimed by practitioners of dark arts, leading to their unfair reputation.</summary>

<keywords>Kabbalah, 4 Esdras, Ezra, secret tradition, written law, Magic, profanation, Theology, Metaphysics, sub cortice</keywords>

# ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD

To communicate the most secret things to the foolish common people would be nothing other than giving what is holy to the dogs. Even Christ himself forbade this in his Gospel; he who was promised through the law and <margin>Matthew 7</margin> desired in the law, finally came at the opportune time as the fulfiller and perfecter of the law, just as he himself said: "For I have not come to abolish the law, but to fulfill it." <margin>Matthew 5</margin> For the entire law was divided into three parts: they were either shadowy figures of the light to come, or prophetic words of the truth to come, or precepts for living toward the perfection to come. But if you grasp only the literal sense of the law, without the spirit of the future light, truth, and perfection, nothing will be more ridiculous than the law, and it will be most similar to an old wives' tale or a Milesian story <note>original: "milesijq; sermonis"; a reference to Milesian tales, which were ancient Greek fables known for being low-brow or scurrilous</note>. Furthermore, Christ came as the sun of justice, the true light, the clearest truth, and the true perfection of life for all men who believe in his name. He himself fulfilled the law, so that from now on there is no need for the law; nor do we henceforth know God in the darkness of created things, nor in the shadow of the Jewish law, but in the light of the faith of Jesus Christ. This is true knowledge, <margin>Ephesians 1</margin> the wisdom of the Father, and the understanding of man, in whom—as Paul says—all things are summed up, both those in heaven and those on earth. Therefore, let us now speak consequently about the final and perfect knowledge of God, which is through the Gospel of Jesus Christ our Lord.

Therefore, all those who pursue divine matters with various contentious disputes run in vain, and, armed with the evasions of sophisms and the tricks of <term>dialectic</term> <note>The art of logical argumentation, which Agrippa argues is insufficient for spiritual truth</note>, they think they can break down the doors of the sacred scriptures. Disputing, they always seek great things, yet they find nothing, because they lose themselves; and as Paul says, they are always learning, and never arriving at the knowledge of the truth. <margin>2 Timothy 3 <note>Original: 2 Thim. 2</note></margin> Hence the same Paul commands the Corinthians that they obey and stand firm in faith, <margin>1 Corinthians 1</margin> and take care lest they be deceived through dialectic and philosophy, which are empty, deceitful, and the inventions of men. These follow the elements of this corruptible world, of which all knowledge is derived from the senses; from these, reason takes all its material for knowledge—by wandering, composing, dividing, and collecting universal propositions from experiences. But God and Jesus Christ are above the world, and the creator of the world is above all natures, qualities, shapes, numbers, orders, and actions; and, as <term>Dionysius</term> <note>Dionysius the Areopagite</note> says in his *Mystical Theology*, <margin>Dionysius, *Mystical Theology*</margin> God is above all speech, <term>position</term> <note>Original: *positionem*; the theological method of making positive affirmations about God (kataphasis)</note>, and <term>ablation</term> <note>Original: *ablationem*; the theological method of "taking away" or negating attributes to describe God (apophasis)</note>; He is above every affirmation and negation. He is even above those supramundane angels and the "wings of the winds," He who "ascends above the cherubim" and "made the darkness His hiding place"; <margin>Psalm 18 <note>Original: Psalm 17</note></margin> who is the King of kings and Lord of lords, both of those things which are, and of those which are not. <margin>Romans 4</margin>

**ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD**

...instrument <note>original: *strumẽto*, likely completing *instrumento* from the previous page.</note>, nor by any art, except that which—when it is turned entirely toward the mind (its own head and supreme portion) by the total intention of reason ascending into it—is converted into it; just as sometimes, because of the love of inferior and sensible things, it is entirely turned toward the <term>phantasy</term> <note>original: *phantasia*. In Renaissance psychology, this is the faculty that processes sensory data into mental images, often seen as a distraction from higher intellectual thought.</note>. We know indeed that the human mind exists as an image of the heavenly countenance and as a light inscribed within us, which, migrating from the fountain of truth, grasps and embraces truth alone. But the whirlwinds of phantasms <note>original: *fantasmatum*. These are illusory or sensory images that cloud the mind's higher perception.</note> overshadow, distract, dissipate, and disperse it—not indeed in itself, but within us—to such an extent that the soul can scarcely enter the narrowest gate of truth. Therefore, while our soul is enclosed in corruptible flesh and submerged in too much commerce with it, unless it overcomes the way of the flesh, regains its original nature, and emerges as a pure mind—almost like an angel—it labors in vain concerning divine matters. 

But what soul is this, if not the one that, through indubitable hope and desire for the heavenly divinity, commands the phantasy to be silent? It is the soul which, having firmly embraced true faith, no longer trusts in the accustomed discourses of natural reason; and which, adhering to God with ardent love, lives by the mind alone. Such a soul has emerged as an angel and receives God with its whole heart. 

Whence that saying of Jeremiah: "In this let him glory, who glories in knowing me." <margin>Jeremiah 9 <note>original: *Hiere. 9.*</note></margin> Hence Zoroaster <note>A legendary ancient Persian prophet and sage, often cited by Renaissance writers as a primary source of ancient, mystical wisdom (*Prisca Theologia*).</note>, that most ancient philosopher, says: "The soul of man in a certain way draws God into itself when, retaining nothing mortal, it is entirely intoxicated with divine draughts." And then also such a soul often exults in the harmony of the body, when it leaps forth after contemplation; returning to bodily duties, it produces in these the fruits of faith and the food of justice. Therefore, John says that a soul of this kind is born <margin>1 John 2 etc.</margin>

->G 4<-

# HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

It daily urges the soul immersed in the flesh so that, stripped of all carnality, it may lay aside all <term>animal powers</term> <note>original: *potentias... animales*. In Renaissance philosophy, 'animal' refers to the *anima* or the sensory soul responsible for movement and perception, rather than 'beast-like' qualities.</note> and rational powers and operations. Agrippa uses it here to describe the mystical birth of divine presence within the purified soul.</note>. 

Whenever such a soul sets aside its outward actions, returns into itself, and turns to contemplate the eternal God, it is then no longer sluggish from any earthly impulse. Instead, supported by the Father of Lights <note>A reference to James 1:17: "Every good gift and every perfect gift is from above, and cometh down from the Father of lights."</note>, it ascends to the sublime peak of divine knowledge. There, it is continually filled with prophetic oracles and is often chosen as God’s instrument for performing miracles. Even its prayers regarding the public changes of the world itself are not in vain, <margin>James 5</margin> just as James admonishes us, saying: Elijah was a man similar to us, and subject to suffering; yet praying, he prayed that it might not rain upon the earth, and it did not rain for three years and six months. And again he prayed, and the heaven gave rain, and the earth gave its fruit <note>original: *Helias homo erat similis nobis...* A translation of the Latin Vulgate for James 5:17-18.</note>. 

Oh, what a great miracle! A man—and especially a Christian man—who, while situated in the world, may know those things which are above the world and the author of the world itself. In that state, he perceives and understands every lower thing: not only those which are and which have been, but even those which are not yet, and which are to come. Great...

### ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD
<note>original: *DE COGNOS. DEO*, an abbreviation for *De Cognitione Dei*.</note>

Certainly, the Christian man is a miracle, who, while established in the world, rules over the world; he performs operations similar to those of the creator of the world himself. These works are commonly called miracles, the root and foundation of all of which is faith in Jesus Christ. Through this alone, man is made one and the same with God, and enjoys the same power, just as Christ promised, saying: "Truly I say to you, he who believes in me, the works that I do, he will do also; and greater works than these he will do, because I go to the Father; and whatever he asks the Father in my name, I will do, and whatever he asks me, I will do, so that the Father may be glorified in the Son." <margin>John 14</margin> And elsewhere he says: "If you have faith like a grain of mustard seed, and you say to this mountain, 'Throw yourself beyond the sea,' it will be done." <margin>Matthew 17</margin> <margin>Luke 17</margin> "Therefore I say to you, whatever you ask for in prayer, believe that you will receive it, and it will be yours." <note>This is a paraphrase of Mark 11:24.</note> Hence it is that truly Christian men, and those devoted to God, speak in tongues, predict future events, command the elements, drive away mists, summon rains, command the winds, turn aside storms, heal the sick, give light to the blind, cure the lame, cleanse lepers, cast out demons, and often raise the dead, and things of this kind. So the Prophets, so the Apostles, so many holy bishops, priests, <term>doctors</term> <note>original: *doctores*. In this context, it refers to the "Doctors of the Church," eminent theologians whose teachings carry great authority.</note>, and other men of God have shone forth, and continue to shine, with the greatest powers. Therefore, that power is most fitting for those who are more perfect in faith, to whom Paul says he speaks wisdom only <margin>1 Corinthians 2</margin> and preaches the Gospel separately <note>original: *segregatim*. This suggests a distinction between the public preaching of the Gospel and a more esoteric or "separate" understanding shared among the spiritually mature.</note>. For the Gospel also has, just like the <term>Mosaic law</term> <note>The Law of Moses, or the Torah.</note>, something in its <term>bark</term> <note>original: *corte* [cortice]. Renaissance writers often used the "bark" (the outer shell or literal meaning) and the "pith" or "marrow" (the inner, spiritual meaning) as a metaphor for interpreting scripture.</note>...

HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA
<note>original: *HENRI. COR. AGRIP.*</note>

...these things are set forth for the weaker, but another thing is found in the **marrow** <note>original: *medulla*. Agrippa frequently contrasts the "rind" (outward ceremony) with the "marrow" (inner spiritual truth).</note> which is separately revealed to the perfect, just as Paul speaks regarding those people in the Letter to the Hebrews, calling: "These things the milk for infants, and the basic elements of the beginning of the words of God," but those other things he calls "solid food, the word of righteousness, and the perfect doctrine of Christ," <margin>Hebrews 5 & 6</margin> saying: "If you wish to be carried toward the perfect doctrine of Christ, the word of initiation must be left behind," in which, of course, is treated the principles and foundations of divine wisdom. 

These consist of repentance from dead works, baptism, the sacraments, the laying on of hands, the authority to absolve sins, the resurrection of the dead, and eternal judgment. All such things are held in the **rind** <note>original: *cortice*. This refers to the literal or surface-level meaning of scripture.</note> of the Gospel; they are handled in the schools by scholastic theologians, and are brought forth as problems to be disputed and discussed. 

However, those matters pertaining to a better wisdom and perfect doctrine—namely, what constitutes the heavenly gift and the **hidden manna** <note>original: *manna absconditum*. A reference to Revelation 2:17, signifying secret spiritual nourishment.</note> which no one knows except he who receives it—are of a different nature. This is the "good word of God," which is better than that which is commonly handed down to the public in parables. This mystery of the kingdom of God is granted to be known only to the more secret disciples. It concerns what the powers of the age to come are, what the origin and end of the soul might be, and the ministries of angelic spirits. It explores the condition and quality of that immense glory and happiness which we expect, "which eye has not seen, nor ear heard, nor has it entered into the heart of man." <margin>1 Corinthians 2</margin> 

All these things are contained in the **marrow** and the **kernel** of the Gospel, and they are not known except to the more perfect, to whom is given the knowledge of powers and...

<summary>Agrippa distinguishes between the "milk" of basic Christian dogma (the rind) and the "solid food" of mystical revelation (the marrow) intended for advanced practitioners.</summary>
<keywords>esoteric Christianity, Paul the Apostle, scholasticism, sacraments, hidden manna, angelic ministries, soul's origin, sacred mysteries</keywords>

### ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD
<note>original: *DE COGNOS. DEO*</note>

...of the powers of miracles, and of prophecy, and the rest, which men cannot investigate by their own strengths, unless they have been subjected to the power of the **Holy Spirit**. Those who are chosen and appointed for this reason to exercise leadership in the church, so that they themselves—enlightened in faith and knowing the will of God, and instructed through the Gospel—are, according to the words of Paul, **leaders of the blind**, **a light for those who are in darkness**, <margin>Romans 2</margin> **instructors of the foolish**, and **teachers of infants**, possessing the form of knowledge and truth in the Gospel. Such men in the church are the pontiffs, bishops, prelates, doctors, and those to whom the care of souls and the building up of the church has been committed. To these people, Paul writes and says: <margin>1 Corinthians 14</margin> "He who speaks in tongues speaks not to men, but to God; for no one understands <note>original: *audit*. Literally "hears," but in this context of Paul's letter, it implies hearing with understanding.</note>, but in the spirit he speaks mysteries. For he who prophesies speaks to men for their building up, and exhortation, and comfort. He who speaks in a tongue builds up himself, but he who prophesies builds up the church of God." And he follows in the same place: "Tongues are for a sign, not to believers, but to unbelievers; but prophecy is not for unbelievers, but for believers. If, therefore, the whole church comes together into one place and all speak with tongues, and an unlearned <note>original: *idiota*. In the Renaissance context, this refers to a layperson or someone without formal theological training rather than a "fool."</note> or unbelieving person enters, will he not say, 'Are you not insane?' But if all prophesy, and an unbeliever or unlearned person enters, he is convicted by all, he is judged by all; for the secrets of his heart are made manifest, and so falling on his face he will worship God, declaring that truly God is among you." And finally he concludes: "If anyone seems to be a prophet..."

<summary>Agrippa describes the role of church leaders as divinely illuminated guides who must move beyond basic ritual to the spiritual power of prophecy. Citing the Apostle Paul, he distinguishes between the gift of tongues and the gift of prophecy, emphasizing that clear, inspired teaching is what truly converts the unlearned and builds the church.</summary>

be, or is spiritual, let him acknowledge that the things I write to you are the commandments of the Lord; but if anyone is ignorant, he shall be ignored. <note>A citation of 1 Corinthians 14:37-38, where Paul asserts his apostolic authority.</note> Behold, the Apostle proposes these things not as mere advice, but as a divine mandate and precept. Therefore, if the pontiffs, prelates, and our doctors of divine wisdom do not possess the prophetic spirit, and if they do not prove their admirable profession before the church by the effect of some divine power, then certainly their spirit is possessed by a worldly mind <note>original: "mentis liuinæ" likely a typo for "mundanae" or "liminae"</note>, their faith in Christ is weak, and it languishes because the flesh dominates too much over the spirit. For this reason, all those with such a sterile soul shall be judged and condemned by God for their impiety and injustice.

Now you have the description of what sort of person he ought to be who desires to attain the knowledge of God, and who truly deserves to be called a "theologian" <term>Theologian: literally 'one who speaks of God'</term>—one who desires to speak with God and to meditate on His law day and night. They believe that nothing can be brought up in conversation about which they cannot most eloquently argue on either side, offering a "probable" opinion. They are a crafty people, overflowing with borrowed learning <note>original: "abundantes alienis literis," literally "abounding in others' letters," suggesting they rely on quoting authorities rather than personal experience.</note>, and at the same time insolent, relying on a certain artificial <term>Dialectic: the art of logical argumentation or formal reasoning.</term> dialectic. Though they know nothing at all, they desire to be seen as learned. Therefore they dispute openly in the halls of learning, bolstered by sophisms <note>Sophisms are clever but fallacious arguments used to deceive.</note>, speaking through evasive detours and judging themselves to be wise. But they are miserably deceived by these follies and by the cunning of a versatile mind; what they think is a help to them is actually a hindrance. They vanish in their own thoughts and are handed over by God to a reprobate sense; <note>A "reprobate sense" or "depraved mind" refers to Romans 1:28, where God abandons those who refuse to acknowledge Him to their own flawed judgment.</note> so that where they believe they see most clearly and are most able to find the truth, in that very place they are most obscured—

Writing on this, <margin>Jerome</margin> Jerome says: Logic and Aristotle—who is the prince of logicians—are accustomed to spread out the nets of their arguments, and to trap the wandering freedom of theology within the thorny thickets of syllogisms. <note>original: "Dialectica & Aristoteles... in syllogismorum spineta concludere." Saint Jerome (c. 347–420) was a Church Father who often warned that pagan philosophy could stifle the living word of the Bible.</note> These people, therefore, who spend entire days and nights in such things, so that they might either question or...

# **ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD**

Whether they respond or offer a premise, or whether they accept, assume, confirm, and conclude, they call those people "contentious" who think one should litigate as one pleases, not by reason, but by sheer temper. If, therefore, those men do this—whose very craft is contention—what ought a Christian to do, if not flee contentions altogether? Thus he spoke. And so, if we wish to truly know God, we must set aside all turbulent reasoning, all sophisticated argumentation, and all dialectical <note>The art of investigating or discussing the truth of opinions through logic.</note> inquiry. For reason and inquiry suggest a certain amount of despair and distrust. Faith, however, ought to be fixed and tranquil; for this reason, Ambrose says in his book *On the Trinity*: <margin>Ambrose in the book *On the Trinity*</margin> "Remove arguments where faith is sought; even in their own schools, let logic now be silent; we believe fishermen, not logicians." <margin>The same to Gratian on faith</margin> The same author, writing to Gratian regarding faith, says: <margin>Urban, and it is held in Distinction 37</margin> And Pope Urban, writing to Charles, says: "It did not please God to save His people through logic. For the Kingdom of God is in the simplicity of faith, not in the contention of speech." For there is no greater plague to the soul than reasoning, than altercation, than disputation concerning divine matters, which overturns reason, perverts the intellect, and casts down faith. <margin>To the Colossians 2; 1 Corinthians 1 and 2; 2 Timothy 2; To Titus 3</margin> Therefore, Paul commands that it be avoided above all, and James calls it "earthly, animalistic, and demonic wisdom." <margin>James 3</margin> From this source come errors, doubts, lies, and heresies; from here the first sin arose in the human race. Moreover, the inventor of this so pestilential faculty was the devil—that first cunning and ruinous sophist—who proposed little questions, invented disputations, and as if...

<meta>This page continues Agrippa's argument that the fall of humanity was not caused by a simple act of eating, but by the abandonment of faith in favor of logical inquiry and "sophistical" debate with the devil.</meta>

->## **OF HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**<-

...instituted a kind of school. Not content that he had destroyed himself <note>The "he" refers to the devil, following the previous page's discussion on the origins of sin.</note>, he discovered a craft by which he might also destroy others, and increase and propagate his own evil. Therefore, not permitting man to stand in simple faith, he wished to propose a question concerning the commands of God, judging this to be a most convenient engine for the overturning of man. Like a sophist <note>A "sophist" was a teacher of rhetoric, but in this theological context, it refers to someone who uses clever but fallacious and deceptive reasoning.</note>, in accordance with <margin>Genesis 3</margin> he first approaches Eve, and provokes her into a contest of inquiry and reason, asking: "Why did God command you that you should not eat from every tree of paradise?" <note>original Latin: "Cur præcepit uobis deus, ut non comedatis, ex omni ligno paradiſi."</note> If Eve had not disputed with him, she would not have been deceived. But because she descended into an altercation with the devil, she was deceived by a single false and sophistical reason; and not only did she then fall from faith, but she simultaneously lost her reason. From this, she first began to interpret the words of God falsely. Whereby she also committed a lie, as soon as she presumed to doubt and distrust the utterances of God. For she responded thus concerning the fruits of the trees which are in paradise: "We may eat of the fruit; but of the fruit of the tree which is in the middle of paradise, God commanded us that we should not eat and that we should not touch it, lest perhaps we die." <note>original Latin: "ne forte moriamur."</note> In this, she falsely interpreted the command of God by saying "to us" in the plural, which God had commanded to Adam alone in the singular before Eve was created; moreover, she added "neither should we touch it," in which she lied on both counts. Then she even expressed doubt, where she added "lest perhaps." You see how that crafty and diabolical discussion, proposed through questions, deceived reason; and reason, in turn, casts down faith. This is the fruit, this the utility, this the end of sophistical disputations, which...

<summary>Agrippa uses the Fall of Eve to illustrate how logical inquiry and "sophistry" lead to the destruction of faith and the corruption of reason. He argues that the devil's first tactic was to draw humanity into a debate rather than allowing them to remain in simple obedience.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s critique of the corruption of faith. Having previously described how the devil used sophistry to deceive Eve, he now applies this critique to the "modern" theologians of his time who have abandoned the simple Gospel for complex, scholastic debate.</meta>

->### OF THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD ###<-

...in this time, [these things] are practiced by several recent theosophists <note>literally "seekers of divine wisdom," though here used disparagingly for those who prioritize intellectualism over faith</note> and lovers of pomp, to every kind of vanity. These men, with a poorly translated Aristotle and certain commentaries—and moreover Peter Lombard, whom they call the Master of Sentences <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1100–1160) was the author of the *Four Books of Sentences*, which became the standard textbook of medieval scholastic theology.</note>—worship these works as the archetype of all theology, while neglecting the Gospel of Christ and the apostolic dogmas, and having seen I know not what else of that kind. Then, relying on their sophistical insolence, they think they are able to attempt, attack, dissolve, and interpret everything. Then, rushing in with their ineptitudes, defilements, and babblings, and with quarrelsome disputes—for which craft they now have their tongues armed—they have rendered all things that are simple, sincere, and pure in faith and religion into things that are complex, dark, and filthy. 

They have confused, disturbed, and polluted all theology with their absurd alterations and futile verbosity; they have invented a theology that is neither divine nor even human, but I know not what of their own—I do not say a "theology," but a squalid, hateful, caviling, and diabolical vanity: a rhapsody of human opinions and philosophical trifles. 

But as for that true and ancient theology, which flowed from the first saints and true Christians: it came first from Christ and from the apostles, whom Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian and philosopher; Agrippa refers to the "Corpus Areopagiticum," which was highly influential in the Renaissance.</note> followed among the Greeks, whose most divine writings exist, though not in their entirety. Likewise, the divine Origen, a most consummate theologian, of whose almost innumerable writings very few remain because of the corruption of rivals. Likewise Basil, surnamed the Great, Athanasius, Alexan... <note>Agrippa is listing the "Church Fathers," preferred for their proximity to the original Christian spirit compared to the later Scholastics.</note>

H

->### HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA ###<-

[Athana]sius of Alexandria <note>original: *Alexandrinus episcopus*</note>, the bishop who argued with such constancy against the Arians <note>The Arians were a group in the early Church who followed the teachings of Arius, who argued that Jesus Christ was not co-eternal with God the Father; this conflict dominated 4th-century Christian theology.</note>. Cyril, the bishop of that same cathedral, who presided over the Council of Ephesus <note>Cyril of Alexandria (c. 376–444 AD) led this council in 431 AD to address the teachings of Nestorius regarding the nature of Christ.</note>, and whose excellent commentaries on the Gospel of John still exist. Didymus, nicknamed "the Blind" <note>Didymus the Blind (c. 313–398 AD) was a renowned Alexandrian theologian who, despite losing his sight at age four, became a master of sacred scripture.</note>, who wrote on the procession of the Holy Spirit—a work which Jerome <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the primary translator of the Latin Vulgate Bible.</note> translated into the Latin tongue. Eusebius of Caesarea, who wrote the *Preparation for the Gospel*. Likewise Gregory Nazianzen <note>Gregory of Nazianzus, a 4th-century Archbishop known as "The Theologian" for his influential defense of the Trinity.</note>, and many others. 

Furthermore, from among the Latins: Cyprian, Lactantius, Tertullian, Ambrose, Rufinus, Jerome, Augustine, Leo, Gregory, Bede, Anselm, Bernard, Cassian, and those others whom that earlier age produced. These holy doctors—this theology, I say—[the moderns] utterly disregard, reject, and mock; yet without these men, they can know nothing correctly, not even within their own fields. 

The inventor of this so-perilous mastery was that ancient serpent-sophist <note>Agrippa refers here to the Devil in the Garden of Eden, framing academic sophistry as a continuation of the first deception of humanity.</note> who deceived Eve. Imitating him, certain "theosophists" <note>literally "seekers of divine wisdom," though used here as a biting sarcasm for academic theologians who prioritize logic over faith.</note> of recent times have stood out as the leaders, authors, and spreaders of this great crime in our century, whom countless other men of the same kind follow miserably every day. 

From this has arisen that horrid and tangled forest, and that dark grove of disputes, in which they labor continually with wretched toil and damnable zeal, yet with little fruit. They do not invite Christ through faith, hope, or charity; nor do they ask, seek, or knock <note>A reference to Matthew 7:7: "Ask, and it will be given to you; seek, and you will find; knock, and it will be opened to you."</note> through prayers, fasts, or vigils, so that the secret <note>original: *ar-* [likely *arcanum*]</note> of divine knowledge might be opened to them...
