->### ON KNOWING GOD ###<-

...Hilary <note>original: *Hilarium*; Hilary of Poitiers (c. 310–367 AD) was a prominent Latin theologian who defended the divinity of Christ.</note>, but like Titans warring against God <margin>Daedalian</margin> with their machines of sophistry, these enemies of sacred letters believe they can break them apart. Hence, whatever was written by a philosopher or some theologian that falls into their hands, they do not reduce to just principles. Instead, they lead it further astray, away from the primary sources <note>original: *primos fontes*; a reference to the humanist ideal of returning to original Greek and Hebrew texts rather than later commentaries.</note> from which it flowed. They tear it apart with their follies and foolish distinctions; they scatter and grind it as if in a mortar into dust, so that—with all its strength lost due to excessive thinning—when it is placed before the light and air, it vanishes with the slightest breeze.

Because of this, they use a common proverb among themselves, saying: “With a single breath <note>original: *unico flatu*; "flatus" can mean a breath, a puff of wind, or even a fart, emphasizing the emptiness of their speech.</note>, I shall dissolve your argument.” Rightly so! This is a truly and wisely spoken saying. For among these debaters, there is nothing but breath, meeting breath, and dispelling it. 

Consequently, when they depart even a little from their schools <note>original: *gymnasiis*; refers to the university halls where formal scholastic disputations were held.</note>, they sit mute and brooding, like dullards or lifeless trunks of wood. Like a withered fig tree <note>A biblical allusion to the fig tree cursed by Jesus in Matthew 21:19 for appearing healthy but bearing no fruit.</note>, they have nothing to say and no fruit...

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa's critique of the "sophists" and "pseudo-theologians" mentioned on the previous page, contrasting their derivative, empty scholarship with the authentic, grace-filled wisdom of the Church Fathers and early theologians.</meta>

->## [OF] HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA ##<-

...not so much to teach others, but rather to hunt for the glory of their own reputation and to appear widely read. They fail to consider that if everyone were given back what is rightfully theirs <note>original: *unicuique quod suum est tribuerint*; a legal and moral principle of "giving each his due," suggesting these writers are essentially plagiarists.</note> from these men's words and writings, nothing of their own would remain. 

Wandering in this error and in the poverty of their own natural wit—like some unrefined sect—because they know nothing themselves and can produce nothing from within, they exhaust all their efforts in excerpting and compiling. Content with this, though they know nothing at all beyond utilizing the labors and examples of others, they rashly claim the name of wisdom for themselves. It is amazing how much they please themselves with this strategy, and how exceptionally learned they consider themselves to be. 

Those ancient theologians did not act in this way. They were men of profound wisdom, venerable authority, and holy lives—men like those we mentioned earlier. In their writings, citations of Scripture are found only where something truly needs recording, and even then, they are simple and rare. They drew from the Old Testament, the Gospels, the Apostles, and the most distant antiquity, yet they boasted of nothing. They were men who trusted solely in divine grace; they were the finest teachers, aware of their own wisdom yet fearing no man’s judgment. Truthful and impartial <note>original: *non respicientes in faciem hominum*; literally "not looking at the faces of men," an idiom meaning they were not swayed by the status or opinions of others.</note>, they bestowed gifts upon us from their own treasures. In this, they imitated Christ, who, like a good head of a household, brought forth both new and old things from his storehouse <margin>Matthew 13</margin> <note>Agrippa refers to Matthew 13:52: "Every scribe... is like a master of a house, who brings out of his treasure what is new and what is old."</note>. Through every word and deed, they brought to fruition in humanity the harvest of true religion and faith, leading toward eternal salvation. 

But let us return to where we digressed. Therefore, regarding how much [benefit there is] in the knowledge of God...

<summary>Agrippa condemns contemporary scholars for plagiarizing and compiling the work of others to look learned, contrasting them with the ancient Church Fathers who spoke from their own spiritual abundance and divine grace.</summary>
<keywords>plagiarism, ancient theologians, Church Fathers, Matthew 13, divine grace, scholasticism vs. patristics, wisdom, vanity</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa's critique of contemporary "sophists" and "pseudo-theologians" who rely on derivative knowledge. He contrasts their chaotic, contradictory arguments with the singular, consistent nature of truth, and laments the persecution of those seeking the "pristine theology" of the ancients.</meta>

->### **ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD** ###<-

Those who presume they can come to know God through a mere parade of intellectual excitement <note>original: *exhilirationis discursu*; refers to the frantic, prideful exercise of logic rather than spiritual contemplation</note> commit a grave error, as the almost countless contradictions in their own traditions demonstrate. For no question—no matter how simple—is put to them that they do not entangle in flawed reasoning and Daedalian <note>original: *dedaliaq; labyrinthis*; referring to the mythical architect Daedalus, creator of the Labyrinth; signifies something overly complex or confusing</note> mazes. Like dogs, they turn on one another, condemning each other with rabid barking and vicious bites, as their many books and volumes clearly show. 

If, according to Aristotle’s view, the nature of truth is to be consistent with itself at every point, and if the harmony of opinions is a necessary footprint of the truth, then it follows by necessity that whatever is everywhere discordant cannot be true. Therefore, among these debaters and modern "theosophists," <note>original: *theosophistas*; here used by Agrippa to mock those who claim a "divine wisdom" that is actually just empty academic posturing</note> there can be no truth, nor even a trace of it. It is inevitable that this disordered and monstrous heap of human opinions regarding divine matters will eventually collapse under its own weight. 

But alas, wretched ignorance still spreads wide across the world; no one with a pious mind seeks the true knowledge of God. Almost all of us have professed ignorance through this "new theology"—new teachers with their new doctrines. Nothing is ancient, nothing is holy, nothing is truly religious. And what is worse: if there are any who dedicate themselves to this <term>pristine theology<gloss>The *prisca theologia*, a Renaissance concept suggesting a single, true theology exists which was given by God to ancient prophets and philosophers</gloss></term> and religion, they are called insane, ignorant, irreligious, and sometimes even heretics. And as Hermes says <margin>Pimander, 9. Asclepius, 9.</margin>, they are hated, they face capital danger, they are covered in insults, and they are often deprived of their lives. Nevertheless, to return to the original springs and the pure water...

<column-break/>

->**H 3**<-

<summary>Agrippa argues that the contradictory nature of modern theological debate proves its falsehood, contrasting it with the self-consistent nature of truth. He laments that those who seek the "pristine theology" of the ancients are often persecuted as heretics.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Hermes Trismegistus, prisca theologia, religious persecution, consistency of truth, scholasticism critique</keywords>

BY HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA.

...to drink <note>original: *haurire*; likely referring to drawing from the "font of truth" or "sacred springs" mentioned in the preceding text</note> from where the form of piety and justice is offered; it shall always be permitted to both debate and teach, and to act, by the favor of our Lord God, Jesus Christ of Nazareth, the crucified, who as the Angel of Great Counsel <note>original: *magni consilij angelus*; a traditional Christological title derived from the Latin Vulgate translation of Isaiah 9:6</note> <margin>Isaiah 9</margin> truly illuminates minds with light. We profess Him to be both true God and true man, and we look for Him as the Father of the age to come and as our Judge.

### CHAPTER SIX ###

Since the Gentiles <note>referring here to the pagan philosophers of antiquity</note> now recognize God as the primary cause and producer of all things from the very condition of created things, the condition itself shows who founded it, the craftsmanship demonstrates who made it, and the world itself makes manifest the one who set it in order. The Jews also—first from the tradition of the first-formed man <note>original: *protoplasti*; a Greek-derived term for Adam, the "first-molded" human</note> and then from Moses and the Prophets—received the same God as the maker of heaven and earth, and came to know Him through the ministry of the Law. We, however, in the Gospel from the Apostles, have received perfect knowledge of Him who is the same God above all gods, whose name is above every name. We have learned that His Word, naturally invisible, was made flesh as a visible and tangible man; that He humbled Himself even unto death—the death of the cross—and that those who believe in Him shall be made like Him, becoming incorruptible and beyond suffering <note>original: *impassibiles*; a technical theological term meaning exempt from pain or decay</note>, and shall receive the kingdom of heaven. Therefore, every man who is ignorant of God is without excuse, and cursed is he who, knowing Him, does not worship Him. For it is impossible, as the Apostle <note>referring to the author of the Epistle to the Hebrews 6:4</note> says, for those who were once illumined—

<summary>Agrippa outlines the three ways God is known: the Gentiles know Him through the natural world, the Jews through the Law and Prophets, and Christians through the Incarnation of Christ. He concludes that ignorance of God is inexcusable given these revelations.</summary>

<keywords>natural theology, revelation, Christology, Adam, Moses, Apostles, Incarnation, Isaiah 9, Gentiles, Jews</keywords>

...enlightened <note>Original: *illuminati*; the text begins with the suffix *-minati*, completing the word from the previous page</note> and have tasted the heavenly gift, and were made partakers of the Holy Spirit, and have tasted the good word of God, and the powers of the age to come, and have fallen away, to be renewed again to repentance. Therefore, we, warned by the dictate of creatures <note>The "dictate of creatures" refers to the "Book of Nature," the idea that the existence of the world naturally points toward a Creator</note>, taught by the announcement of the Prophets, and informed by the preaching of the Apostles, dare to say: That there is one alone who is the true God—uncreated, immense, eternal, almighty—Father, Son, and Holy Spirit; truly three persons co-eternal and co-equal to one another, yet one essence and substance, and an entirely simple nature, so that we may thus confess one God in Trinity, and Trinity in Unity, neither confounding the persons nor dividing the substance. For the Father from eternity begat the Son, and gave His own substance to him, and nonetheless retained it; the Son also, by being born, received the substance of the Father, yet did not assume the Father's own person, nor did the Father transfer his own person into the Son, for they are both of one and the same substance, but of distinct persons. This Son also, though he be co-eternal with the Father, begotten from the substance of the Father before the ages, nevertheless was born in time <note>original: *in seculo*; literally "in the age," referring to human history or the temporal world</note> from the substance of the Virgin, and his name was called Jesus, who is the Christ: perfect God, perfect man, subsisting from a rational soul and human flesh, to whom nothing human was lacking except for sin: one person, two natures; begotten as God before the ages without a mother; born as man in time without a father from a virgin, who remained incorrupt before and after the birth.

### ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD

...us <note>Completing the name "Jesus" from the previous page.</note> suffered on the cross and died, but on the cross he restored life, and by death he dissolved death. He was buried and descended to the underworld <note>original: *ad inferos*; in this theological context, this refers to the "Harrowing of Hell," the belief that Christ descended to the realm of the dead to liberate the souls of the righteous who died before his incarnation</note>, but he brought back the souls of the fathers from the depths. He rose again on the third day by his own power, and ascended into the heavens, and sent the Holy Spirit, the Comforter <term>Paraclete|From the Greek *paraklētos*, meaning an advocate, helper, or counselor; a title used in the New Testament specifically for the Holy Spirit</term>. He is to come again to judge the living and the dead, at whose coming all men must rise again in their own flesh and give an account of their own deeds. This is the perfect knowledge of God, in which it is necessary for us to be saved; anyone who does not acknowledge this, or does not believe those who do acknowledge it, or presumes to doubt it, is a stranger to the hope of life and eternal salvation.

### D FATHER AND LORD,

<note>The decorative initial letter "V" contains a figure of a bearded man, possibly a depiction of a saint or a classical scholar, seated and gesturing as if in mid-discussion.</note> I can scarcely express (most distinguished Prelate) with what great joy your letter has affected me, in which you indicated to me the benevolence and generosity of the royal majesty, and finally, with how much respect you have placed me in your debt: you who have worked so much on my behalf before the same Royal Majesty <note>original: *R. M.* abbreviation for *Regia Maiestas*; likely referring to the French court where Agrippa sought patronage</note>, and moreover promise that you will do even greater things. Thus, as I was considering by what testimony of my studies I might be able to show some gratitude of my soul toward you for such a great benefit, and looking around at what I held hidden among my paper furnishings <note>original: *chartacea mea suppellectile*; a metaphor for his collection of unpublished manuscripts and notes</note>, suddenly there presented itself a certain oration, or let us say a letter, with which I once decided to respond to the persistent requests of certain friends, and to discourage them from the study of Gentile theology <term>Gentile theology|The study of pagan philosophers and ancient mystical traditions, such as those of the Greeks, Romans, or Egyptians, which Agrippa often synthesized with Christian thought</term> in favor of Gospel doctrine. And so, reading this over, I revised it in a few places, judging it a worthy subject with which to gift you during this brief space of time, in which I do not have the opportunity to write more extensive works, nor for you, who by reason of your office...

<meta>This page continues the dedicatory epistle from Henry Cornelius Agrippa to Symphorien de Bullioud, Bishop of Bazas. It follows the previous page where Agrippa described finding an old manuscript in his papers regarding the superiority of the Gospel over "Gentile" (pagan) theology.</meta>

### HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

...ct to send to you. Since you are now occupied with other studies and cares on your behalf at the Royal Majesty <note>original: *R.M.* for *Regia Maiestas*; the French court where Agrippa sought patronage</note>, and there is leisure to read more extensively, I accordingly thought it best to send a small book on a useful and beneficial matter, rather than a very large volume on cheaper and more trivial topics. Receive, therefore, under a happy omen, our tiny literary gift, however small it is (though your virtue deserves greater things), and judge it not by the smallness of the volume, but by the utility and truth of the doctrine, and by the respect of my devotion toward you. 

I wished this *Dehortation* <note>original: *Dehortationem*; a formal speech or letter intended to discourage or advise against a certain path, in this case, the study of pagan philosophy over the Gospel</note> to be addressed to you for this reason: so that you may show yourself as a patron of that argument, and persuade your Most Christian King <note>This title, *Rex Christianissimus*, was the traditional formal style of the Kings of France</note> that—in accordance with the excellence of his name (by which he is called "Most Christian") and his duty in this Most Christian kingdom—he might command and protect the teachings of Christ to be taught freely, once they have been purged of all pagan, exotic, and adulterated doctrine, and set free from the snares of sophists <term>Sophists|In Agrippa's time, this was a derogatory term for Scholastic theologians who used complex, abstract logic and circular arguments rather than focusing on the literal and spiritual truth of the Bible</term>. 

For in this, he will show himself to be a most worthy and pious King, fitting for his "Most Christian" name. Farewell most happily, and [continue to] love me with that same affection with which you began... <note>The Latin text ends mid-sentence: *quo cœpisti amore me*, likely intended to conclude with a request for continued favor.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the formal dedication to the Bishop of Bazas to the main text of the "Dehortatio." **HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA’S**
**DISSUASION** from Pagan Theology, once delivered as an oration to some friends.

<note>A large decorative woodcut initial 'E' features a human figure intertwined with scrolling foliage.</note> You have now often demanded of me that, during this time of leisure, I explain to you the book of Thrice-Greatest Mercury <note>original: *ter maximi Mercurij*; referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary figure to whom the *Hermetica*—ancient texts on philosophy, magic, and religion—were attributed</note> regarding the wisdom and power of God (which some years ago I lectured on in a public reading at the famous university of Pavia <note>original: *Tyanensi*; likely a printer's error for *Ticinensi* (Pavia), where Agrippa is known to have lectured on the *Poimandres* in 1515</note>), and that I should show you the light of the *Poimandres* <note>original: *Pimandri*; meaning "The Shepherd of Men" or "Divine Mind," the primary spirit or intelligence who reveals divine secrets in the Hermetic texts</note> from that same book. 

However, since I perceived that this request was not useful to you, nor did I judge the intention itself to be right, I refused. Nevertheless, so that you might fulfill your vow and reach the limit of your desire, I offered to explain the Epistles of Paul to you; but now, having rejected those, you persist again and again, singing the same old song. I am greatly amazed and stunned, and I am angry, that you—who were baptized in the Gospel of Christ, <margin>A Rebuke</margin> and who perform the duties of the priesthood in the church of Christ—should seek knowledge of God from pagans after holy baptism and after the sacred chrism, as if the Gospel were incomplete <margin>Matthew 12</margin> <margin>4 Kings 1 <note>This refers to 2 Kings in modern Bibles; in the Vulgate, the books of Samuel and Kings were titled 1-4 Kings.</note></margin> and as if sinning against the Holy Spirit were pardonable. For whoever thinks that the knowledge of God is to be obtained from anywhere other than the Gospel, they are truly the ones who assault the Holy Spirit; this is a blasphemy that is forgiven neither in this world nor in the one to come.

<summary>Agrippa sternly rebukes his friends, who are ordained priests, for demanding he teach them Hermetic philosophy instead of the Epistles of St. Paul. He argues that looking to pagan "Gentile" sources for divine truth is a blasphemous rejection of the Gospel's sufficiency.</summary>
<keywords>Hermes Trismegistus, Poimandres, Gospel vs. Paganism, Epistles of St. Paul, Sin against the Holy Spirit, Pavia lectures, Priesthood, Renaissance Theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s "Dissuasion," transitioning from the specific request of his friends to a stern biblical warning. He compares their interest in Hermetic philosophy to the idolatry of the biblical King Ahaziah.</meta>

->**HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**<-

Have you not read about that great king of Samaria, Ahaziah <note>original: *Ochoziam*; a king of Israel who reigned in the 9th century BCE</note>, who, while he lay sick and dying in his bed, sent messengers to consult Baal-Zebub <note>original: *Belzebub*; the Philistine god of the city of Ekron</note>, the god of Ekron <note>original: *Accaron*</note>, to ask about his life and health? Have you not read how the angel of the Lord warned Elijah the Tishbite <note>original: *Heliam Thesbiten*</note>, the prophet of the Lord, who met those messengers and said to them: "Is there no God in Israel, that you go to consult Baal-Zebub, the god of Ekron? Because of this, the Lord says to the king: From the bed you have climbed onto, you shall not descend, but you shall surely die."

Could I not deter you with this example, or at least warn you and say: are there no doctors <note>meaning "teachers" or "learned theologians" of the Christian faith</note> in the church who can instruct you abundantly and rightly concerning God, that you must go to consult Mercury Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary author of the Hermetic texts</note>, the philosopher of the demons of Egypt? Or do you not know that your father is God, who created you, and your mother is indeed the Church, who gave you new life in baptism? Why then, like runaway children, do you beg for the nourishment of wisdom from elsewhere than from your parents? 

And you, who once renounced Satan in baptism, why do you rebel against God and return to Satan? You who were bought into freedom through the blood of Christ, why do you again expose yourselves as prey to the enemies of faith and truth? See to it and beware, lest while you seek the power and wisdom of God from somewhere other than God himself, you commit a wicked and unpardonable crime against that very power and wisdom. Beware, lest you be eventually abandoned to a depraved mind <note>original: *reprobum sensum*; a reference to Romans 1:28, describing a mind that has lost its moral compass after rejecting God</note>, and lose your life along with your salvation. For if the evil demon once steals the words of salvation from your memory, even if you wish to be saved, you will not [be able]... <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page: *non po[testis]*.</note>

<summary>Agrippa uses the biblical story of King Ahaziah to scold his friends for seeking divine wisdom in "pagan" Hermetic texts rather than in Christian scripture and the Church. He warns that such intellectual curiosity is a form of spiritual rebellion that risks their salvation.</summary>

### EXHORTATION AGAINST THE THEOLOGY OF THE GENTILES <note>original: *Exhortatio adversus Gentilium Theologiam*; "Gentiles" here refers to the non-Christian or pagan Greeks and Romans.</note>

...truth, you have lost the way through which one arrives at salvation. Therefore the Apostle warns you to beware lest anyone plunder you through philosophy. <margin>1 Corinthians 2</margin> <note>The marginal note refers to 1 Corinthians, but the quote is a paraphrase of Colossians 2:8.</note> If, therefore, a desire for divine things possesses you, it is not necessary to unearth these things from the heathens <note>original: *ethnicis*; meaning pagans or non-Christians</note>, for you have the testimony of God and the Gospel of truth set out in the open. You know that the Scriptures are indeed perfect, since they are given by the Word of God and His Spirit. 

<margin>The Objection</margin> But here you say that many things are found in the books of the heathens which agree with the teachings of the Gospel. Furthermore, you argue that these matters are treated by them with greater elegance of words, with harmony of sentences, and with a sweetness of speaking; and that these things provide you with a great argument for believing our faith, acting as a spur <note>original: *calcar*; a stimulus or motivation</note> to you. 

<margin>The Response</margin> You are entirely mistaken if you think you can imitate the principles of the Christian life from the writings of philosophers, or that you can be raised to virtue by them. Beware lest the philosophy of the heathens hold such power over you that Christian reason and doctrine become objects of contempt, even though many of their ideas may coincide with ours. 

But what is this madness of yours, what is this enchantment, that the Gospel's teaching pleases you more when found in Hermes, Plato, Plotinus, Aemilius, Iamblicus, Proclus, and other philosophers of that sort <note>original: *huius farinæ*; literally "of this flour," a common idiom meaning "of that ilk."</note>, than when found in the Apostles, the Evangelists, or in Christ himself? Are you not now like those who, <margin>Luke 4</margin> distrusting the words and miracles of Christ, believed the voices of demons and those <margin>Mark 1 & 5</margin> possessed by them? They had more faith in the demons who confessed Christ as the Son of God than in Christ himself. Do you also doubt to believe those things from Christ God, which he himself...

<summary>Agrippa warns his readers against valuing pagan philosophy over the Gospel, refuting the idea that the "elegant" style of Plato or Hermes makes their truths more valid. He compares those who prefer pagan sources to the biblical figures who trusted the testimony of demons more than the word of Christ.</summary>

Thus they conquer rather than deceive, while they take pride in the divine ark with vain voices, as if wisdom were located not in the heart, but on the tongue.

Do you not know that in the Gospel, Abraham responded to the rich man buried in hell—who was requesting a messenger be sent to his brothers—that those who did not wish to descend into that place should listen to Moses and the prophets?

<margin>Objection</margin> But far be it from you to say that we give the victory of faith to the dogmas of the heathens, or that we receive them as the word of God; rather, we receive them as the opinions and decrees of philosophers and literate men, so that we may compare them with the divine scriptures and adorn the scriptures through them.

# ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD

<note>original: "DE COGNOS. DEO." - De Cognoscendo Deo</note>

...you think you see most clearly, it is precisely there that you are most blind to the very light of truth. Just as Christ spoke of having come into the world so that those who do not see might see, and those who see might become blind <note>A reference to John 9:39</note>, are you not wandering in blindness if you believe you are honoring the Holy Scriptures with these things <note>referring to pagan philosophical ideas</note>, by which they are instead defiled and polluted?

But how can you judge yourselves able to bring these things together with those, <margin>2 Corinthians 6</margin> since there is no agreement between Christ and Belial, between light and darkness, or between a believer and infidels? Are you not like a sick man who invites both a doctor and poisoners to his cure? While the latter can indeed do harm, they cannot heal; the former refuses to mix his medicines with their poisons, lest either the destruction be attributed to the good man, or the health be credited to the harmful ones. Do you not yet understand that while you wish to join these things together, this very act is the subversion of your salvation? Granted, Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with a syncretic system of religion and philosophy</note> is a weighty author; suppose you also include Plato and Plotinus...

<summary>The author argues that mixing Christian scripture with pagan philosophy (like Plato or Trismegistus) is spiritually dangerous, comparing it to mixing medicine with poison.</summary>
<keywords>theology, philosophy, Hermes Trismegistus, Plato, Plotinus, spiritual blindness, syncretism</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s critique of human wisdom, specifically contrasting the authority of ancient philosophers with that of Christian scripture.</meta>

->HENRI. COR. AGRIP.<-

The wise are said to have spoken, and while their words may carry some weight in refuting the vanity of the pagans, they are not sufficient for us to embrace their authority. For as great a distance as there is between the preaching of the angels and the confession of demons regarding the coming of Christ, so great is the distance between the authority of the Prophets and the sacrilegious curiosity of the heathens. If we must grant much to the authority of the most serious men, to whom, I ask, do you think more is owed? To Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the Hermetic corpus</note>, Plato, Plotinus, and similar philosophers? Relying on their own devised opinions and with darkened intellects, they failed to perceive the highest secrets of God and nature; they handed down things concerning God and divine matters that are neither supported by reason nor suggested by the Holy Spirit, but are instead certain ravings, unworthy even of sensible men. Or is more owed to Moses, David, Solomon, the prophets, Evangelists, and Apostles? These men, filled with the divine spirit and through every kind of holiness, oracles, miracles, and even the peril of their own lives and the testimony of their shed blood, proved their doctrines. If, having set these aside, you run back to the philosophers and labor to learn from ethnic <note>original: "ethnicis," referring here to non-Christian or pagan sources</note> masters of error those things which you ought to receive from Christ and the Holy Spirit, will you not be insane as well as impious? If, therefore, you are now Christians, learn the letters of Christ. Indeed, He himself prescribed this mark to his own in **John 14**: "He who loves me," he says, "hears my words." From Him, therefore, one must learn what is to be understood concerning God; for the justice of God is found not in the traditions of philosophers, but in the Gospel.

<summary>Agrippa argues that the authority of Christian prophets and apostles far outweighs that of pagan philosophers like Plato or Hermes Trismegistus, urging readers to seek divine truth in the Gospels rather than human traditions.</summary>

<keywords>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, Hermeticism, Neoplatonism, Biblical authority, Gospel vs Philosophy, Hermes Trismegistus, Plato, Plotinus</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s argument from the previous page, asserting that Christ is the only true source of divine knowledge, and begins to address the counter-argument that the Church Fathers used pagan philosophy.</meta>

### ON THE KNOWLEDGE OF GOD

If you have not known God in Him <note>referring to Christ as the Word, continuing from the previous page</note>, do not hope to know Him in others at all; for no one else could ever describe the things that belong to God the Father, except His own Word. For who else has known the mind of the Lord, or who has become His counselor? <note>A citation of Romans 11:34, a common scriptural defense for the inscrutability of God’s will</note> But now you flee to examples as a refuge, and you say: <margin>Objection.</margin> "Will it not be permitted for us to imitate the holy fathers—Jerome, Augustine, Cyprian, Hilary, Lactantius, Eusebius, Victorinus, and many others—whose books are full of the little flowers of the gentiles <note>original: "gentilium flosculis," a metaphor for rhetorical ornaments or excerpts taken from non-Christian/pagan authors</note> and the opinions of philosophers, and indeed overflow with them?"

<margin>Response</margin> And yet, this is the single cause of evils (as Seneca says): <margin>Seneca.</margin> to live according to examples, and not to be composed by reason, but to be led astray by imitation. I know Jerome and Augustine, and I am well acquainted with the rest of the Doctors; but who are you, who desire to imitate them in those very things for which they themselves felt no small amount of regret for the effort spent? Indeed, they all agree on this: that these writings of the nations are a plague to religion and the law of Christ. Learn now from their warnings to avoid these things, rather than imitating the error of those who wandered before, rather than the repentance of those who later grew wiser with age.

For if examples hold any weight with you, let this one also hold: that Jerome, because he was reading Cicero <note>original: "Tullium," referring to Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman orator</note> and Plato too greedily, was struck by an angel. While many saints stood by and interceded on his behalf, he was at last released only upon swearing these words of a sacred oath: <margin>Jerome in his epistle</margin> <margin>to Eustochium.</margin> "Lord, if I ever possess secular books, if I read them, I have denied you." <note>This refers to Jerome's famous dream (Epistle 22) where he was brought before the divine judgment seat and accused: "You are a Ciceronian, not a Christian."</note> Nor indeed do I forbid us from imitating those holy men, but I do not praise your proposal to follow what is the least and lowest in them; instead, I urge you...

Specifically, you must cut and shave away her hair <note>Agrippa refers to a passage in Deuteronomy 21:11–13, interpreted by St. Jerome as a metaphor: just as a beautiful captive woman must be purified before a Hebrew could marry her, pagan philosophy must be stripped of its errors before being used by Christians.</note>; and whatever pleasure or error of idolatry there was, you should cast it out, so that from this union you might generate for the God of Hosts <note>term: "God Sabaoth" (Lord of Hosts)</note> offspring born into His service. It is just as the prophet Hosea fathered a son from a harlot, who was called the "seed of God." 

If you are able to do this—transferring the cleansed literature of the pagans to Christian erudition, and snatching away the rich spoils from the Egyptians as if from unjust possessors, either secretly or by force, to enrich the Church of God with their wealth—then I no longer strongly discourage it. Indeed, I then advise you toward ethnic literature. 

<margin>Book 2 of *On Christian Doctrine*, chapter 8.</margin>
But I fear that you may attempt this in vain. Therefore, I command you to use the counsel of Augustine: namely, that the Holy Scripture should be your primary focus, lest alien opinions occupy a weak mind.

->AN EXHORTATION TO THE STUDENTS OF THEOLOGY<-

For indeed, to reach a truthful knowledge of divine matters, there is need of a pure mind, one not infected by corrupt opinions. For if the mind is preoccupied by certain less-than-right opinions, then through those—as if under the guise of some kind of knowledge—the error of ignorance becomes more deeply rooted in it than can be easily pulled out. It then happens that a person believes himself to know that which he is actually ignorant of, and he defends as true and universal <note>original: "catholicum"</note> that which is in fact an error or a heresy.

Therefore, in your theological studies, nothing ought to come before the Gospel doctrine; you must enter by this path <margin>2 Thessalonians 2</margin>, even if you do not understand every single mystery to perfection <note>original: "ad unguem," literally 'to the fingernail,' a Latin idiom for flawless precision</note>. Next, apply the commentaries of the ancient Holy Fathers who have been received by the Church <margin>To Titus 3</margin>; after that, consult the modern authors <note>original: "neotericos," referring to more recent scholastic or contemporary theologians</note>. However, you must entirely avoid curious inquiries about God and divine things, the wrangling of questions, and the battles of disputations, according to the precept of the Apostle.

But when your mind has finally been strengthened and fortified by universal doctrine, then, by its light, you shall walk securely through all the shadows of error. Then you will be able to freely penetrate all kinds of disciplines. Then, like Ulysses, if you should enter the cave of the Cyclops or descend to the underworld, you shall return unharmed; if you are carried away to the Lotus-eaters or to the quicksands of the Syrtes, you shall return safe; if you drink the cup of Circe, you shall not be transformed. If you sail past Scylla, you shall not be endangered; if you hear the Sirens, you shall not fall asleep. Instead, you shall be "judging all things," as the Apostle says <margin>1 Corinthians 2</margin>, "and judged by no one." At that point, the teachings of the pagans <note>original: "ethnicorum," referring to classical Greek and Roman authors</note> will be of the greatest help to you, and their...

...by its support, you will then easily ascend to the summit of theology.

But if you enter upon this journey in a reversed order, you will lose both yourselves and your studies. Like those blinded with their eyes gouged out, unable to see the right path, you will wander aimlessly back and forth, stumbling against every stone. Once you have turned away from the right path, you will no longer be able to be guided back to it, not even with the keenest-scented dog <note>original: "nasutissimo cane," literally 'the most big-nosed dog,' a Latin idiom for a tracking hound with an acute sense of smell</note> as your guide.

Now, therefore, believe me—though I am but a younger man—while you still have time for learning. For if you do not believe me now, you will believe me too late in the end, and you shall at the same time lament your choice.

->THE END.<-

### ON THE LITTLE BOOK CONCERNING THE TRIPLE WAY <note>original: "De Triplici Ratione," referring to the three ways of knowing God: through nature, through the soul, and through revelation</note> of knowing God.

If you, O mortal race, seek to know the powerful, true God,
In whose image you are said to be made,
Read this work which Cornelius <note>Referring to the author, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), a famous Renaissance polymath and theologian</note> has constructed with marvelous skill,
Whose noble intellect has forged this task.
He wisely urges you to know yourself first, and then he adds
By what method you may be able to know God.

To the REVEREND FATHER IN CHRIST, and lord doctor of sacred theology, Lord Theodoric, Bishop of Cyrene, and most worthy administrator in sacred matters for the suffragan see of the Archbishopric of Cologne, Henry Cornelius Agrippa commends himself.

# ON ORIGINAL SIN

[Large decorative initial D] Regarding original sin and the first transgression of man, various opinions are offered among the most learned and excellent interpreters of sacred letters, both ancient and modern. When I frequently revolved these in my mind, it seemed to me that they had written nothing but certain obscure and ambiguous things. Therefore, not out of contempt, but having left their opinions behind, I shall bring forward another new opinion of my own (merely as a matter for debate, and perhaps not false). It would be a sin if anyone else had thought the same before me; but if such a person exists, I do him no injury if I call this opinion my own, insofar as I have rescued it from the darkness of neglect by no other means than the diligence of my own wit and the examination of the scriptures. I have therefore produced a brief and concise declamation of my opinion, which I dedicate to your reverend and sincere paternity, so that by your authority it may emerge more secure from the future storm of attackers. For I know how great a storm of syllogisms <note>A form of logical argument where a conclusion is drawn from two premises.</note> will rush upon it among certain "theophists" <note>A derogatory term Agrippa uses for scholastic theologians who prioritize logic over spiritual insight.</note>—among those, I say, with whom there is no room for simplicity, but only ambitious contention reigns. This material will not collapse, nor is it provoked by their syllogisms, provided it is protected by your support against Egyptian gnats <margin>Psalm 143 <note>Likely a reference to the Vulgate numbering; Agrippa compares his critics to the stinging plagues of Egypt.</note></margin> of this kind. Farewell.

I 3

<margin>Genesis 1</margin> In the beginning of the book of Genesis it is written that God said, "Let us make man in our image and likeness." And God made man; he perfected him in his image and likeness. We believe this was said concerning the **inner man** <note>Agrippa follows a tradition—influenced by both Plato and Saint Paul—that distinguishes the "inner man" (the rational, eternal soul) from the "outer man" (the physical, mortal body).</note>, who is the rational soul created in the likeness of the divine Trinity as well as its unity. Once this inner man was thus divinely established, God bestowed upon him a suitable garment and dwelling: namely, the human body. As the following <margin>Genesis 2</margin> text shows when it says: "God formed man from the mud of the earth." And again the scripture adds, "and he breathed into his face the breath of life," which is a certain power by which the intellectual soul and the earthly body are joined and united into one complete man. Thus, man was composed of a divine soul, an earthly body, and a **heavenly spirit** <note>In Agrippa's philosophy, the 'spirit' (spiritus) acts as a middle ground or 'glue' that allows the immaterial soul to communicate with and animate the heavy, material body.</note>. He was immortal in his body until, by his own will and through contempt for the divine command, he fell into a mortal body. We shall declare in order what kind of fall this was, occurring in both the inner and outer man. <margin>Genesis 2</margin> Therefore, once man was first created (as we have already said), God placed him in the middle of paradise. For us, paradise [signifies] a garden of delights...

There were in paradise various fruit-bearing trees, <margin>Genesis 2 & 3</margin> and above all others were the **tree of life** and the **tree of the knowledge of good and evil**, which was in the middle of paradise. 

The tree of life is the knowledge of God and the constant contemplation of Him, in all piety and justice; its fruit is wisdom and chastity, from which comes eternal life. <margin>Proverbs 3</margin> Whence Solomon says: "Wisdom is a tree of life to those who take hold of her." The tree of the knowledge of good and evil is the affection of the flesh and worldly prudence; its fruit is the neglect or ignorance of God, from which comes the death of both body and soul. This was...

# HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

...[more] cunning than all the living creatures of the earth. <note>original: "callidior cunctis animantibus terrae." A reference to Genesis 3:1.</note> Adam is indeed faith established in God: the foundation of reason. Therefore, from Adam’s rib Eve was created: free reason. But the serpent is sensuality itself, which crawls among earthly, decaying, lowest, and carnal things. And so this serpent, through the longing for <term>sensible things</term> <note>In this context, "sensible things" (sensibilia) refers to objects that can be perceived by the physical senses—the material world—rather than spiritual truths.</note>, tempted Eve—that is, reason—who, by yielding to the serpent (namely, the allurements of the senses), was deceived. 

She then, through argumentative discourse concerning sensible things, deceived the man Adam, casting down faith. This faith, having slipped down into sensible things and believing nothing more of the truth than what can be demonstrated by reason from the senses, fell away from God and from divine knowledge and contemplation, for which it was created by God. Through this ignorance and neglect of God—which we previously called the fruit of the forbidden tree—man was deprived of divine light; then he did not at all obtain life, but incurred perpetual death.

# ON ORIGINAL SIN

...belongs to faith, nor should faith succumb to reason, but rather hope firmly in God. And therefore God condemned Adam <margin>Genesis 3</margin> because he believed the woman; nor indeed should we dispute about divine matters, but firmly believe and hope. 

Regarding those things that have been created, however, it is permitted for us to philosophize, dispute, and reach conclusions through the service of reason—yet we must not place our faith and hope in them. From this, one can now see how foolish are those who pursue the "revolutions of the world’s loves," nativities, <term>questions</term> <note>In traditional astrology, "questions" (horary astrology) involves casting a chart to answer a specific question asked at a specific moment.</note>, <term>elections</term> <note>"Elections" (electional astrology) is the practice of choosing the most astrologically auspicious time to begin an undertaking.</note>, and any similar artifice of images, intentions, divinations, and the like. 

They believe in such vanities and hope for future things from them, placing their confidence in the powers of created things—whether natural, celestial, or spiritual. These include <term>Physics</term> <note>Here used in the classical sense of "natural philosophy" or the study of the material world.</note>, Alchemy, Mathematics, Astrology, and Magic. Although these are sciences proven through the discourse of reason or through many kinds of sensory experience, they very often deceive human minds, pervert the judgment of conscience, and cast down the soul itself; therefore, the faith and hope of men should not be defiled by them. 

This is what Isaiah himself reproaches the Babylonians with, saying: <margin>Isaiah 47</margin> "Your wisdom and your knowledge have deceived you." And: "Let the astrologers of the heavens save you"—for thus it is read more correctly than 'the <term>haruspices</term> <note>Diviners in ancient Rome who interpreted the will of the gods by inspecting the entrails of sacrificed animals.</note> of heaven'—"from whom may come to you the things that are to come; you have grown weary in the multitude of your counsels." <note>original: "Sapientia tua & scientia tua ipsa decepit te... Saluent te astrologi coelorum." Agrippa is quoting Isaiah 47:10-13, arguing that reliance on intellectual systems and divination leads to spiritual exhaustion.</note>

These things concerning the tree of life, the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, and the fall of the inner man, it seemed right to first explain according to the moral sense. It remains for us to show what original sin was <term>effectively</term> <note>In scholastic philosophy, "effectively" (effective) refers to the actual cause or the mechanical reality of how something occurred, moving from allegory to literal or historical explanation.</note> in the matter itself, on account of which man himself [was cast out] from paradise...

<meta>Continuity with previous page: This page continues from the discussion of how human reason and worldly sciences can lead the soul astray, moving now into Agrippa's specific, controversial theory regarding the nature of the Fall and Original Sin.</meta>

**HENRICUS CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**

...driven out, he was made subject to the death of the body and the soul, and to all other evils. Many theologians, both ancient and modern, have held many opinions on this matter, and each of them <margin>Romans 14</margin> "abounds in his own sense" <note>original: "unusquisq; eorū in suo sensu abundat." A reference to Romans 14:5: "Let every man be fully persuaded in his own mind," often used in the Renaissance to justify offering personal interpretations on non-dogmatic issues.</note>. I do not know if I am fully worthy of pardon for contributing my own opinion—and I say opinion, not faith, nor knowledge—so that if my opinion should be erroneous, I might remain far from the error of true faith and from any violation of undefiled Christian wisdom. 

On this condition, it shall now be permitted to opine freely; and even if (for I am a man of immature age, and of lesser intellect and learning) I do not abound in my own sense with as many testimonies of the scriptures as the matter itself requires, perhaps some more learned men will follow me, to whom this opinion of ours will not be displeasing. I trust that I can be helped in no small way by them, insofar as they might make our opinion stronger through their own more valid reasons and arguments. 

<margin>Agrippa's Opinion.</margin> Our opinion itself is this: that original sin was nothing other than the carnal copulation of man and woman. We have already persuaded some of this with a few conjectures from the sacred scriptures in a certain dialogue of ours regarding man, but now we shall show that very same thing a little more expansively (yet still succinctly). 

<margin>Genesis 3.</margin> For Moses himself, explaining the fall of our first parents through a parable suited to those who contemplate such things, teaches that a certain evil demon—cunning, crafty, and envious of human salvation—clung to man. This demon he calls the serpent, by whose fraud the first parents and all those born from them lost that blessedness of immortality. We believe this serpent to be none other than the sensory and carnal impulse, or rather that which...

<summary>Agrippa introduces his controversial thesis that Original Sin was not the eating of a literal fruit, but rather the first act of sexual intercourse. He frames this as a personal "opinion" rather than church dogma to protect himself from charges of heresy.</summary>

<meta>Continuity with the previous page: Agrippa continues his provocative argument that Original Sin was not a literal eating of fruit, but the first act of sexual intercourse. He begins this page by explicitly identifying the serpent of Genesis with male anatomy.</meta>

**ON ORIGINAL SIN**

And we would say that the male genital member of carnal concupiscence itself—the crawling member, the serpent member, the slippery member, winding with various bends—is what tempted and deceived Eve. To this member, the name and likeness of the serpent rightly belong. Therefore, we believe that no other demon tempted Eve than that one of whom Job says: *His strength is in his loins, and his power is in the navel*, <margin>Job 40</margin> <note>original: "Cuius uirtus est i lūbis, & potestas i umbilico." Agrippa is quoting the Description of Behemoth from Job 40:16 (Vulgate), which he allegorically interprets as the source of sexual desire.</note> and whom Paul calls the *thorn in the flesh* and the *messenger of Satan*. <margin>1 Corinthians 12</margin> <note>original: "stimulū carnis & angelū sathanæ." While the margin notes 1 Corinthians, this is a reference to 2 Corinthians 12:7. Agrippa identifies Paul’s "thorn" as sexual impulse.</note> This is that Satan who persuaded her toward the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, regarding which the prohibition was true—not because God begrudged man the knowledge of good, but because he prohibited the knowledge of evil. He did not forbid man to be wise, but to be wiser than was proper. Furthermore, that knowledge of evil—this "knowledge of superfluity"—is nothing other than the knowledge of the flesh, the knowledge of impurity. Paul teaches us this: not to be wiser than is proper, <margin>Romans 12</margin> but to be *wise unto chastity*—not "unto sobriety," as Jerome testifies is wrongly read and translated in the Latin codices. <margin>Jerome against Jovinian</margin> <note>original: "sapere ad castitatē (nō ad sobrietatē, ut teste Hieronymo male in latinis codicibus legitur traductū)." Agrippa is engaging in textual criticism of Romans 12:3, arguing that the Greek "sōphronein" should be understood as sexual purity/chastity rather than general moderation or sobriety.</note> Therefore, whatever exists besides chastity, in the pollution of the flesh, is this evil and superfluous knowledge. When man had fed upon this carnal knowledge, the Almighty was indignant, and he *regretted that he had made man*; <margin>Genesis 6</margin> God regretted it because *the virgin of Israel has fallen, and there is no one to raise her up*. <margin>Amos 5</margin> He regretted it, because while God can do all things, he cannot raise a virgin after her ruin <note>Agrippa suggests a theological paradox: that even God's omnipotence cannot undo the historical fact of lost virginity.</note>. Therefore, as the divine light and the spirit of chastity were indignant and withdrew, the knowledge of the flesh grew strong, <margin>Genesis 3</margin> in which *the eyes of both were opened*, and they knew evil. What then did they know? What did they see? They certainly knew their own evil, their own sin, their own death, on account of their impurity; and they saw that they were naked, and they were ashamed, and...

<summary>Agrippa presents a radical phallic interpretation of the Fall, identifying the serpent of Eden with the male reproductive organ and the "Tree of Knowledge" with carnal copulation. He argues that the loss of virginity is the "evil knowledge" that caused God to regret creating humanity.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: Agrippa continues his argument that the "Serpent" was a metaphor for the male sexual organ and that the "Fall" was the first act of sexual intercourse. This page focuses on the shame following the act and the expulsion from Eden.</meta>

# HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

They hid themselves from the face of the Lord, and they hid those members <note>The Latin *membra* (limbs/members) here refers specifically to the genitals.</note> in which they had sinned through immodesty. On account of the lust they had committed, they blushed; and having covered their shameful parts, they felt the shame of their nakedness. Once their eyes were opened, they recognized the evil fruit they had produced <margin>Romans 6</margin>—the fruit of corruption and death. Paul addresses them with these words: *What fruit did you have then in those things of which you are now ashamed? For the end of those things is death.* <note>original: "quem ergo fructum habuistis in illis, in quibus usque nunc erubescitis, nam finis illorum mors est." Agrippa quotes Romans 6:21 to support his claim that the "fruit" of the Fall was not an apple, but the mortality resulting from sexual procreation.</note>

For even to this day, human nature is undoubtedly ashamed of this lust, which is not without shame even among wives. Nor is that which has been granted to us with a wife—on account of the fragility of our flesh—so just or lawful that one would not be ashamed to do it openly.

But if anyone, following the dogma of Diogenes the Cynic <note>Diogenes of Sinope (c. 412–323 BCE) was a Greek philosopher who famously rejected social conventions, reportedly performing even private acts in public to demonstrate that what is natural should not be shameful.</note>, were to dare to attempt this openly, even with his wife, I believe he would be overwhelmed not so much by the weapons of those throwing stones, but by the filth of dung and the saliva of those spitting upon him.

Therefore, on account of this shameful lust of the flesh, man was cast out from the Paradise of God and delivered unto death, because "flesh and blood," as Paul says, <margin>1 Corinthians 15</margin> "cannot possess the kingdom of God, nor can corruption possess incorruption." <note>original: "caro & sanguis... regnum Dei possidere non possunt, neque corruptio incorruptionem." Agrippa cites 1 Corinthians 15:50 to equate sexual "corruption" with the inability to enter heaven.</note> For the corruption of the flesh cannot possess the rewards of modesty.

Now, "corruption" is sexual intercourse, and it pertains to intercourse, the reward of which is death; but the reward of chastity is eternal life, the kingdom of God, and a dwelling in Paradise. For we know that Adam and Eve inhabited Paradise as long as they were virgins, but they were cast out of Paradise when their marriage was consummated through the flesh. Thus, just as virginity granted Paradise—

<summary>Agrippa argues that the "shame" felt by Adam and Eve proves that their sin was sexual, noting that even "lawful" sex in marriage remains a source of private embarrassment today. He concludes that the Fall occurred the moment the first couple lost their virginity.</summary>

<keywords>Original Sin, sexual ethics, Diogenes the Cynic, Romans 6, 1 Corinthians 15, virginity, Eden, corruption, modesty</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: Agrippa is continuing his argument that the "Original Sin" was the transition from a spiritual, virginal state in Paradise to a physical, lustful state of procreation after the Fall.</meta>

# CONCERNING ORIGINAL SIN

...in this way, marriage exists on earth. In paradise, there were marriages of virginity, intended to be consummated in the word and spirit of God; but outside of paradise, there are marriages of the flesh, consummated in corruptible coitus <note>The Latin *coitu* refers to sexual intercourse. Agrippa argues that the physical act itself is a mark of our fallen state.</note>, by which all who are born are corrupted. This is according to the scripture: *Whatever a man sows, that he will also reap; he who sows <margin>Galatians 6</margin> in his flesh, will from the flesh reap corruption; he who sows in the spirit of chastity, will reap eternal life.* <note>original: "Quodcunq; seminauerit homo & metet, qui seminat in carne sua, de carne metet corruptionem, qui seminat in spiritu castitatis, metet uitam æternam." Agrippa is quoting Galatians 6:7-8.</note> 

But man, when he was in the honor of virginity, refused to understand <margin>Psalm 48</margin> that he should live chastely, but through the lust of the flesh he became like the irrational beasts <note>original: "iumentis," literally beasts of burden or cattle.</note>, and through death he was made equal to them. God said to men: *Do not become <margin>Psalm 31</margin> like the horse and the mule, in which there is no understanding.* They, however <margin>Jeremiah 5</margin> (according to the word of the Prophet), became horses raging after females; each one was neighing after his neighbor’s wife. 

The canonical epistle speaks of these people, because <margin>2 Peter 2</margin> they walk after the flesh in the desires of pollution, or like irrational beasts they are prone to the belly and to lust; therefore, they will be destroyed in their own corruption. And so, while man began to generate from the pleasure of the flesh in the manner of brutes, he lost the spiritual seed of divine and intuitive intelligence. As God said: *My spirit shall not remain <margin>Genesis 6</margin> with man, because he is flesh.* 

Thus, his eyes were opened in the darkness of the senses, from which the human reason now gathers all the material for its knowledge. His eyes grew dim to the light of pure intellect, just as usually happens to those coupling in the dark: once the carnal seed is spent, all things that were previously visible to them become obscured; and even when they return to the clear light, their vision is dazzled and weakened. <note>Agrippa uses a physiological metaphor here: just as physical sight is momentarily blurred after sexual climax, he argues that the human "spiritual eye" was permanently clouded by the first act of lust.</note>

<summary>Agrippa argues that the Fall transformed "spiritual marriage" into "carnal marriage," reducing humans to the level of irrational animals driven by lust. He claims this physical shift caused humanity to lose its divine intuition, replacing spiritual light with limited sensory perception.</summary>

<keywords>Original Sin, virginity, lust, procreation, Galatians 6, animal nature, spiritual intelligence, sensory perception, fall of man</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s argument regarding the inherent corruption of the flesh and the spiritual consequences of sexual generation, drawing on both Hermetic and Biblical sources.</meta>

->HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

Therefore, that which is born of the flesh was corruptible and mortal flesh, participating in nothing of the intellect from the one who generates it, but only in carnal sensibility. For only the sensible and carnal soul comes from the progenitor; into this soul—already infected by the flesh—the intellect created by God is simultaneously immersed and suffocated. Hence, it is necessary that all who are begotten of the flesh, and who themselves beget through the propagation of the flesh and the senses, contract original guilt and corruption. <margin>Poimandres, chapter 1</margin> This is also what Hermes <note>referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of Hermeticism</note> shows in the *Poimandres* in a sufficiently long discourse: namely, that man fell from divine contemplation into the nature of the generation of the world's mortal and irrational living creatures; whence he himself brought forth an irrational form, perceiving the evils of death through his senses.

But these things which have already been said might perhaps seem to have been explained by violently twisting the scriptures to strengthen our opinion, unless we bring forward still other testimonies, by which it may be more clearly seen <margin>Genesis 3</margin> that the mingling of the flesh was displeasing to God. This is now shown first from the curse of childbirth: that she who conceived a child in the pleasure of the flesh should bring it forth in pain. It is as if it were said: <margin>Psalm 7</margin> "Behold, he has conceived injustice, he has been in labor with sorrow, and has brought forth iniquity; he opened a pit and dug it out, and fell into the ditch which he made; let his sorrow be turned back upon his own head, and let his iniquity descend upon his own crown." <note>Agrippa applies the imagery of Psalm 7:14-16 to the act of procreation and the Fall</note> Added to this is a certain indignation of nature, since neither is a virgin overpowered <note>original: "opprimitur," likely referring to the loss of virginity</note> nor does a woman give birth without blood. But let us see what followed. Adam begat two sons: the first, Cain, <margin>Genesis 4</margin>

<summary>Agrippa argues that human intellect is "suffocated" by carnal birth, citing the Hermetic Fall and the biblical curse of painful labor as evidence that sexual reproduction is the vehicle for original sin.</summary>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s argument regarding the corruption of the flesh through sexual generation, now focusing on the biblical lineages of Cain and Seth to illustrate the distinction between carnal sin and spiritual righteousness.</meta>

**ON ORIGINAL SIN**

He who was conceived through transgression <note>referring to Cain, whom Agrippa views as the product of Adam and Eve's fallen state</note> was a son of sin, a son of blood, envious, a murderer, and one who despaired of God’s mercy; for he who was conceived with such great evil could not be born good. But another son was born, Abel, who was conceived after the repentance for the transgression—a son of mercy and a righteous man. This man, a virgin, was slain, becoming the first sacrifice accepted by God for the expiation of the flesh; he washed away the filth of carnal marriages with the blood of martyrdom. Following him comes a third son, Seth, <margin>Genesis 4, 5</margin> whose name signifies "resurrection." For from him, Jesus Christ was destined to be born, <margin>Genesis 5</margin> through whom we were eventually to rise again from such a great fall. Regarding this, it should be noted that while it is read that Seth fathered many sons and daughters, the scripture does not specifically express the name of any female born to him. In the generations of Cain, however—the "son of wrath"—a female born last, named Naamah, <margin>Genesis 4</margin> is specifically mentioned. By this, it is elegantly signified that the generation of Cain (which is described through the carnal coupling of males and females) is a generation of sin leading toward death, from which are born the sinners destined to perish in the flood. 

The generation of Seth, however, is a generation of chastity and of resurrection toward life. He fathered Enos, whose name is interpreted as "true man"; it was then that the name of the Lord began to be invoked, and this generation is saved from the flood of waters and from the death of sinners. Therefore, the generation of Cain, beginning from Adam, ends in the number of eleven persons <note>Agrippa uses biblical numerology here: the number eleven was traditionally seen as a symbol of overstepping or "transgressing" the completeness of the number ten</note> at the time of the flood. This is the number of sin and transgression, for the number ten is the number of the law and of justice. It was in this tenth position that the righteous Noah was born, being the tenth from Adam through the line of Seth. Therefore, the number eleven, by departing from the number ten, signifies the transgression of the law and the commandments; it was in this number that the generation of Cain perished.

<summary>Agrippa interprets the genealogies of Cain and Seth as allegories for sin and righteousness, using biblical numerology to argue that Cain's line represents carnal corruption while Seth's line represents spiritual restoration.</summary>

<keywords>original sin, Cain, Abel, Seth, genealogy, Noah, Enos, Naamah, numerology, transgression, resurrection</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s argument that original sin was rooted in sexual intercourse. Having established the contrast between the lineages of Seth and Cain, he now uses biblical numerology and the story of Noah’s Ark to prove that carnal pleasure was the specific cause of the Fall and the subsequent Great Flood.</meta>

# BY HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

From this it should be noted that just as transgression and the flood of sins began through a woman <note>Referring to Eve’s role in the Fall</note>, so also the "flood of waters" was brought to its peak in a woman—specifically in Naamah, the final daughter of Lamech the bigamist, for the name Naamah signifies "pleasure" <note>original: "Noema uoluptatē sonat." In Hebrew, Naamah (נַעֲמָה) means "pleasant" or "lovely."</note>. From this we can reasonably conjecture that the transgression consisted of the pleasure of the flesh, and that the flood of sins arose from it.

Added to this is the fact that the lineage of Cain from Adam is washed away in the eighth generation; this number "eight" oversteps the number seven in the same way that eleven oversteps ten <note>In medieval numerology, moving beyond a "perfect" number like 7 or 10 symbolized excess or transgression</note>. Furthermore, the sacred doctors call the number seven the "number of virginity" because it is the first number that neither is generated nor generates. For it cannot be divided into two equal parts so as to be born from another repeated number <note>i.e., it is prime</note>, nor does it produce any other number from itself when doubled that remains within the limits of ten—which is established as the first boundary of numbers <note>Agrippa is using Pythagorean arithmetic: 7 is the only number in the first decade (1–10) that is neither a factor of another number nor a product of two others. This "sterile" quality was equated with virginal purity.</note>.

Thus we easily perceive that the transgression of virginity through carnal pleasure was the sin of our first parent. This was already punished once by the flood, but it could not be abolished. For those who were saved in the flood—Noah and his sons—were themselves born through the flesh and they fathered children. Nevertheless, as long as Noah and his sons inhabited the Ark of Salvation (which serves as a figure for the earthly paradise and a type <note>A "type" is a person or event in the Old Testament that foreshadows a reality in the New Testament or the Church</note> of the Church), they were separated from their wives. From this let it be known to us that in paradise it was forbidden to our first parents to couple carnally, a truth which Paul, the herald of the Christian religion, proclaims toward the heavens: "It is good," he says, "for a man not to touch a woman."

<margin>1 Corinthians 7</margin>

<summary>Agrippa argues that the Great Flood was a punishment for carnal pleasure, using the name of Naamah and the numerology of the number seven to link the Fall of Man to sexual generation. He highlights that Noah and his family practiced celibacy while inside the Ark to mirror the original, intended state of humanity in Paradise.</summary>

**ON ORIGINAL SIN.**

"not to touch," <note>original: "non tangere." Referring to 1 Corinthians 7:1: "It is good for a man not to touch a woman."</note> he does not say "not to have," but "not to touch," because there is danger in the touch—not the touch of the hands, but that of the serpent, whose strength is in his loins and whose power is in his navel. <margin>Job 40</margin> <note>Agrippa refers to the description of Behemoth in Job 40:16. In medieval theology, this was often interpreted allegorically as the devil's power residing in human lust and the reproductive organs.</note>

Regarding those who marry because of the "tribulation of the flesh," <margin>1 Corinthians 7</margin> he speaks by way of indulgence: "if a virgin marries, she does not sin." He does not say that she "does well," but he grants an indulgence so that she might not sin; <note>Agrippa is making a legalistic distinction: marriage is a "concession" to prevent the greater sin of fornication, rather than a positive virtuous act.</note> for it is one thing not to sin, and quite another to "do well." Concerning this he says elsewhere: "he who keeps his virginity does well." And if anyone should say, "he who joins his virgin in marriage does well," the Apostle responds: "he who does not join her does *better*." <margin>1 Corinthians 7</margin>

Finally, he says to the married: "It remains that those who have wives should be as though they had none." And elsewhere, explaining the passage "the two shall be in one flesh," he adds: "this is a great **sacrament** <margin>Ephesians 5</margin> <note>original: "sacramentum." In this context, it carries the sense of a "profound mystery" or a sacred sign.</note>—but I speak in Christ and the Church." For how can a sacrament be "great" in the flesh, which is unclean? For the purity of the body of <margin>1 Peter 1</margin> Christ—of which we have been made members and in which we have been <margin>James 1</margin> reborn—comes not from corruptible intercourse, but through the Word of the living and enduring God; who (as James says in his epistle), "willingly begot us by the word of truth." Therefore, compared to the purity of *this* sacrament, of *this* "intercourse" (if I may call it that), and of *this* generation, all physical intercourse is unclean.

For this reason the Apostle says: "Husbands, love your <margin>Ephesians 5</margin> wives as Christ loved the Church"—namely, in sanctification and chastity. Concerning which we read elsewhere: "Let each one know how to possess his own **vessel** <margin>1 Thessalonians 4</margin> <note>original: "uas suum." Paul uses "vessel" to refer to either one's own body or one's wife; Agrippa uses it here to emphasize self-control over carnal desire.</note> in sanctification and honor, not in uncleanness and the passion of carnal desire, as the Gen[tiles]..."

<margin>Genesis 17</margin> After the flood, the seal of circumcision followed, given to Abraham, the beloved of God. It was at once a punishment and a medicine for original sin. It was performed upon the foreskin, in that very member in which the first parent had sinned; <note>Agrippa explicitly identifies the male genitals as the site of the first sin, reinforcing his argument that "Original Sin" was a sexual act.</note> this was done so that the sin of origin and of nature might be cut away in that very member of nature and origin, and that a memory of transgressed continence and chastity might be imprinted upon that very member of lust. 

However, because this sin is contracted from the male father and not from the female mother—since it is believed that women add no "form of life" to the offspring to be born, but only supply the "matter" of the body—women therefore remained uncircumcised. <note>Agrippa relies on the Aristotelian biological theory common in the Renaissance: the father provides the active "seed" or "soul/form," while the mother provides only the passive physical "matter." Therefore, in his logic, the spiritual stain of sin is transmitted through the father.</note> Instead, the precept of purification after childbirth was given to them. <margin>Leviticus 12</margin>

# THE WORKS OF HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA

Jude <note>original: "Iudā"; a reference to the Epistle of Jude 1:5</note> records that the Lord saved and led the people of Israel out of Egypt, and brought them into the land of promise. This was a land which Moses—being a married man—was not permitted to enter; instead, he died in the land of Moab and was buried in a valley opposite the temple of Peor <note>original: "Phegor"</note>. This name signifies Priapus <note>The Greco-Roman god of fertility and lust; Renaissance scholars frequently identified the biblical Baal-Peor with Priapus</note>, which is also interpreted as "ignominy." But let us now return to our point. <margin>Exodus 19</margin> When the people of Israel were about to approach Mount Sinai to hear the word of God, they were ordered to be sanctified for three days and to abstain from their wives. This is that mountain of which it is said elsewhere: "Make yourself safe upon the mountain." <margin>Genesis 19</margin> This is the Mountain of Virginity; for whoever cannot ascend to it is permitted to remain in Zoar <note>original: "Segor"</note>—that is, in lawful marriage—because it is better for the incontinent to marry than to be cast headlong down the precipices of fornication <note>A paraphrase of 1 Corinthians 7:9: "It is better to marry than to burn."</note>. <margin>1 Corinthians 7</margin> <margin>Leviticus 10</margin> Furthermore, in the Law itself, those who offered sacrifices for the people not only remained away from their own homes, but were purified by being separated from their wives for a time; they also drank neither wine nor strong drink <note>original: "sicara"; any fermented liquor other than wine</note>, which are known to provoke lust.

<margin>1 Samuel 21</margin> Even to David, when he was fleeing, it was said by Ahimelech the priest: "If the young men are clean of women [they may eat]." David responded that they had been clean "from yesterday and the day before yesterday." For it was not permitted to eat the bread of the Presence <note>original: "panes ppoſitionis"; the twelve loaves kept in the Tabernacle, also called showbread</note> if one had just risen from the beds of wives. <margin>Deuteronomy 20</margin> And in the Law, he who had taken a wife, or he who had planted a vineyard (representing a lineage of sons), is forbidden from going forth to war. This is because a slave to a wife cannot freely serve as a soldier for the Lord; and according to the sentiment of Paul—

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa's theological argument that the state of virginity allows for a direct, spiritual perception of God. He specifically addresses the nature of the "Falling of Man" and begins to tackle the common counter-argument regarding the biblical command to multiply.</meta>

->HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA<-

in Christ, and his "falling asleep" is not death but a passage <note>original: "transitus"; a common term in early Christianity for the death of a saint, implying a transition rather than an end</note>; for in virginity our first parents were immortal, but through the transgression of their virginity, they brought forth the death of both natures <note>likely referring to both physical death and the spiritual death of the soul</note> for themselves and their descendants. While in virginity, they enjoyed the knowledge of the Good itself; but once virginity was lost, with a darkened intellect and the eyes of the flesh opened, they knew evil. They were deprived of the light of the Good itself, which they had enjoyed as virgins through the intimate vision of God. <margin>John 21</margin> Hence when the Apostles, after the resurrection of Christ, were fishing in the Lake of Gennesaret, and Jesus was standing on the shore, the Apostles did not know whom they were seeing; but only the virgin <note>John the Evangelist was traditionally held to be a lifelong virgin, which in Agrippa's view granted him superior spiritual sight</note> John recognized God, and said to Peter, "It is the Lord." 

Now from these mysteries of the Scriptures, it can be sufficiently proven that the union of the flesh <note>original: "copula carnis"; a reference to sexual intercourse</note> was the original sin. Many, because of what has already been said, will easily agree with me in this opinion, unless one small doubt <note>original: "scrupulus"; literally a small sharp stone, used here to mean a nagging intellectual objection</note> stops them. For they will say: if the mingling of the flesh was forbidden, how were our first parents to fulfill that blessing: <margin>Genesis 1</margin> "Increase and multiply"? How were humans to be born, so that the seats of the angels who fell with Lucifer might be filled? 

This is indeed a difficult and thorny objection, the solution to which is all the more difficult the deeper this mystery is investigated. I speak of a <margin>Colossians 1</margin> mystery hidden from ages and generations, but manifested only to the saints, to whom God willed to make known the riches of the glory of this sacrament. This is that mystery of which <margin>John 16</margin> Christ says: "I still have many things to tell you, but you cannot bear them now," and elsewhere he says of the same: <margin>Matthew 19, Luke 8</margin> "Not everyone can receive this word." This is that mystery which he reveals in secret to the Apostles, while parables are presented to the common people, which Paul...
