# THE SUREST ANTIDOTES
## AGAINST THE PLAGUE
### BY HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA
->Dedicated to the Lord Theodoric of Cyrene, Administrator of the Archdiocese of Cologne for sacred suffrages.<-

<note>A decorative woodcut initial 'Q' contains a bird-like figure, possibly a stork or crane, which in the Renaissance often symbolized vigilance or medicinal care.</note> **A**S TO WHAT lifestyle <note>original: "regimen"; the holistic management of diet, environment, and habits</note> should be maintained during a time of pestilence, doctors have handed down many rules. From these, I shall provide you with a summary—albeit a very brief one—of those which are most important and necessary. 

First, therefore, it is necessary to abstain from all excess of external heat, such as from the sun, from fire, from [too much] clothing, and from hot air. One must also avoid internal heat, such as that caused by the excessive eating of salt, spices, medicinal herbs, and foods of intense heat, as well as from strong and smoky <note>original: "fumoso"; referring to heavy, potent wines believed to send "vapors" to the head</note> wine. 

Likewise, one must abstain from heavy exercise—and most especially from that which induces sweating—as well as from sexual intercourse and the passions of the mind <note>The "passions" include intense emotions like fear, anger, or grief, which were believed to open the body's pores and allow plague-bearing air to enter.</note>. One should abstain from all overindulgence in food and drink, yet also from hunger and thirst; from <term>constipation</term> <note>original: "stipticitate uentris"</note> of the bowels; and from long periods of sleep, especially during the day. 

I will omit here which specific foods should be chosen above others and which should be avoided; for even if it were easy to describe these things, it is not easy for everyone to follow such rules. I only advise that in the seasoning of foods...

<summary>Agrippa begins a medical treatise on the plague, offering practical advice on maintaining a moderate lifestyle, or "regimen," to avoid the disease. He focuses on avoiding excess heat, physical exertion, and emotional distress.</summary>
<keywords>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, plague, medicine, regimen, lifestyle, public health, Cologne, humoral theory</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa's practical medical advice for avoiding the plague, transitioning from general lifestyle prohibitions (heat, sex, excess) to specific herbal remedies, dietary adjustments, and environmental purification techniques. This reflects the Renaissance belief in "Galenic" medicine, where diseases like the plague were fought by "cooling" the body and "cleansing" the air.</meta>

HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGAINST THE PLAGUE.

...mostly indeed of those things that resist [the disease], among others you should not omit **tormentil**, **dittany**, **gentian**, **zedoary** <note>a root similar to ginger, used as a stimulant and carminative</note>, **sandalwood**, and **roses**. Use also **vinegar**, especially rose-infused vinegar, and the juice of the **citron**, or **orange**, or **lemons**, and the juice of **sorrel**, as well as <term>verjuice</term> <note>original: "agresta"; the tart, acidic juice of unripened grapes</note>, and the wine of **pomegranates** and of **barberries**. For all things with a harsh and acidic taste resist the pestiferous poison, for they cool, dry, strengthen, and preserve. 

If, however, the chest is harmed by these <note>many of these acidic ingredients were thought to be "sharp" on the lungs</note>, they should be tempered with sugar; if the stomach is affected, use a small portion of **pepper**, for it resists putrefactions, aids digestion, and strengthens the stomach. Provided it is not reduced to too fine a powder, but is instead coarsely crushed, it does not harm the liver. Furthermore, **onions**, **radishes**, <term>sweet flag</term> <note>original: "acorea"; an aromatic root used in traditional medicine</note>, and **capers** are suitable among seasonings, for they also oppose the poison.

Your dwelling, however, and your clothing you shall purge with a bright flame <note>original: "lucida flamma"</note>, kindled especially from the wood of **juniper**, **pine**, **cypress**, or **ash**. You shall also temper the air <note>original: "aerem"</note> with sprinklings of rose water mixed with vinegar, herbs, and flowers, and also with fragrant fumigations; likewise, you shall treat your garments, undershirts, and linens with these same things. Moreover, you shall wash your hands with these many times a day. 

You should not leave the house or go out in public unless you keep a root of **dittany**, or **zedoary**, or some aromatic confection in your mouth, and carry a <term>pomander</term> <note>original: "pomo odorifero"; literally an "odoriferous apple," a ball made of perfumes, resins, and herbs held to the nose to ward off "miasma" or foul air believed to carry disease</note> in your hands to be smelled. And here, it is best to sprinkle crushed **rue** mixed with vinegar and pressed out onto a red-hot iron plate, and [inhale] that smoke and vapor.

**HENRICI CORNELIUS AGRIPPA**

...to apply <note>original: "exaspere," likely a typo for "aspergere" (to sprinkle) or "exhalare" (to breathe out)</note> to the mouth and nostrils, and throughout the whole body and clothing, before you go out into your usual public rounds. Regarding preventative antidotes, out of many available I will now describe for you a few of the more reliable ones. 

The first of these is that you take **rose water**, **rose vinegar**, **sorrel water**, and **white wine**—either aromatic or an old Malmsey <note>a sweet, potent wine originally from Greece, highly prized in the Renaissance for its medicinal properties</note>—in equal portions. In these you should infuse powders of **zedoary root** <note>a root similar to ginger, used here to warm the stomach</note>, **citron peels**, and **Armenian bole** <term>Armenian bole</term> <note>a red medicinal clay rich in iron oxide, used since antiquity as an astringent and an antidote to poison</note>, also measured in equal portions relative to each other, adding a little **saffron**. If you wish, you can also add old **sugar of rose preserve**. You shall keep these combined in a well-sealed glass vial, from which you should drink a small amount every morning and several times throughout the day. 

For this antidote does not allow the putrefaction of the blood and humors to occur, and it powerfully resists the pestilential poison. Furthermore, if you also take the paste of **Rufus's pills** <note>a standard Renaissance remedy consisting of aloes, ammoniacum, and myrrh, named after the physician Rufus of Ephesus</note>—which are otherwise called "common" or "pestilential" pills—dissolved with a sixfold amount of **celandine water**, or **honey water** (which is better), after three days...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of preventative medicines against the plague, moving from liquid distillations to solid electuaries (medicinal pastes) made from nuts, rue, and eggs.</meta>

->**AGAINST THE PLAGUE**<-

Take half a pound of **walnuts** cleaned of their inner husks, a quarter pound of **domestic rue leaves**, and one and a half drams of ground **sea salt**. Let all these be thoroughly incorporated in a mortar with **clarified honey**, or with **syrup of citron** <note>original: "acetositatis citri"</note> or **currant syrup**. A quantity of this the size of a hazelnut should be administered on an empty stomach with a little vinegar and rose water. There are some who add other spices and roots to this electuary <term>electuary</term> <note>a medicinal paste made by mixing powdered herbs with honey or syrup</note>, but there is no great need for them.

There is also another effective medicine which they call the **"Electuary of the Egg,"** the composition of which is as follows: take a fresh **egg** with its shell, empty out the white while leaving the yolk behind, and fill the space with powdered **Oriental saffron**. Having closed the opening, roast it over a slow fire until it is reduced to blackness. Then, grind it into a powder in a mortar and add an equal weight of **rocket seed** <note>original: "seminis erucæ"</note> powder—this is **white mustard**. These must be thoroughly incorporated through long grinding. A quantity of this electuary the size of a maple seed <note>original: "grani aceris"</note> is administered in the morning with a little wine and rose water.

However, later practitioners—not without successful experimentation—added to this composition two drams each of **white dittany** and **tormentil**, and one dram of **nux vomica** <note>the "poison nut" tree; used cautiously in Renaissance medicine as a stimulant and tonic despite its toxicity</note>. Each should be powdered separately at first, and then incorporated with the egg through long grinding. 

There are some who even now add to these the roots of **pimpinella** <note>burnet saxifrage</note>, **zedoary**, **angelica**, and **camphor**. By adding **theriac** <term>theriac</term> <note>an ancient, complex "universal antidote" believed to protect against all poisons and infectious diseases</note> in a weight equal to all the other ingredients, they prepare an electuary in the form of an opiate <note>referring to the thick, paste-like consistency of the medicine</note>, which they administer as we have already said for preservation, and also even...

It notes how later physicians refined these recipes by adding potent ingredients like nux vomica and the famous universal antidote, theriac.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for treating the plague, transitioning from the preparation of the "Electuary of the Egg" to the dosages and additional preventative measures including pills, theriac, and the initial steps of emergency treatment.</meta>

### BY HENRY CORNELIUS AGRIPPA
<note>original: "HENRICI COR. AGRI." This refers to Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa von Nettesheim (1486–1535), a famous polymath, physician, and occult philosopher.</note>

...administered for treatment, in a dose of up to one **dram** <term>dram</term> <note>a unit of weight, roughly 1/8th of an ounce</note> with **white wine**, if the disease has attacked with a chill, or with **rose water** or **scabious water** <note>distilled from the Field Scabious plant, traditionally used to treat "scabs" and pestilence</note>, if it attacks with heat. However, it must be administered within twelve hours from the onset of the disease if it is to be of any help. 

And these are the antidotes which I currently find to be the most useful, safe, and proven above all others, and which you may use healthfully. Just do not neglect to also take **Rufus’s pills** <note>original: "pillolis ruffi"; a famous classical recipe attributed to Rufus of Ephesus, usually consisting of aloe, myrrh, and saffron used as a purgative to "cleanse" the body of corrupt humors</note>, once or twice a week, in the weight of one **scruple** <term>scruple</term> <note>a small unit of weight, about 1.3 grams</note> at dawn, abstaining on that day from any other medicine. 

Note this: if the weather is intensely hot, then the **aloes** and **myrrh** which are included in the composition of said pills should be carefully washed with rose water. You may also add as much of the purest **camphor** as is equal to a third part of the myrrh. But in cold seasons, and for those of a **cold complexion** <note>In Galenic medicine, "complexion" refers to a person's temperament or humoral balance; a "cold" person was thought to have an excess of phlegm or black bile</note>, these specific preparations should be avoided; instead, those pills should be taken without camphor and without washing the ingredients. 

Furthermore, because these same pills sometimes open the bowels and loosen the system too much, it is useful to add a small portion of **Armenian bole** <note>a medicinal red clay or earth from Armenia, used as an astringent to stop bleeding or diarrhea</note> to them, or to coat them in its powder. Because they also sometimes irritate the stomach, this harm should be corrected by mixing in a small amount of choice **mastic** <note>a resin obtained from the mastic tree, used to soothe the digestive tract</note>. 

Furthermore, it is best to take **old theriac** <term>theriac</term> <note>a complex "universal" ointment or paste containing dozens of ingredients, including viper flesh and opium, used since antiquity as an antidote to poison and plague</note> or **mithridate**, in the weight of two or three scruples every ten days, taken with a draft of rose or **sorrel water**, while also observing the usual rules of physicians. 

However, if anyone feels themselves already struck by the plague, if their age and strength allow it, let them undergo **bloodletting** <note>original: "phlebotometur"</note> as soon as possible...

<meta>This page continues the medical instructions for plague treatment, specifically detailing an emergency potion made of roasted onions and theriac, and discussing the importance of driving toxins out of the body through sweat and the emunctories.</meta>

# AGAINST THE PLAGUE
<note>original: "CONTRA PESTEM"</note>

...[blood should be let] from a suitable vein, and soon the following medicine should be offered; and the sooner, the better, even if **phlebotomy** <term>phlebotomy</term> <note>the practice of bloodletting, common in Galenic medicine to balance the body's humors</note> has not first been performed—for instance, if a surgeon is not immediately at hand—because this evil <note>the plague</note> allows for no delay. Therefore, it is appropriate to offer the following drink immediately, and if necessary, repeat it often according to the patient's strength. 

The potion is as follows: take **six white onions**, hollow them out from the top, and having removed the core, fill them with **old theriac** <term>theriac</term> <note>original: "teriata ueteri"; a complex medical concoction used as a universal antidote, often containing dozens of ingredients including opium and viper flesh</note> mixed with a little powder of **dictamnus** <note>original: "dictami"; likely Dittany of Crete, a plant prized for its supposed ability to draw out poisons and arrowheads</note> and **tormentil** <note>original: "tormentillæ"; a plant in the rose family used as an astringent</note> roots. Wrap them in wet paper and roast them under hot ashes until they are cooked to softness. 

Then a portion of these should be set aside; for this, crushed into the manner of a **poultice**, you shall place upon the **abscess** <note>original: "abscessui"; referring here to a plague bubo or swelling</note>. It is indeed a most safe antidote, drawing out and destroying the poison. 

But to return to the potion: take the remaining cooked onions, as we said, crush them with **one ounce** of **syrup of lemon juice** <note>original: "siropo acetositatis citri"</note> and a little **vinegar**. Squeeze this through a cloth, and let **three ounces** of this warm strained liquid be given to the sick person. Let him be placed in bed well covered, abstaining from food and drink for six hours. 

By all means, however, take care that he does not sleep; for the power of this drink is most effective at driving the poison from the **principal organs** <note>the heart, liver, and brain</note> to their **emunctories** <term>emunctories</term> <note>original: "emunctoria"; anatomical points like the armpits, groin, or behind the ears where the body was thought to naturally expel waste</note>, and evacuating it through **sweat** and other **critical purgations** <note>original: "creticas expurgationes"; refers to a "crisis" in a disease, the point where the body's nature fights to expel the cause of illness</note>. If you mix a small portion of the **Electuary of the Egg** <note>a specific plague remedy mentioned on the previous page</note> into this potion, it will be wonderful. 

Finally, it must be noted if the patient is **plethoric** <term>plethoric</term> <note>original: "plectoricus"; having an overabundance of blood, one of the four humors</note> or **cacochymic** <term>cacochymic</term> <note>original: "cacochimicus"; having "bad juices," or an imbalance of corrupt humors</note>, namely, filled with bad humors...

# HEINRICH CORNELIUS AGRIPPA
<note>original: "HENRICI CORNEL. AGRIP."</note>

...or if by chance he has not been **phlebotomized** <term>phlebotomy</term> <note>original: "phlebotomatus"; the medical practice of opening a vein to let blood, used here to balance the body's humors during infection</note>, or if it is not appropriate to reduce the blood, then **purgative medicines** <note>original: "medicamina solutiua"; substances intended to "solve" or loosen the bowels to expel the pestilential poison</note> must be added to that potion. Namely, for those of a more delicate constitution, half an ounce of **Trifera Persica** <note>a traditional compound medicine, often containing peach kernels, used as a gentle laxative</note> with one **drachma** <term>drachma</term> <note>an apothecary weight approximately equal to 3.9 grams</note> of **Electuary Hamech** <note>a complex purgative formula attributed to Mesue the Younger, often containing myrobalans and colocynth to purge "black bile"</note>. 

For the more robust, however, let it be given without the *Trifera*, but with an equal amount of **Confection Hamech**, as much of the **electuary of rose juice**, as much of the **Indian electuary**, and two drachmas of **Diaphoenicon** <note>original: "diafeniconis"; a laxative electuary made primarily from dates, from the Greek 'phoenix'</note>. In both cases, the potion should be administered warm with water of **scabious**, **sorrel**, or **roses**. 

As for what remains of these matters, I pass over them, for these allow for both time and further counsel; I believe I have done enough if I have described only those things which allow for no delay. 

But I still wish to remind you that the highest and ultimate remedy for this evil is the **Adamical Earth** <note>original: "terram adamam"; in Agrippa’s occult philosophy, this refers to the "First Matter" or the pure, uncorrupted earth from which Adam was created, believed to hold the secret to the Philosopher's Stone and universal healing</note>, namely the first matter of our creation. For this alone is the unique source that bestows a medicine than which there is none greater. It will be of no small power if it has been purified through the **artifice of fire** <note>an alchemical reference to distillation or calcination</note> and restored to its simplicity by a suitable washing. 

But of these things, I will speak at another time.

My Cornelius, having diligently surveyed your opinion concerning that original flaw within us <note>original: "originario in nos vitio"; the writer is referring to the theological doctrine of Original Sin and its transmission.</note>, we have examined it in its entirety. ->TO THE REVEREND<-
->FATHER IN CHRIST AND<-
Lord, Doctor of Sacred Theology, Lord Theodoric <note>Dietrich von Wichereid (Theodoricus Cyrenensis), who served as the suffragan bishop of Cologne from 1518 to 1554.</note>, Bishop of Cyrene and most worthy administrator by suffrage in sacred matters for the Archbishopric of Cologne, Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa commends himself.

**I** recently received your letters (reverend father) in the city of Metz <note>original: "ciuitate Metensi"; at this time, Agrippa was serving as the town orator and legal advocate in Metz.</note>, together with letters from my mother, bringing me—alas, all too much—the sad news of my father’s death. I grieve most vehemently for this. But I find the sole comfort for this sorrow, and no other, than that one must yield to the divine ordinance. For I know that God grants to humans things that, although not always pleasant and very often even adverse, are nevertheless always useful, intended to benefit us either here or in the heavenly homeland. For God acts according to what His nature brings, namely according to His essence, which is all goodness; therefore, He works nothing but what is good and salutary

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa's letter to Theodoric, the Suffragan Bishop of Cologne. He moves from discussing his father's death to recounting his survival through military service and his recent marriage.</meta>

is worked <note>original: "ratur"; completing the thought from the previous page that God works only what is good.</note>; nevertheless (such is my human frailty), I grieve vehemently and am troubled within myself. But your most welcome letter—you who have been left to me as a surviving second father—has tempered my grief in no small measure. I must now, therefore, respond to this letter; for you say that you rejoice greatly, and are affected by immense pleasure, that I am still among the living, I who was reported some years ago to have perished in the war. 

Certainly, for many years now, by Imperial command <note>Agrippa served under Holy Roman Emperor Maximilian I, both as a soldier and a scholar-official.</note> and by my own duty as a soldier, I followed the Imperial and royal camps. I took part in many conflicts with no lack of vigor; death preceded me before my face, and I followed as its minister. My right hand was steeped in blood, my left hand divided the spoils; my belly was filled with plunder, and the steps of my feet were upon the corpses of the slaughtered. I became unmindful of my own dignity, and was entirely stained beneath the **shadow of Tartarus** <note>original: "tartarea... sub umbra"; Tartarus is the deepest abyss of torment in Greek mythology, used here to describe the hellish nature of the battlefield.</note>. 

But the grace of God abounded over my iniquities and delivered me from the **mouth of the lion** <note>A biblical allusion to Psalm 22:21 or 2 Timothy 4:17, signifying a narrow escape from certain death.</note>. That grace preserved me unharmed amidst hostile crowds and a bloodstained populace—among whom no one is a victor unless he is a murderer, and no one is defeated unless he is dead—saving me from the swords and snares of so many enemies. Therefore, I rejoice with you that I have finally returned alive to Cologne, and that the sad and mournful news has turned into joy for my parents and friends. 

And I confess that I owe immortal thanks to your love and benevolence toward me. Furthermore, as you now congratulate me on my marriage, because I have taken a wife who pleases me <note>Agrippa's first wife was Luisa de Cyon, whom he married in 1514; he frequently wrote of his domestic happiness during this period.</note>, from whom I hope for posterity, I indeed give boundless thanks to Almighty God, who [has given me] a wife...

<summary>Agrippa reflects on his violent years as a soldier in the Imperial army, expressing remorse for the bloodshed he witnessed and gratitude to God for his survival and his new marriage.</summary>
<keywords>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa, military service, Maximilian I, Cologne, marriage, divine grace, warfare, Luisa de Cyon</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa's personal defense and biographical sketch in his letter to the Suffragan Bishop of Cologne. Having described the horrors of his military life, he now turns to his domestic happiness and his transition from secular sciences to sacred studies.</meta>

joined to me a woman according to my own heart: a noble virgin, well-mannered, young, and beautiful. She lives so much in harmony with my own habits that not a single reproachful word has ever passed between us. I might even call myself the happiest of men because, no matter how things turn—whether in prosperity or adversity—she is always equally kind to me, affable, constant, possessing a most upright soul and sound judgment, and she is always self-possessed. 

Furthermore, you praise the fact that, having cast aside secular matters as much as possible, I have devoted myself to **sacred letters** <note>original: "sacris literis"; referring to the study of the Bible and theology.</note>. You advise me that by offering chaste prayers to God, I should guard against diabolical, seductive machines and errors (by which I believe you mean **dialectic** <note>The formal system of logic used in medieval universities, which Agrippa often criticized as being full of empty hair-splitting.</note> and philosophy). Indeed, you know in part how, after my study of dialectic and the investigation of natural things, I furthermore surveyed the entire **host of the heavens** <note>original: "caelorum militiam"; an astronomical and astrological term for the stars and planetary spheres.</note>. I did this by following those two great wisdoms of **Albertus Magnus** <note>A 13th-century German Dominican friar and polymath. Agrippa is referencing the *Speculum Astronomiae* (Mirror of Astronomy), a work then attributed to Albertus that discussed which books of magic and astrology were permissible for Christians.</note>, which he describes in his *Mirror*, a small work that is not particularly highly praised. 

At last (to tell the truth), after much time and labor were consumed, and with the loss of the goods of my soul, body, and fortune, I gained nothing from all these things except sin—since all these things are not of **faith** <note>A reference to Romans 14:23: "Everything that does not come from faith is sin." Agrippa is expressing a "crisis of vanity" regarding his earlier occult and philosophical studies.</note>. But by the grace of God, eventually recognizing such great vanities in human sciences, I turned toward sacred letters. This happened after I had already received the scholastic **caps and rings** <note>The traditional insignia granted to a person receiving a doctoral degree.</note> in both **civil and canon law** <note>original: "utriusque iuris"; refers to the "Doctor of Both Laws," the highest legal qualification of the time.</note> and in medicine—doing so to satisfy the desires of my family, who prefer that I be titled a "Doctor" rather than simply be "learned." Though I have come to it late, I have now applied myself to the study of the Scriptures with all my zeal. I embrace them entirely (though far removed from contentions and quarrelsome **syllogisms** <note>A form of logical argument where a conclusion is drawn from two premises; Agrippa uses this to mean the overly complex arguments of academic theologians.</note>), and I cannot express how much my mind is both delighted and finds rest in them. 

But lest you think of me as one still a novice, wandering outside the limits in this infinite abyss of divinity...

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues Agrippa’s letter to the Suffragan Bishop of Cologne. Having discussed his personal life and shift toward sacred studies, he now addresses a specific theological dispute regarding his views on the nature of Original Sin.</meta>

...guide my opinions, I appoint you—as a sacred prelate, an elder, and the dearest friend of all my studies and actions—as my censor and judge. For I know that it belongs to the sacred order and office of Bishops to approve or condemn, to declare or commend, or to tolerate for debate any new opinions concerning morals and faith. Therefore, under that authority and censorship, I have submitted to you my opinion on **original sin** <note>original: "originali peccato"; the Christian doctrine of the fallen state of humanity inherited from Adam.</note> to be evaluated, so that you might indicate how you agree or disagree with me. Rest assured that I would prefer to disagree with myself alongside you, than to agree with myself against your judgment. 

Nevertheless, I ask your pardon for imposing such a heavy burden upon you, since this matter, as you say, carries no small weight. But now, because I have once begun to speak, I beg you not to be indignant as I respond to you once again. You say in your letter that the common opinion of theologians is that the **original stain** <note>original: "originalem maculam"; the spiritual "mark" or corruption left by original sin.</note> cannot exist where the **rational soul** <note>In medieval philosophy, the *anima rationalis* is the highest part of the soul, responsible for reason and intellect, which was thought to be unique to humans.</note> is not present. 

<margin>He responds to the objection.</margin>

I hold to this opinion. However, since original sin was such that it cast down the **whole man**—that is, the man constituted of a divine soul, a heavenly spirit, and an elemental body <note>Agrippa follows a "tripartite" view of humanity common in Renaissance Neoplatonism: the *soul* (intellect), the *spirit* (the intermediary link), and the *body* (matter).</note>—from his former nobility, both as a whole and in each of its parts, I would say that original sin was such that all and every individual power of man (the rational, the sensory, the **vegetative** <note>The "vegetative" power refers to the basic biological functions like growth and nutrition, which humans share with plants.</note>, and the corporeal) failed the law given to them and truly concurred in this act. Therefore, even if original sin had its seat in the rational power (which, by acquiescing to the senses and the flesh, consented to the fault), it was still necessary that this consent, having been thus corrupted, was also carried out in the action of the body. Therefore, the original...

<summary>Agrippa submits his theological writings on Original Sin to the Bishop for judgment, arguing that while the rational soul is the seat of sin, the fall corrupted the entire human being—including the physical body and biological functions.</summary>
<keywords>Original Sin, Rational Soul, Tripartite Man, Bishop of Cologne, Theology, Human Nature, Humanist correspondence</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>Continuing the argument regarding the nature of Original Sin, Agrippa moves from the theoretical "stain" on the soul to the physical manifestation of the first sin. He argues that while sin begins in the mind, it is only truly "sin of the flesh" when enacted physically, specifically identifying the Fall with sexual lust.</meta>

...sin, an effect or some act of a depraved reason
or will, but when brought into the exercise of the body—where
that act has become real, not simulated, not merely thought,
not imaginary—I find no other such sin, 
except the sin of the flesh, as the Apostle Paul says: *Every sin* <margin>1 Corinthians 6</margin>
*that a man does is outside the body, but he who* 
*commits fornication sins against his own body.* <note>original: "Omne peccatū quodcūq; fecerit homo extra corpus est, q̃ aut fornicatur, etiā in corpus suū peccat." Agrippa cites 1 Corinthians 6:18 to support his argument that sexual sin is uniquely tied to the physical body.</note> But someone will say, "even 
he who has lusted after a woman has already committed fornication with her," <margin>Matthew 5</margin>
I say he has committed fornication with her in his mind; 
nevertheless, that effect of lust in the reason, and of the image in the
imagination, is not the same as a thing in its
real existence. Indeed, let us now examine this even in the rational 
power, and let us seek what kind of fall it suffered. 
The highest citadel and the unique act of reason is religion,
which was evidently commanded from the beginning: specifically, the true 
and unique spiritual worship of the one, alone, and true God the Creator. 
But when reason was depraved, with the serpent as its teacher, 
soon all religion collapsed into nothing but the lust of the flesh. 
Men sought this as if it were the highest happiness, and the most 
filthy Priapus <note>A Greek fertility god known for a permanent, oversized phallus. Agrippa uses him as a symbol for the "organ" of the first sin.</note>—the organ of the primordial sin—was 
held to be a God. To him they sacrificed; to him all temples were 
first dedicated; his most filthy idols <note>original: "simulachra"; statues or representations of a deity.</note> were the 
first of all to be built. Him did those first architects of
religions worship: the Chaldeans, Egyptians,
Assyrians, Babylonians, Arabs, Scythians, Phoenicians, Ethiopians, 
and likewise all Asia, Africa, and Europe; nor was it 
lawful for any priest to be made, who had not first been 
initiated into the rites of Priapus. This is that Belphegor <note>A biblical idol (Baal-Peor) associated with the Moabites, frequently linked by Renaissance theologians to obscene or carnal rites.</note>, 
the oldest idol of all, which was also called Chemosh, after Ham 
the son of Noah, who—† according to the testimony of Berosus <note>A Babylonian priest and historian. Agrippa likely refers to "pseudo-Berosus," a popular Renaissance forgery by Annius of Viterbo that claimed Ham was the source of ancient idolatry and magic.</note>—was 
therefore nicknamed "Esenna." 

                                                                        L 3

<summary>Agrippa argues that the physical manifestation of Original Sin was specifically a sexual act, asserting that ancient pagan religions were actually perversions where the "organ of sin" (Priapus) was worshipped as a god.</summary>
<keywords>Original Sin, Priapus, Belphegor, sexual lust, Ham son of Noah, Berosus, ancient religions, 1 Corinthians 6, Matthew 5</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>The previous page established that the "citadel of reason" fell into the "lust of the flesh," identifying the Phallus/Priapus as the physical manifestation of this corruption. Agrippa now identifies the historical figure who restored this "foul religion" after the Great Flood.</meta>

...the impure, or rather the ignominious propagator <note>Agrippa refers here to Ham (Cham), the son of Noah, who in Renaissance hermeticism was often blamed for reintroducing forbidden antediluvian knowledge and "filthy" sexual rites after the Flood.</note>. For he was the first to restore, after the flood, this foul religion of depraved reason—a religion inherited from those ancient giants who lived before the flood. From this source emerged those orgies, bacchanalia, rites of Priapus <note>Priapalia: Festivals or rites dedicated to Priapus, the god of fertility and permanent erection.</note>, and other most shameful ceremonies. From this also came Venus, Cupid, the genitals <note>original: "pudenda"</note> of Osiris, the Pans, and the Satyrs; finally, even the stinking goat was accepted as a god because of its lewd lust. 

Behold, you see now that from the fall of the rational power, no sin can be considered more ancient than that of lust and fornication; you see how human reason preferred the worship of lust to divine contemplation. Consequently, while each and every member of a man is moved only by the command and will of the rational soul, lust has so bound the genital organ alone to its own work, and so enslaved it to its own command, that if lust is absent—or if it is not further excited and aroused—that member cannot be moved to its work at all. 

Therefore, it seems to me that the act of lust itself was this primordial sin of human nature and the corruption of our entire species. To this day, humans are ashamed of it and, blushing, avoid the eyes of onlookers. As <margin>Augustine</margin> says: A man can better endure a multitude of spectators when he is unjustly angry with another man, than the sight of even a single person when he is lawfully joined to his wife. <note>original: "Magis fert homo spectantiū multitudinē q̄n̄ iniuste irasatur hoī, quā uel unius cōspectū qn̄ iuste miscetur uxori." Agrippa cites St. Augustine’s *City of God*, noting that even "holy" marital sex is performed in private due to the inherent shame attached to the act since the Fall.</note> 

As for what remains of these matters, it stays "stored deep in my mind" <note>original: "alta mente repostū" — a quote from Virgil's Aeneid (1.26), referring to deep-seated thoughts or grievances.</note> until I can speak with you in person. It remains now to satisfy your request that I send some preservative antidote against the plague. This is a difficult matter and not entirely without risk, and it is a subject upon which I ought to consult a physician more learned and skilled than myself. Nevertheless, I will in no way hide from you those things which I use myself, provided I first warn you of this one thing, which is the safest of all...

<summary>Agrippa identifies the biblical Ham as the restorer of sexualized pagan worship and argues that the involuntary nature of sexual arousal proves that lust is the root of human corruption. He then begins to pivot toward a request from his correspondent for a medical/alchemical antidote to the plague.</summary>

<keywords>Ham, Priapus, sexual sin, Original Sin, St. Augustine, Osirian rites, lust, plague antidote, rational soul, fallen nature</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of the "original sin" of lust and the shame associated with it, citing Augustine. This page shifts from theological speculation to the author's immediate, practical circumstances—surviving a literal plague in the city of Metz.</meta>

...is to flee far away, and indeed to those places that the pestilence left a year ago, rather than to those it has not yet invaded; nor is it safe to return to the place you left unless the plague has ceased there for several months. For very often, there is more danger in returning than there would have been in staying. 

However, since I do not have this opportunity, and have been forced throughout this entire year to live in the midst of plague victims, I have composed for myself and my family certain antidotes <note>In the Renaissance, an "antidote" was not just a cure for poison, but a preventative medicine or "preservative" against the "miasma" or foul air believed to cause the plague.</note> according to the opinions of the most excellent physicians. I have used these continuously, not without great benefit and the health of all my household. I am sending these to you as a brief regimen <note>A "regimen" is a prescribed way of life—including diet, exercise, and medicine—intended to maintain health or recover from illness.</note> attached to this letter. If these satisfy your mind less than you hoped, I shall not avoid describing the whole matter to you from the start in its proper order in a larger volume. 

But I hope this calamity will soon be expiated <note>Here used in the sense of "purged" or "cleansed," suggesting the plague is a divine punishment or a natural corruption that must run its course.</note>, and that we shall proceed with more fruitful and sublime study—if only that nourishing Creator of the world <note>original: "almus ille mũdi opifex"</note>, after the various turmoils of fortune and of my life, might so direct my affairs that I may enjoy my ancestral home <note>original: "patrijs laribus" — the "Lares" were the household gods of ancient Rome, here used poetically for home and hearth.</note> in a peaceful lot. 

For the rest, I pray that you keep me commended and dear to Count Hermann of Neuenahr <note>Hermann von Neuenahr (1492–1530) was a prominent German humanist, scholar, and diplomat who was a patron to many intellectual figures of the era, including Agrippa.</note>, a most noble as well as most learned man. Farewell most happily, and love me as you usually do. 

From the city of Metz, the sixth of February, in the year 1518.

->Antwerp, at the house of Michael Hillenius at the sign of the Turnip,<-
->In the year 1529.<-
<note>Michael Hillenius (also known as Hoochstraten) was a prolific printer in Antwerp. "In Rapo" (At the Turnip) refers to the visual sign hanging outside his shop, which served as his address and trademark.</note>

<summary>Agrippa concludes his letter by discussing the practicalities of surviving the plague in Metz, offering medical antidotes to his correspondent, and expressing a weary desire for a peaceful life of study. The page ends with the letter's date (1518) and the printer's colophon (1529).</summary>
